# Adductor Strain

*Knee & Sports · 2 / 61*

## 1 Basics

**Incidence** — **10–30% of soccer and hockey players**

**Adductor complex** — **adductor longus (most commonly injured)**, adductor magnus, adductor brevis, gracilis, obturator externus, pectineus · all **obturator nerve** (Orthobullets)

## 2 Mechanism

**"Pulled groin"** — **forceful hip extension + external rotation of an abducted leg** → **strong eccentric contraction** of adductors

**Avulsion** — muscle **fails in tension as the leg is abducted**

**Repetitive hip extension + abduction** (hockey, soccer) → **athletic pubalgia** — adductor longus torn and retracted from the **common aponeurosis** with rectus abdominis (detail in Athletic Pubalgia & Adductor strain chapter)

## 3 Presentation

**Pain** — **immediate, severe groin pain**

**Tenderness** — **subcutaneous border of the (inferior) pubic ramus** · medial pubic ramus

**Strength** — pain ± **weakness on resisted adduction** vs other leg

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Study | Finding |
|---|---|
| X-ray: **AP pelvis + lateral hip** | **Fleck of bone** if avulsion |
| **MRI** | **Avulsion from pubic ramus**, muscle oedema + haemorrhage · T2 ↑ fluid signal at tendon origin |

![T2 MRI: adductor origin on the pubic ramus (1) and avulsed adductor tendon (2)](../../MCQ/attachments/q1413-illa-adductor-tear.jpg)

### Adductor strain classification

| Degree | Findings |
|---|---|
| **1st** | Pain, **minimal loss** of strength + motion |
| **2nd** | **Compromised strength** |
| **3rd** | **Complete disruption**, loss of muscle function |

## 5 Management

### Groin injuries — adductor strain / avulsion management

**Mainstay (incl. avulsion)** — **rest, ice, protected WB as needed** → gentle stretching → resistance → **core strengthening** → gradual return to sport

**Avoid immobilisation** — promotes **muscle tightness + scarring** / contracture

**Rugby player, MRI adductor avulsion** — **immediate rehabilitation with increasing passive + active motion**\
✗ Wrong options: strict immobilisation in flexion + adduction · tendon repair · tenodesis · repair + allograft

**Open repair** — **no data / no high-level evidence** it beats nonoperative care

**Hockey groin syndrome** — **external oblique aponeurosis tear + ilioinguinal nerve entrapment** → ilioinguinal nerve ablation + external oblique reinforcement

### Adductor insertion avulsion syndrome ("thigh splints")

**What** — painful **proximal-to-mid femur at adductor insertions** · insidious thigh pain + limp, no trauma

**Exam / labs** — pain on **resisted adduction** only · afebrile, **normal WBC, ESR, CRP**

**Imaging** — **X-rays normal** · MRI (TSE inversion recovery) → **linear high signal along medial (posteromedial) periosteum** of proximal–mid femoral shaft, **4–9 cm** long

![Inversion-recovery MRI: linear high signal along the medial proximal femur](../../MCQ/attachments/q8881-c-adductor-mri.jpg)

**Treatment** — **activity modification + NSAIDs**, observe · resolves in **1–2 months**\
✗ Wrong options: antibiotics · I&D · prophylactic fixation (not a stress fracture) · biopsy

**Differential** — osteomyelitis, neoplasm · biopsy only if pain **persists or increases**

## X vs Y

| | **Adductor strain** | **Thigh splints** |
|---|---|---|
| Onset | **Acute**, severe | **Insidious** |
| Site | **Pubic ramus** (origin) | **Femoral shaft** (insertion) |
| MRI | Avulsion / oedema at pubis | **Periosteal high signal**, posteromedial femur |
| Treatment | Rest, ice, protected WB, **early motion** | Activity modification, NSAIDs |

## How it's asked

**"Adductor avulsion on MRI — treatment?"** → **immediate rehabilitation with increasing passive and active motion**

**"Insidious thigh pain, normal labs, periosteal signal medial femur — next step?"** → **activity modification and NSAIDs**

**"Repetitive motion causing adductor longus tear from common aponeurosis (sports hernia)?"** → **hip extension and abduction**

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Anterior Inferior Iliac Spine Avulsion (AIIS)

*Knee & Sports · 3 / 61*

## 1 Basics

**Who** — adolescents **14–17 yr**, **males > females** · **kicking sports** (soccer)

**Why adolescents** — **apophysis weaker than the musculotendinous unit** during forceful contraction

### Anatomy

**AIIS** — bony prominence **just above the acetabulum** → origin of the **direct head of rectus femoris** (**femoral n.**)

**Reflected head** — **supra-acetabular groove + anterior hip capsule**

**Insertion** — rectus femoris → **quadriceps tendon → superior pole of patella**

### Pelvic apophyseal avulsions — sites and muscles

| Site | Muscle | Frequency (Rossi, 203) |
|---|---|---|
| **Ischial tuberosity** | Hamstrings (BF long head, SM, ST) + adductor magnus | **\~50% — most common** |
| **AIIS** | **Rectus femoris (direct head)** | **25%** |
| **ASIS** | **Sartorius** + TFL | 20% |
| Superior corner of pubic symphysis | Adductors | 4% |
| **Iliac crest** | Abdominals | **1% — least common** |
| Lesser trochanter | **Iliopsoas** | — |
| Greater trochanter | Gluteus medius + minimus | — |

**Most common site** — **ischial tuberosity \~50%** (Rossi, OB) · **AIIS most common** (Schuett, 228 avulsions, OB — see ASIS chapter) — read the options

**Sports** — **soccer + gymnastics** most common

![Common pelvic apophyseal avulsion sites and their muscles](../../MCQ/attachments/q210190-illa-pelvic-avlusion-fractures.jpg)

## 2 Mechanism

### Rectus femoris origin avulsion — mechanism, innervation & nonoperative care

**Mechanism** — **eccentric contraction of rectus femoris** **as hip extends + knee flexes** → avulses origin · **kicking** (missed ball, foot strikes ground) · hurdler's **trailing limb** striking hurdle

**MRI label** — rectus femoris = anterior central thigh muscle\
✗ Wrong options: vastus lateralis · vastus intermedius · vastus medialis · sartorius

**Nerve** — **femoral**\
✗ Wrong options: genitofemoral (cremaster) · pudendal (levator ani, bulbo- / ischiocavernosus) · obturator (obturator externus, adductor longus / brevis, gracilis) · superior gluteal (gluteus medius / minimus, TFL)

**Distractor insertions** — sartorius → **pes anserinus** · TFL → **Gerdy's tubercle** via ITB · iliopsoas → lesser trochanter · gluteus medius / minimus → greater trochanter

## 3 Presentation

**History** — **sudden "pop" in the pelvis**

**Exam** — antalgic gait · **anterior hip pain + hip flexion weakness** · pain on **resisted hip flexion**, local tenderness

**Differential** — SCFE, Perthes, congenital hip subluxation, malignancy, infection

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

**Radiographs** — AP pelvis / **AP + frog lateral hip** show the AIIS avulsion · **X-rays sufficient** to diagnose + decide\
✗ Wrong options: MRI before treatment

![AIIS avulsion fragment above the acetabulum](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3131-aiis-avulsion.jpg)

## 5 Management

```
AIIS avulsion
├─ Minimally displaced (almost all) → REST, ACTIVITY MODIFICATION, PT
└─ Selected cases → SURGERY
       ├─ Displaced > 2 cm / painful nonunion → ORIF
       └─ Symptomatic heterotopic ossification → ARTHROSCOPIC EXCISION
```

**Nonoperative** — **almost all succeed** · bed rest, ice, **activity modification**, NSAIDs, supervised PT · **hip flexed 2 wk** (↓ stretch on muscle + apophysis) → **guarded WB 4 wk**

**Pelvic avulsions (OB)** — protected WB **2–4 wk** → stretching + strengthening → normal activity **\~2 months**

**16-yr soccer, minimally displaced AIIS** — **rest, activity modification + physical therapy**\
✗ Wrong options: corticosteroid injection (impairs healing) · early aggressive strengthening (displacement / delayed union) · MRI · ORIF

### Operative criteria

| Surgery for | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Significant displacement** | **Commonly > 2 cm** |
| **Painful nonunion** | ORIF |
| **Symptomatic heterotopic ossification** | Excision (arthroscopic) |
| High-level athlete | Persistent limitation despite adequate nonop |

**Skeletally mature elite athletes** — complete / retracted proximal rectus femoris avulsion → surgery gives **faster RTP + lower recurrence** · individualise

**Post-traumatic rectus femoris HO** — **arthroscopic excision** → better pain, ROM, function at 2 yr

## 6 Complications

**Complications** — **loss of reduction** · **delayed union**

## How it's asked

**"Hurdler's AIIS avulsion — nerve to the muscle?"** → **femoral nerve**

**"AIIS avulsion — eccentric contraction of which labelled muscle?"** → **rectus femoris (C)**

**"Avulsion at origin of muscle inserting on superior pole of patella?"** → **AIIS radiograph (Figure B)**

**"Minimally displaced AIIS avulsion — next step?"** → **rest, activity modification, physical therapy**

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Anterior Superior Iliac Spine (ASIS) Avulsion

*Knee & Sports · 4 / 61*

## 1 Basics

**Who** — adolescents **14–17 yr** approaching skeletal maturity, **males > females** · sprinting, running, baseball, soccer, hockey

**Avulsion physiology** — sudden violent contraction or excess stretch across an **open apophysis** · often **no external trauma** · low-energy → **no associated life-threatening injuries**

### Anatomy

| Muscle from ASIS | Nerve | Insertion |
|---|---|---|
| **Sartorius** | **Femoral** | **Pes anserinus** → source of **pes anserine bursitis** |
| **Tensor fascia lata** | **Superior gluteal** | Gerdy's tubercle via ITB |

**Insertion distractors** — fibular head = **biceps femoris** · femoral insertion anterior + distal to LCL = **popliteus** · ACL autograft = **gracilis (inferior pubic ramus) + semitendinosus (ischial tuberosity)**, both at pes · extends hip + flexes knee = **hamstrings**

## 2 Mechanism

### Sartorius origin avulsion — mechanism, innervation & protected weight bearing

**Mechanism** — indirect · **sudden forceful (eccentric) contraction of sartorius ± TFL** · **hip extended** (+ knee flexed) → **sprinting** (out of blocks, 400 m) or **swinging a baseball bat**\
✗ Wrong options: external oblique · rectus femoris · iliopsoas · gluteus medius

**Avulsed muscle nerve** — sartorius → **femoral**\
✗ Wrong options: sciatic · obturator · pudendal · inferior gluteal

## 3 Presentation

**History** — **"pop" or snap** · may be **misdiagnosed as a muscle strain**

**Exam** — tender over ASIS (superior to hip joint), groin ecchymosis · **pain with straight-leg raise** · weak **hip flexion + knee extension** · limp if severe

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Study | Use |
|---|---|
| **AP pelvis** | Displaced fractures visible · small fragment **easily missed** |
| CT / MRI | Confirm **nondisplaced** fractures |
| CT (relative) | **Rarely needed** — measure displacement, or tell **ASIS vs AIIS** origin (✗ Wrong options: urgent CT · extensive work-up) |

![Left ASIS avulsion](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3091-asis2-1-moved.jpg)

### Diagnostic criteria

| Criterion | ASIS avulsion |
|---|---|
| **History** | Sudden contraction of sartorius + TFL |
| **Exam** | ASIS tenderness, pain on SLR |
| **Age** | Adolescent / young adult |
| **Radiographs** | Fracture on standard pelvis views |

### White classification

| White | Muscle | Activity | Fragment |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Type 1** | **Sartorius** | **Sprinting** | **Small**, displaced **anteriorly + inferiorly** |
| **Type 2** | **TFL** | **Twisting trunk / start of bat swing** | **Larger**, displaced **laterally** |

## 5 Management

```
ASIS avulsion
├─ Displacement < 3 cm (most) → PROTECTED WB, CRUTCHES, EARLY ROM
└─ Displacement > 3 cm or painful nonunion → ORIF
```

**Nonoperative** — rest, ice, NSAIDs, **protected / progressive WB as tolerated with crutches**, early ROM + stretching, PT · position limb to ↓ stretch on muscle · **return when pain-free motion** · outcomes **equivalent to surgery**

**14-yr sprinter, pop, ASIS fragment** — **progressive weightbearing, return when pain-free**\
✗ Wrong options: fix femoral neck stress fracture · iliopsoas recession (internal snapping hip has normal X-rays + can bear weight) · fix sartorius origin · fix rectus femoris direct head (that is AIIS)

**1 wk after injury, improving, still on crutches** — **WBAT + crutches for 3 weeks total**\
✗ Wrong options: NWB 6 wk · closed reduction + pinning (operative choice is ORIF) · aspiration · urgent CT

**12-yr bat swing, 2 cm displaced** — **rest + protected WB with crutches**\
✗ Wrong options: ORIF with rectus femoris / sartorius / iliopsoas · excision

### Operative criteria

**ORIF** — **displacement > 3 cm** (Orthobullets, most OB) · **> 2 cm in elite athletes** or failed nonop (OB) — read the options · **painful nonunion**

**High-energy paediatric acetabular fracture** — ORIF if **> 2 mm** intra-articular or triradiate displacement (contrast with low-energy avulsions)

### Iliac crest apophysis avulsion — abdominal wall innervation

**Mechanism** — **rapid lateral trunk flexion / twisting** → contraction of **external + internal oblique, transversus** · sprinter out of blocks · **rare**

**Nerve** — abdominal wall = **lower intercostal nerves**\
✗ Wrong options: sciatic (hamstrings) · femoral (rectus femoris) · obturator (adductor longus) · superior gluteal (TFL)

![Left iliac crest apophyseal avulsion](../../MCQ/attachments/q218866-a-iliac-crest-avulsion-fx.jpg)

**Treatment** — **protected WB + activity as tolerated**, rest, analgesics, PT\
✗ Wrong options: oncology work-up + biopsy · ORIF · abduction brace in 45° flexion · **120° knee flexion 24 h — quadriceps contusion**

**Surgery** — displaced **> 3 cm** or failed nonop

### Apophyseal avulsion series

| Series | Finding |
|---|---|
| Schuett (228, mean 14.4 yr, **76% male**) | **AIIS > ASIS > ischial tuberosity > iliac crest** · surgery **3%** |
| Schuett — nonunion | 5 nonunions, **4 ischial tuberosity** · displacement **> 2 cm → 26× nonunion risk** |
| Rossi (203) | **Ischial tuberosity \~50%** > AIIS 25% > ASIS 20% > pubic symphysis 4% > iliac crest 1% · soccer + gymnastics |
| Schiller (lower-limb avulsions) | Surgery: displaced tibial tubercle, failed nonop, nerve impingement, **> 2 cm** |

**Most common site** — ischial tuberosity (Rossi) · AIIS (Schuett) · ASIS listed first (other OB) — read the options

## 6 Complications

**Missed ASIS avulsion** — **exostosis** needing excision

**Painful nonunion** — ORIF

## How it's asked

**"ASIS avulsion — eccentric contraction of?"** → **sartorius**

**"Sprinter, ASIS avulsion — treatment?"** → **progressive weightbearing, return when pain-free motion achieved**

**"True of the avulsed (ASIS) muscle's insertion?"** → **can be source of pes anserinus bursitis**

**"Sprinter, iliac crest avulsion — next step?"** → **protected weight bearing and activity as tolerated**

**"1 wk after ASIS avulsion, improving — management?"** → **weight-bearing as tolerated and crutches for 3 weeks total**

**"Bat swing, ASIS displaced 2 cm?"** → **rest and protected weight bearing with crutches**

**"ASIS avulsion — nerve to avulsed muscle?"** → **femoral**

**"Iliac crest avulsion — nerve to attached muscles?"** → **lower intercostal nerves**

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Articular Cartilage Defects of Knee

*Knee & Sports · 5 / 61*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology & pathomechanics

**Incidence** — **5–10% of people > 40 yr** have high-grade chondral lesions · isolated defects in athletes est. **36%** (OB)

**Chronic ACL tear** — **anterior lateral femoral condyle + posterolateral tibial plateau**

**OCD** — **70%** on **posterolateral (lateral aspect of) medial femoral condyle**

**Mechanism** — acute trauma or chronic repetitive overload → softening, fissuring, flap tears, delamination

**Threshold** — impaction **> 24 MPa** disrupts normal cartilage

**Healing** — **limited spontaneous healing + propensity to worsen**; defects isolated from blood supply / marrow → **no repair response** (basis of microfracture)

**Overloaded cartilage (runner)** — chondropenia, chondrocyte apoptosis, subchondral bruising, sclerosis, fracture, necrosis; cartilage volume ↑ linearly with loading until a threshold

### Genes & lab markers (collagen type of each repair tissue)

| Tissue / procedure | Collagen | Note |
|---|---|---|
| **Hyaline (articular) cartilage** | **Type II** | Columnar organisation + **lamina splendens** (separates it from "hyaline-like") |
| **Microfracture / drilling → fibrocartilage** | **Type I > II** (mostly I + III, some II) | Stiffer, less durable → deteriorates over time |
| Microfracture (TO) | **Types I, II and III** | Fibrocartilage alone → mix of fibrocartilage + hyaline-like |
| **ACI / MACI** | **I + II, type II predominates** | "Hyaline-like"; ACI (TO) may give hyaline-like, hybrid or fibrocartilage (I + II) |
| **OATS / mosaicplasty, OCA** | **Type II** | Direct transfer of articular cartilage; chondrocytes viable |
| Particulated juvenile cartilage allograft | Type II | Live cells in native ECM |
| Fibrous tissue | No type II | Made by fibrocytes |
| Elastic cartilage | — | Ear, epiglottis, larynx |

**Labs** — only to rule out inflammatory disease

## 3 Presentation

**History** — precipitating trauma common · **some found incidentally** on MRI / arthroscopy

**Symptoms** — asymptomatic vs localised pain, effusion, motion loss, **mechanical symptoms** (catching, locking) — esp. unstable / loose lesions

**Exam** — look for background factors: **laxity, malalignment, compartment overload**; ROM, ligaments, gait

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Study | Use / finding |
|---|---|
| Standing AP, lateral, **Merchant** | Rule out arthritis, bony defects; check alignment |
| **Semiflexed 45° PA** | **Most sensitive for early joint-space narrowing** |
| **Long-leg alignment** | **Mechanical axis** |
| CT | Bone loss; **TT-TG** for patellofemoral joint |
| **MRI** | **Most sensitive for focal defects**; FS T2, PD, T2 FSE ↑ sens / spec |
| dGEMRIC, T2 mapping | Evolving — cartilage defects + repair |
| MRI after microfracture | **High fill rate** = good short-term outcome |
| MRI ↑ T2 signal under defect | **Subchondral bone involvement / deficiency** → favours **OCA** (✗ Wrong options: ACI) |
| MRI axial slices superimposed | **TT-TG > 20 mm** = patellar maltracking |

![Semiflexed 45° PA view — most sensitive for early joint-space narrowing](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3133-45-deg-pa-xr.jpg)

### Outerbridge arthroscopic grading

| Grade | Orthobullets | TO version |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | Normal | — |
| **I** | **Softening + swelling** (probe) | Softening + swelling |
| **II** | **Partial-thickness**, surface fissures, **not to subchondral bone, ≤ 1.5 cm diameter** | Fragmentation + fissuring, area **< 0.5 inch** |
| **III** | **Deep fissures to subchondral bone, > 1.5 cm diameter** | Fragmentation + fissuring, area **> 0.5 inch** |
| **IV** | **Exposed subchondral bone** | **Erosion to bone** |

**II / III cut-off** — **1.5 cm diameter** (Orthobullets) · **0.5 inch area** (TO) — read the options

**Outerbridge grade IV** — **indication** for cartilage restoration, not a failure risk factor

### ICRS grading

| Grade | Finding |
|---|---|
| 0 | Normal |
| 1 | Nearly normal — superficial lesions |
| 2 | Abnormal — **< 50% cartilage depth** |
| 3 | Severely abnormal — **> 50% depth** |
| 4 | Severely abnormal — **through subchondral bone** |

### Chondromalacia patellae — diagnosis & grading

**Gold standard (TO)** — **arthroscopy**\
✗ Wrong options: X-ray · MRI · CT

| Modified Outerbridge — MRI (TO) | FS PD finding |
|---|---|
| I | Focal hyperintensity, **normal contour** |
| II | **Fissure or blister-like swelling** reaching surface |
| **III** | **Focal ulceration + "crab meat" lesions** |
| IV | **Full-thickness loss** + reactive underlying bone |

**Tram-track sign (TO)** — **mucoid degeneration of PCL**\
✗ Wrong options: crab meat

## 5 Management

### Decision factors & size thresholds

**Nonop first** — rest, NSAIDs, physio, weight loss when **mild**; viscosupplement, steroid, unloader brace — **controversial**, symptom relief, defect won't heal

**Treatment spectrum** (Alford) — **marrow stimulation (MST)** → **ACI** → **osteochondral grafting (OCG**, autograft / allograft)

**Patient factors** — age, skeletal maturity, demand, rehab tolerance · **defect factors** — size, location, **contained vs uncontained**, **subchondral bone involvement**

**First correct** — **malalignment, ligament instability, meniscal deficiency** (before / at time of cartilage surgery); patellofemoral → **maltracking + malalignment**

```
Femoral condyle defect (after correcting axis, ligaments, meniscus)
├─ < 4 cm² → MICROFRACTURE or OATS (palliative if older / low demand)
└─ > 4 cm² → OSTEOCHONDRAL ALLOGRAFT or ACI
```

```
Patellofemoral defect (after correcting maltracking)
├─ < 4 cm² → MICROFRACTURE or OATS
└─ > 4 cm² → ACI (microfracture if older / low demand)
```

| Size / bone status (OB algorithm) | Procedure |
|---|---|
| **< 1 cm², no osseous defect** | **Microfracture** |
| < 1 cm², osseous defect | OATS |
| **1–2 cm², no osseous defect** | Microfracture or ACI |
| 1–2 cm², osseous defect | OATS |
| **> 2 cm², osteochondral defect or AVN** | **OCA** or ACI + bone graft |

| Procedure | Size range quoted (source) |
|---|---|
| **Microfracture** | **< 2 cm²** / < 2 × 2 cm (Orthobullets, OB, TO) · < 1 cm² (OB patella, OB algorithm) · with OATS < 4 cm² (Orthobullets) |
| **OATS** | < 2 cm², some 2–4 cm² (OB) · up to 4–6 cm² (OB, OCD chapter) · grafting **up to 5 cm** (TO) |
| **ACI** | **1–10 cm²** (OB) · **2–10 cm²** large grade IV (TO) · > 4 cm² (Orthobullets) |
| **OCA** | **> 2 cm²** (OB) · > 4 cm² (Orthobullets, OB) · > 6 cm² (OB, OCD chapter) |
| Threshold debate | **4 cm²** usual; some use **2 cm²** (OB) — read the options |

### Operative criteria

| Indication | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Failed nonop** | Symptomatic defect |
| **Acute osteochondral fracture** | **Full-thickness cartilage loss** |
| Size (OB) | Defects **> 2 × 2 cm** failing nonop |
| **Arthroplasty instead** | **> 55 yr** (OB) · PF arthroplasty **40+ yr** with isolated PF arthritis |

### Debridement / chondroplasty

**Debridement / chondroplasty** — removes loose flaps → relieves mechanical symptoms · **short-term benefit 50–70%** · simple, fast rehab · leaves exposed bone; natural history unknown

**Chondroplasty for large PF defect** — only **older patients with diffuse degeneration**\
✗ Wrong options: young

### Microfracture & marrow stimulation — fibrocartilage, outcomes & overgrowth

**Principle** — perforate subchondral bone → **marrow MSCs** into defect → **fibrocartilage** · includes **microfracture, abrasion chondroplasty, drilling**\
✗ Wrong options: true hyaline regeneration

**Technique** — **stable vertical walls**; **remove calcified cartilage layer** (✓ should be done); awl holes **3–4 mm apart**; entry **perpendicular (90°)** to avoid skipping / hole collapse

**Osseous overgrowth** — risk ↑ with **removal / violation of subchondral plate** (over-aggressive debridement)\
✗ Wrong options: more holes per area · small awls · 90° entry · calcified layer removal

**Awl size** — smaller (**1.0 mm**) better than **1.2 mm** in some studies

**Drilling steps** — debride **sclerotic bone** → trim unstable cartilage edges → drill **past subchondral plate (\~4 mm)** → enough coverage between holes for a **mesenchymal clot**

**Drilling** — alters subchondral plate → micro-cysts + intralesional osteophytes (later fill with cancellous bone)

**Benefits** — cheap, **single-stage**, arthroscopic; **best for acute, contained lesions < 2 × 2 cm**

**Limits** — poor if **> 2 × 2 cm**; not for bone defects or **uncontained** lesions (no rim); **restricted WB 6–8 wk** (Orthobullets) · **\~6 wk** (OB) · NWB 4–6 wk + CPM (Orthobullets OCD note)

**Good short-term outcome** — **high MRI fill rate**, **low BMI**, **short preop symptoms** · **patellar microfracture → poor**

**Durability** — gains maintained only **2–3 yr**; not maintained long term

![Microfracture awl holes in a chondral defect](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3133-mfx.jpg)

### ACI / MACI — hyaline-like repair & patellofemoral indications

**ACI** — cell therapy → autologous **"hyaline-like"** cartilage · arthroscopic harvest from low-load area → culture → inject under **periosteal patch** at 2nd (open) surgery

**Needs** — **full-thickness cartilage margins**, healthy subchondral bone\
✗ Wrong options: significant osseous defect: **< 8 mm deep** OK (OB) · add bone graft if bone loss **> 1 cm** (OB, OCD chapter)

**Limits** — open, **2-stage**, prolonged protection · better histology than marrow stimulation; long-term ≈ microfracture

**Periosteal patch (TO)** — harvested **proximal tibia / distal femur**, slightly oversized · **cambium layer faces the defect** (down to bone) · sewn **watertight** with interrupted **6-0 absorbable** sutures + **fibrin glue** at margins · patch is **chondrogenic** + paracrine\
✗ Wrong options: cambium away · sewn into defect · mixed with matrix

**1st-stage harvest (TO)** — **200–300 mg** cartilage · cells expanded **2–3 wk** (TO) · implanted after **3–6 wk** (TO) — read the options

**Collagen membrane (TO)** — replaced periosteum → avoids **patch hypertrophy + harvest morbidity**

| ACI generation (TO) | Features |
|---|---|
| **1st** (Brittberg 1994) | **2-stage**: arthroscopic biopsy → open / mini-open implantation under **periosteal flap (PACI)** or collagen membrane |
| **2nd = MACI** | **Autologous chondrocytes on a scaffold for expansion**; 3D bioabsorbable scaffold press-fit (hyalograft C, bioseed-C) → **all-arthroscopic** implantation possible; NeoCart (bioreactor loading) |
| **3rd** | **Single stage**; allogeneic tissue or **stem cells**; CAIS (minced autologous chondrocytes + marrow MNCs on polycaprolactone + PGA + PDS scaffold); De Novo ET (minced juvenile allograft); CARTISTEM (umbilical cord MSCs, hyaluronate gel) |

**3rd vs 1st generation (TO)** — **all-arthroscopic, single-stage**\
✗ Wrong options: 2-stage · collagen cover · periosteal sheath

**Generation conflicts** — **MACI = 2nd generation** (TO) vs **3rd generation, first to use scaffold** (OB) · **CAIS = 2nd-gen single-stage** (one TO) vs **3rd-gen classic** (other TO) — read the options

**MACI** — cells cultured on **porcine collagen membrane** · fixed with **fibrin glue or sutures** · **only FDA-approved cell therapy for cartilage (USA)** · 2-stage, expensive · **type II > I**

**5 cm² MFC defect, 32 yr (TO)** — **ACI**\
✗ Wrong options: microfracture · UKA · mosaicplasty

**ACI in PF joint** — **JSN on Merchant view = contraindication** · acceptable: grade 4 MFC / trochlea lesion, varus axis (corrected), concomitant **Fulkerson AMZ** · **ACI not FDA-approved for patella** (off-label)

**PF defect + recurrent dislocation, TT-TG 26 mm, 2.2 × 2.1 cm, exposed bone** — **MPFL reconstruction + autologous chondrocyte implantation + antero-medializing (Fulkerson) TTO**\
✗ Wrong options: lateralising TTO (↑ Q angle) · microfracture (< 1 cm² only) · PF arthroplasty (40+ yr)

**PF lesions** — inferior outcomes vs condyles (**high shear**) · **TT-TG > 20 mm** = maltracking → AMZ protects the repair · recurrent dislocation may avulse the **MPFL patellar insertion** (osseous fragment)

![ACI for a patellar chondral defect](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3133-aci-for-patellar-chondral-defect.jpg)

### Osteochondral allograft — indications, contraindications & failure risk

**Goal** — **live chondrocytes in mature matrix + underlying bone** · **fresh, refrigerated** grafts · bulk (screws) or shell / dowel press-fit · donor = **cadaver hemicondyle** · size + **radius of curvature** matched\
✗ Wrong options: freeze-dried

**Indications** — **large defects (> 2 cm² / > 4 cm²)**, **subchondral bone loss / oedema / AVN**, **uncontained** lesions, **revision** of failed techniques · **single-stage**

**OCA over ACI** — **depth of osseous involvement**\
✗ Wrong options: footprint > 6 cm² · location · age · time since injury

**Contraindications** — **ligament instability, meniscal insufficiency, uncorrectable malalignment, multicompartment / tricompartmental arthrosis, inflammatory arthropathy** · ✓ defect > 2 cm² is an **indication**

**Failure risk** — **patellofemoral joint defect** (adverse biomechanics) · **bipolar** lesions\
✗ Wrong options: unipolar · male sex · Outerbridge IV · concomitant AMZ

**Outcomes** — PF worse than condyles (contact pressure) · **> 80% return** to activity in young · limits: **availability, cost**, infection / immune risk\
✗ Wrong options: more easily available

**Immune response** — only a concern with **allograft** · allografts **cleansed in antibiotics** before implantation\
✗ Wrong options: OATS

**Varus axis + large MFC lesion** — correct alignment **before / at time** of OCA\
✗ Wrong options: isolated OCA

**MFC 25 × 25 mm, 32 yr** — **osteochondral allograft** (ACI also correct if listed)\
✗ Wrong options: 1–2 autograft plugs · microfracture · UKA · abrasion

**MFC 1.7 × 1.3 cm (2.21 cm²) + loose bodies, neutral axis** — **removal of loose osteochondral bodies + osteochondral allograft transplantation to the MFC** · loose bodies sit in **suprapatellar pouch**; hip-to-ankle film neutral, sunrise view normal\
✗ Wrong options: osteotomy · microfracture (osseous defect) · OATS alone (loose bodies stay)

![Osteochondral allograft dowel](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3133-osteochondral-allograft-dowel.jpg)

### Osteochondral autograft (OATS) — graft fixation & donor site

**Principle** — cylinders of normal cartilage + bone from **low-load areas** (periphery of trochlea, notch) press-fit into recipient socket · chondrocytes **viable**, cartilage **heals**, biopsy **type II**\
✗ Wrong options: scaffold · replaced by fibrocartilage

**Benefits** — autologous, cheap, **single-stage**, arthroscopic possible · **limits** — size + **donor-site morbidity**, curvature matching

**Fixation strength** — **initially decreases in early healing, then increases with subchondral bone healing** → **delay WB 3 months**; early non-WB motion

**Donor site** — **distal medial trochlea** = **lowest PF contact pressure + no tibiofemoral contact** · also **peripheral / superior lateral trochlea** · grafts **< 10 mm wide**\
✗ Wrong options: central trochlea · condyles (weight-bearing)

**Ahmad map (figure)** — 1 proximal medial trochlea · 2 medial condyle · 3 central trochlea · **4 distal medial trochlea ✓** · 5 lateral condyle

![OATS donor sites — distal medial trochlea has the lowest contact pressure](../../MCQ/attachments/q216272-illa-femur.jpg)

### Malalignment & unloading procedures (osteotomy, tibial tubercle transfer)

| Procedure | Indication | Contraindication / risk |
|---|---|---|
| **Maquet** (anteriorisation) | **Distal pole** lesions only | **Superior patellar arthrosis**; elevate **≤ 1 cm** → else **skin necrosis** |
| **Fulkerson AMZ** (anterior + medial) | **Lateral + distal pole** lesions, ↑ Q angle, offload PF restoration, **bipolar PF lesions** | **Superior-medial patellar arthrosis**, **medial facet + MFC arthrosis**, skeletal immaturity |
| **Distal femoral varus osteotomy** | **Valgus** + lateral compartment chondral disease | — |
| HTO (medial opening wedge) | Varus-pattern disease | Not if axis neutral |

**Scope before** tubercle transfer — need **intact proximal + medial cartilage**

**Anteriorised 2.5 cm** — **skin necrosis** / wound breakdown\
✗ Wrong options: instability · nonunion · progression · fracture

**Lateral FC + plateau chondromalacia, 10° valgus, 31 yr** — **distal femoral varus osteotomy** · **5° valgus → 5-fold risk of progressing ≥ 1 grade in 18 months** (Sharma)\
✗ Wrong options: OAT · UKA · HTO · Fulkerson

**PF joint load** — up to **6.5 × body weight** in flexion

### Fixation of unstable fragments & instability-related lesions

**Fixation** — needs fragment with **adequate subchondral bone** · debride base, ± drill / local graft · absorbable or nonabsorbable screws · **best: unstable OCD, open physes** · lower healing when mature · **headless nonabsorbable screws removed at 3–6 months**

**Acute patellar dislocation + osteochondral fragment + MPFL tear** — **fragment fixation (ORIF) + MPFL reconstruction** (best: **14-year-old female athlete, first-time dislocation, imaging shown in Figure D**) · sites: **patella + LFC** · chronic, no fragment → OATS\
✗ Wrong options: fixation without OC defect · partial-thickness chronic defect · bucket-handle tear · Outerbridge IV OA

**Unfixable unstable MFC flap 1 × 1.5 cm, single procedure → type I + II** — **microfracture**

### Osteochondritis dissecans & instability-related fragments

**Commonest site (TO)** — **knee: lateral surface of medial femoral condyle** (classic, posterolateral MFC) · others: **talus anteromedial corner**, **femoral head superomedial**, **capitellum**, **1st MT head**\
✗ Wrong options: LFC

**Who (TO)** — **males**, adolescents · **commonest cause of loose body** in knee in adolescents / young adults

**Catchers (OB)** — **posterior femoral condyle** (hyperflexion loading) \
✗ Wrong options: anterior LFC / posterolateral plateau (ACL injury pattern) · patella · trochlea

**Wilson test (TO)** — seated, legs hanging · **pain on internal rotation**, relieved by external rotation

**Imaging (TO)** — X-ray: loose body / loosely attached fragment · **MRI = investigation of choice**, earliest diagnosis, guides management · **MRI = best to follow healing**\
✗ Wrong options: CT (insensitive) · bone scan (can't separate healed vs unhealed; high Tc uptake → repair potential) · SPECT

**"Healing" MRI criteria (TO17118770)** — (1) round homogeneous high signal **≥ 5 mm** beneath lesion · (2) articular focal defect **≥ 5 mm** wide · (3) high-signal line through cartilage + subchondral plate into lesion — another TO lists the same ≥ 5 mm signs as **De Smet instability criteria** — read the options

**JOCD (TO)** — **activity modification** first · most heal · small lesions → **multiple drilling** · large → **mosaicplasty / OATS**\
✗ Wrong options: in situ fixation · microfracture · excision

| ROCK / De Smet (TO) | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Nonop protocol** | Stop impact sport, protected WB, unloader brace or cast · **≥ 3–6 months**; healing on 6-month MRI → extend to **12–18 months** |
| **Strongest success predictor** | **Younger age / open physes** (boys **< 14**, girls **< 12**) |
| Other success predictors | Small **normalised lesion width**; **no cysts** (< 1.3 mm may still heal at 12 months); extreme medial / lateral (coronal) or far posterior (sagittal) site |
| **ROCK nomogram** | Age + normalised width + cyst size → **> 48% healing probability at 6 months** → continue nonop |
| **Strongest failure predictor** | **Subchondral cysts > 5 mm on MRI** (fluid intrusion = instability) (✗ Wrong options: sclerotic rim (common, chronicity)) |
| **De Smet instability** | **T2 fluid line ≥ 5 mm** behind fragment · **cysts ≥ 5 mm** · **focal cartilage defect ≥ 5 mm** · fluid through cartilage into bone |
| Other failure signs | Closing physes, **mechanical symptoms**, **patella / LFC** site, no progress after 3–6 months |
| Surgery options | Trans- / retroarticular **drilling** (intact cartilage), **internal fixation** (unstable), MACI / OCA (unsalvageable) |

**Stable lesion (TO)** — no fluid behind fragment, **firm on probing** → failed conservative → **drilling alone**

**Unstable but salvageable, immature (TO)** — circumferential T2 fluid, **ballotable**, minimally displaced → **in situ fixation of the fragment using bioabsorbable screws, darts or compression screws** ± graft\
✗ Wrong options: drilling alone · excision + microfracture · OATS

## 6 Complications

| Complication | Fact |
|---|---|
| **Osseous overgrowth** (microfracture) | **Subchondral plate removal** → ↓ repair cartilage volume |
| Microfracture deterioration | Type I fibrocartilage; gains last **2–3 yr** |
| **OCA dropped on floor** | **40% contamination**, **Staphylococcus** commonest → **5-min povidone-iodine bath + saline rinse** (max cell viability, ↓ bacteria) |
| OCA | Infection / immune reaction risk |
| **Maquet > 1 cm** | **Skin necrosis** |
| OATS | **Donor-site morbidity** |
| ACI periosteal patch | **Hypertrophy** + harvest morbidity |
| Retained headless screws | Remove at **3–6 months** |

## X vs Y

| | **Microfracture** | **OATS** | **OCA** | **ACI / MACI** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Stages | **1** | **1** | **1** | **2** (3rd-gen ACI: 1) |
| Tissue | **Fibrocartilage (type I)** | Hyaline (II) | Hyaline (II), live cells | **Hyaline-like (II > I)** |
| Healing source | **Marrow MSCs** | Transplanted autologous chondrocytes | Donor chondrocytes; bone by **creeping substitution** | Cultured **autologous** (✗ Wrong options: allogeneic) chondrocytes |
| Size | Small, **contained** | Small | **Large**, uncontained OK | Large (1–10 cm²) |
| Bone loss | No | ✓ small | ✓ **best** | No (needs bone graft) |
| Key issue | Overgrowth, durability | Donor site | Availability, cost, immune | Cost, open, rehab |

## How it's asked

**"Reparative tissue after microfracture (figure)?"** → **fibrocartilage**\
✗ Wrong options: hyaline · fibrous tissue · elastofibroma

**"Tissue after drilling an 8 × 7 mm defect?"** → **fibrocartilage**

**"Microfracture (B) vs mosaicplasty (C) — true?"** → **biopsy of repair site at 3 months will reveal more type I collagen in technique B**\
✗ Wrong options: both 2-stage (only ACI)

**"Short-term microfracture success?"** → **high fill rate of the defect on MRI**\
✗ Wrong options: high BMI · long symptoms · patella · ACL deficiency

**"Risk of osseous overgrowth after microfracture?"** → **removal of the subchondral plate**

**"Single procedure stimulating type I + II collagen?"** → **microfracture**

**"Awl through subchondral bone — true?"** → **content of type I cartilage generated is greater than type II**\
✗ Wrong options: lasts 5 yr · uncontained · no WB limit · > 4 cm²

**"Predominantly type I collagen in defect?"** → **microfracture**

**"Type II > type I collagen on healed surface?"** → **matrix-associated autologous chondrocyte implantation**\
✗ Wrong options: single-core OATS (type II only)

**"OATS — graft at 1 year?"** → **transplanted chondrocytes viable, articular cartilage heals, biopsy shows type II collagen**

**"5 cm² MFC defect, 32 yr (TO)?"** → **ACI**

**"ACI periosteal patch (TO)?"** → **cambium layer faces towards chondral defect**

**"Second-generation ACI (TO)?"** → **autologous chondrocytes scaffold used for chondrocyte expansion**

**"Third vs first generation ACI (TO)?"** → **all-arthroscopic procedure**

**"ACI in PF joint — EXCEPT?"** → **joint space narrowing on Merchant view**

**"Recurrent dislocation, TT-TG 26 mm, 2.2 × 2.1 cm medial facet defect?"** → **MPFL reconstruction + ACI + antero-medializing tibial tubercle osteotomy**

**"MFC 25 × 25 mm, 32 yr?"** → **osteochondral allograft**

**"OCA contraindications — EXCEPT?"** → **osteochondral defect measuring > 2 cm²**

**"Runner, 2.21 cm² MFC osteochondral defect + loose bodies?"** → **removal of loose osteochondral bodies + osteochondral allograft transplantation to the MFC**

**"Best candidate for isolated OCA (radiograph depicted)?"** → **30-year-old man, chronic atraumatic knee pain, 4.2 cm² medial femoral condyle osteochondral lesion, neutral axis (Figure A)**\
✗ Wrong options: 67 yr / 55 yr (arthroplasty) · varus axis · 1.5 cm² (nonop)

**"OCA — true?"** → **replaces cartilage defect with live chondrocytes in mature matrix with underlying bone**\
✗ Wrong options: freeze-dried

**"OCA rather than ACI — deciding factor?"** → **depth of osseous involvement**

**"Highest risk of OCA failure?"** → **patellofemoral joint cartilage defect**\
✗ Wrong options: male · unipolar · Outerbridge IV · AMZ

**"OCA — true?"** → **can be useful in addressing subchondral bone deficiency**\
✗ Wrong options: 2-stage · PF better · uncontained excluded · easy availability

**"Mosaicplasty graft fixation strength?"** → **initially decreases during early healing, then increases with subchondral bone healing**

**"Best knee donor site for capitellar OATS (figure)?"** → **distal medial trochlea (location 4)**

**"Medial facet + MFC arthrosis — contraindicated?"** → **anteromedial (Fulkerson) tibial tubercle osteotomy**

**"Lateral compartment chondromalacia, 10° valgus?"** → **distal femoral varus osteotomy**

**"Tubercle anteriorised 2.5 cm — complication?"** → **skin necrosis**

**"Most common site of OCD (TO)?"** → **medial femoral condyle of femur** (lateral surface)

**"Juvenile OCD femoral condyle — treatment (TO)?"** → **activity modification**

**"Best investigation to follow OCD healing (TO)?"** → **MRI**

**"Immature, failed 6 months, circumscribing T2 fluid line, ballotable (TO)?"** → **in situ fixation of the fragment with bioabsorbable screws, darts or compression screws**

**"Strongest predictor of nonop failure in JOCD — ROCK (TO)?"** → **presence of subchondral cystic changes deep to the lesion measuring greater than 5 mm on MRI**

**"OCD site in a baseball catcher?"** → **posterior femoral condyle**

**"Candidate for fragment fixation + MPFL reconstruction?"** → **14-year-old female athlete, first-time dislocation, imaging shown in Figure D**

**"Gold standard for chondromalacia patellae (TO)?"** → **arthroscopy**

**"Crab meat lesions on MRI (TO)?"** → **grade III chondromalacia patellae**\
✗ Wrong options: tram track (PCL mucoid degeneration)

# Athletic Pubalgia & Adductor strain

*Knee & Sports · 6 / 61*

## 1 Basics

**Definition (TO)** — **chronic lower abdominal + groin pain without a true hernia** · aka **sports hernia, Gilmore's groin, core muscle injury (CMI)**

**Who** — **males > females** (TO: **narrower male pelvis** → greater force shifts, less stability) · **hockey + soccer** · high-level athletes (forces ↑ as strength ↑) (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: more common in females

**Sports hip triad** — **adductor strain + osteitis pubis + athletic pubalgia** (adductor strain itself: see Adductor Strain chapter)

### Anatomy — the pubic fulcrum (TO)

**Fulcrum (TO)** — **pubic symphysis** = common attachment of **rectus abdominis + adductor longus**\
✗ Wrong options: main muscle transversus abdominis

**Pubic aponeurosis (TO)** — **rectus abdominis + conjoint tendon (internal oblique + transversus abdominis) + external oblique** → confluent with **adductor longus + gracilis** at the symphysis

**Opposing pull (TO)** — rectus abdominis vs adductors act in **opposite directions** on the symphysis → **imbalance = origin of pubalgia**

**Pain source** — injury to the **common aponeurosis between rectus abdominis and adductor longus** (Orthobullets)

![Sports hernia: injured tissue at the rectus–adductor aponeurosis](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3092-sports-hernia.jpg)

## 2 Mechanism

### Mechanism & surgical referral

**Movement** — **repetitive hip extension + abduction** (± external rotation) with **eccentric adductor contraction** · abdominal hyperextension + thigh abduction (Orthobullets)\
✗ Wrong options: extension + adduction · flexion + abduction / adduction · extension + IR · flexion + IR · lumbar hyperextension

**Imbalance** — **strong adductors vs weak rectus** (lower-limb-focused training) (OB) · **strong adductors vs weak hip abductors** (OB)

**Type** — overuse microtrauma or acute trauma

**Activities (TO)** — running, kicking, cutting, twisting, explosive turns, change of direction

**What tears** — shear on rectus → **transversalis fascia, rectus, adductor magnus origin** (OB) · **adductor longus** (Orthobullets, coronal MRI) · inguinal hernia + **internal oblique** micro-tears at surgery (OB) — read the options

## 3 Presentation

**Pain** — lower abdominal + **inguinal pain at extremes of exertion** · **unilateral**, exercise-related, radiates to **perineum, inner thigh, scrotum** (TO) · settles with rest, **returns with play** (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: bilateral

**Exam** — **tender adductor longus** · tender **pubic tubercle / inguinal region**, pain over pubic rami · **pain with Valsalva + sit-ups** (rectus aponeurosis) · pain on **resisted hip adduction** · **not** worse with hip rotation

**No palpable hernia** — on Valsalva · differentiate from **subtle true inguinal hernia**

**Check hip** — **concomitant FAI** may coexist

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Study | Finding / use |
|---|---|
| X-ray, MRI, bone scan | **Rule out other causes** (Orthobullets) |
| **MRI** | **Oedema adjacent to the aponeurosis** · coronal T2: adductor longus tear **retracted from common aponeurosis** · "helpful for definitive diagnosis" (OB) vs "**not very helpful**" / diagnosis **largely clinical** (other OB) — read the options |
| CT pelvis | Rules out **bony injury (osteitis pubis)** |
| Scrotal US | **No added value** |

![Coronal T2: adductor longus tear retracted from the common aponeurosis](../../MCQ/attachments/q215514-a-screen-shot-2021-05-04-at-7-57-32-am.jpg)

## 5 Management

```
Athletic pubalgia
├─ First line → REST + PT (core) ± NSAIDs / injections
└─ Failed nonoperative → SPORTS HERNIA SURGEON
       ├─ Pelvic floor / posterior wall repair, rectus reattachment
       └─ Adductor pain + dysfunction → ADDUCTOR TENOTOMY / RELEASE
```

### Nonoperative

**Duration** — **6–8 weeks** (Orthobullets, OB) · core stability **≥ 2 months** before surgery (OB) · surgery if no improvement in **3 months** (TO) — read the options

**Physiotherapy (TO)** — **core strengthening + stabilisation**, restore **pelvic tilt**, postural training

**Drugs (TO)** — NSAIDs, **oral steroid taper**

**Injections (TO)** — selective **corticosteroid or PRP** into rectus abdominis and / or adductor longus origin

### Operative criteria

| Indication | Procedure |
|---|---|
| **Failed extensive nonoperative care** | **Pelvic floor repair (hernia operation)** · mesh hernia repair · **adductor / rectus recession** |
| Failed nonop (TO) | **Reinforce posterior wall + fix rectus abdominis or conjoint tendon** |
| **Adductor pain + dysfunction** (TO) | **Adductor tenotomy** / release |
| Failed nonop (Orthobullets) | **Decompress genital branch of genitofemoral nerve** |

**Pro soccer player, failed nonop, no hernia, malignancy excluded** — **refer to a sports hernia surgeon** (strengthen anterior pelvic floor)\
✗ Wrong options: more nonop · scrotal US · CT pelvis · **cortisone — doesn't strengthen pelvic floor**

**Groin pain, normal imaging** — tends **not to improve without hernia-type surgery** (OB)

**Outcome** — rectus / adductor **aponeurotic plate repair → 96% return to sport**

## X vs Y

| Sports hip triad | **Athletic pubalgia** | **Adductor strain** | **Osteitis pubis** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lesion | Rectus–adductor **aponeurosis** | **Adductor longus** (pubic ramus) | **Pubic symphysis** inflammation |
| Onset | Chronic, exertional | **Acute**, immediate | Gradual, vague |
| Tender | **Inguinal region / lower abdomen**, adductor longus | Subcutaneous border of pubic ramus | **Directly over symphysis** |
| Provoked by | **Sit-ups, Valsalva** | **Resisted adduction** | Resisted rectus testing, adduction / abduction |
| Imaging | Oedema at aponeurosis | Avulsion / oedema at pubis | **Erosions, symphysis widening**, marrow oedema |
| Failed nonop | **Surgery** | Rehab (repair unproven) | Rest (self-limiting) |

## How it's asked

**"True about athletic pubalgia (TO)?"** → **fulcrum is the pubic symphysis**\
✗ Wrong options: more common in females · bilateral radiating pain · transversus abdominis main muscle

**"Movements contributing to athletic pubalgia?"** → **hip extension and abduction**

**"Failed nonop, no hernia, malignancy excluded — next?"** → **referral to a sports hernia surgeon**

# Burners & Stingers

*Knee & Sports · 7 / 61*

## 1 Basics

### Definition & epidemiology

**Definition** — **transient brachial plexus (or cervical root) neurapraxia** = "**dead arm syndrome**" · **mild traction injury of the brachial plexus (TO)** · reversible peripheral nerve injury

**Commonest** — **most common nerve injury in sportspersons (TO)** · most common UE neurologic injury in athletes (OB)

**Sports** — collision: **football**, rugby, ice hockey, wrestling (TO) · under-reported

**Recurrence** — **1 stinger → 3× risk of another** · **stinger in previous season = strongest risk factor** for injury + lasting symptoms (rugby: 33.9% had a history)

**Stenosis** — **Torg ratio < 0.8 → 3× stinger risk** (47.5% of stinger group vs 25.1%) · cervical canal + **foraminal stenosis** ↑ risk → work up recurrent burners

### Anatomy — C5-6 upper trunk

**Commonest level** — **C5-6 (upper trunk)** — greater midcervical mobility in young athletes\
✗ Wrong options: C3-4, C4-5, C6-7, C7-T1

| Root | Muscles tested |
|---|---|
| **C5** | **Deltoid, biceps**, rotator cuff (external rotators) |
| **C6** | Biceps (**elbow flexion + supination**), wrist extensors |
| ✗ Wrong option: C7 | Triceps, wrist flexors |
| ✗ Wrong option: C8 / C8-T1 | Finger flexors / interossei |

**Dorsal root ganglion** — fills most of the neural foramen → takes the brunt → **purely sensory** stinger possible

![Brachial plexus — motor and sensory innervation](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3115-brachial-plexus-with-sensory-566-moved.jpg)

## 2 Mechanism

| Mechanism | How | Typical |
|---|---|---|
| **Traction** (commonest) | **Arm / shoulder depressed + neck bent AWAY** from injured side · TO: shoulder + head distracted opposite ways | **Younger** athletes — **plexus** |
| **Compression** | **Head turned / extended TOWARD affected side** → root pinched in neural foramen | **Older** athletes — **foraminal stenosis** |
| **Direct blow** | **Erb's point**, just superior to clavicle | Plexus |

![Traction mechanism — shoulder depression with neck flexed away](../../MCQ/attachments/q215512-illa-stinger-mech.jpg)

## 3 Presentation

**Symptoms** — **unilateral** burning / stinging pain + paresthesia, shoulder → hand · **not dermatomal / not isolated to one area** ✓

**Weakness** — **C5-6: deltoid, biceps**, external rotators · often > 1 shoulder muscle group (ER, flexors, abductors) (TO)

**Never bilateral (TO)** — **bilateral or lower-limb symptoms → spinal cord injury (cervical cord neurapraxia)** until proven otherwise

**Neck exam** — **full, painless cervical ROM**, no tenderness · neck ROM **doesn't** reproduce symptoms · **positive Spurling's possible** ✓ · ROM often ↓ in chronic cases (TO)\
✗ Wrong options: neck pain on extension

**Course** — resolves quickly, **doesn't progress** · range: paresthesia → **complete arm monoplegia** · seconds → weeks if severe\
✗ Wrong options: progresses 20–25 min

| Duration of symptoms | Source |
|---|---|
| **1–2 min** | Orthobullets, OB |
| Seconds to several minutes | OB |
| **15–20 min** | OB |
| Several minutes; **5–10% last up to several weeks** | TO |
| "Resolution in **6–8 weeks**" accepted as **true** | TO (EXCEPT stem) — read the options |

**Subtle deltoid weakness** — mistaken for shoulder injury · **4/5 deltoid → withhold**\
✗ Wrong options: greater tuberosity tenderness = cuff contusion · AC pain · O'Brien's · labral shear (play on if strength full)

### Differentials

| Condition | Distinguishing fact |
|---|---|
| **Stinger** | Unilateral UE, C5-6, resolves in minutes, no residual |
| Acute transient spinal cord injury | **Temporary complete paralysis UE + LE**, resolves **< 24 h** |
| Central cord syndrome | **UE > LE**, mostly **elderly** |
| Nerve root avulsion | **Permanent** deficit, poor prognosis · **neurotmesis = most severe "stinger"** (arm-tackle traction) |
| Guillain-Barré | **Ascending** paralysis, legs → arms → face, **loss of DTRs** |

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Test | Indication / finding |
|---|---|
| **C-spine radiographs** | Usually normal · **recurrent stingers** → rule out **fracture + cervical stenosis** |
| **MRI C-spine** | **Bilateral symptoms** (not a stinger) → HNP, stenosis |
| **EMG** | **Persistent > 3 wk** (Orthobullets, OB) · **delay 3–4 wk** after injury (OB) · abnormal roots, cords, trunks, peripheral nerves |

**Formal work-up** — neurologic findings present, **persistent symptoms**, prior brachial plexus injury · = EMG + radiographs + MRI

**Not MRI triggers** — unilateral symptoms > 15 min · two prior unilateral episodes (→ **radiographs**) · transient deltoid + biceps weakness · **brief LOC → CT head**, not C-spine MRI

## 5 Management

```
Arm numbness / tingling after contact
├─ Bilateral or lower-limb signs → cord injury → MRI before any RTP
├─ Unilateral, fully resolved, full strength + painless neck ROM → same-day RTP
├─ Recurrent stingers → out for game → C-spine radiographs / imaging
└─ Weakness persists → withhold → EMG / NCS at 3–4 wk
```

### Return to play & red flags (bilateral or recurrent symptoms)

| RTP criteria | Contraindications to RTP |
|---|---|
| **Complete resolution of symptoms** | **Bilateral symptoms** (until MRI) |
| **Normal (5/5) strength**, normal sensation | **Multiple stingers** (until off-field work-up) |
| **Full, painless ROM of neck + shoulder** | **Recurring symptoms** (until C-spine X-rays) |
| Normal cervical exam — compression, **Spurling's, Adson's**, resisted head pressure | **Persistent** symptoms / weakness |

**Not RTP criteria** — fitting a brace · MRI excluding avulsion · normal EMG · partial sensation + arm overhead

| Number of stingers | RTP recommendation |
|---|---|
| **First** | RTP after **pain-free full ROM + normal strength** |
| **Second** | **Withhold rest of game** → re-examine → next game if no deficit |
| **Third** (any duration) | **Withhold rest of game + advanced C-spine imaging** (foraminal / uncovertebral stenosis, disc herniation) |
| Any, **persistent pain / weakness** | **Withhold** + **electrodiagnostic study** |

**Same-contest return** — **allowed after complete resolution** (Orthobullets, OB) · TO counts "**precludes participation in present contest**" as true — read the options

**3rd stinger (2 today + 1 last week), resolved in 5 min** — **withhold from the remainder of the game + pursue further off-field workup**\
✗ Wrong options: RTP after halftime · RTP once asymptomatic · 2 wk + EMG (only for persistent weakness)

**Biceps weakness > 10 min + 2 stingers last season** — **hold until off-field work-up incl. C-spine radiographs**\
✗ Wrong options: agility test · "benign" immediate RTP · graduated RTP protocol (not a concussion)

**Prevention** — **prevent recurrence** = key · neck collars / rolls (football) · avoid spear tackling · fitted equipment · recurrent stingers → possible **chronic proximal arm weakness + pain**

## X vs Y

| | **Stinger / burner** | **Cervical cord neurapraxia (transient quadriparesis)** |
|---|---|---|
| Lesion | **Root or brachial plexus** (peripheral) | **Spinal cord** |
| Limbs | **Unilateral upper limb** | **Bilateral**, > 1 limb ± legs |
| Mechanism | Traction, compression, Erb's point blow | Hyperextension, hyperflexion, **axial load** |
| Resolution | Minutes | **10 min – 48 h** (OB) · minutes – hours (Orthobullets) |
| Imaging | Radiographs if recurrent | **MRI** before RTP |
| Same-day RTP | **Yes** if fully resolved | **No** |

| | **Younger athlete** | **Older athlete** |
|---|---|---|
| Cause | **Traction** of plexus (midcervical mobility) | **Foraminal stenosis + extension → root compression** |

## How it's asked

**"All true about stingers EXCEPT (TO)?"** → **bilateral arm weakness + paresthesia**

**"Acute transient finding consistent with a stinger?"** → **unilateral upper-extremity pain + weakness**

**"Muscles most likely affected?"** → **deltoid + biceps**

**"Shoulder + finger numbness gone in 15 min, no residual?"** → **stinger / burner**\
✗ Wrong options: transient cord injury · central cord · root avulsion · GBS

**"Most commonly involved level?"** → **C5-6**

**"Finding consistent with a stinger?"** → **physical examination may show a positive Spurling's sign**

**"Where were the paresthesias (figure)?"** → **not isolated to any single area**

**"Indication for C-spine MRI before RTP?"** → **symptoms + signs in both upper extremities**

**"Condition before RTP after a stinger?"** → **no pain, normal sensation, full ROM, 5/5 strength**

**"Exam finding that keeps him out?"** → **full painless ROM but 4/5 deltoid strength**

**"Third stinger, resolved in minutes — next step?"** → **withhold from remainder of game, pursue further off-field workup**

**"Persistent biceps weakness + 2 prior stingers?"** → **hold until off-field work-up incl. C-spine radiographs**

**"Risk of another stinger after one?"** → **3×**

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Cardiac Conditions

*Knee & Sports · 8 / 61*

## 1 Basics

### Overview

**Leading cause** — cardiac conditions = **most common cause of sudden death in young athletes** → **HCM (most common)**, coronary artery disease, commotio cordis

**Conditions covered** — syncope, HCM, CAD, long QT syndrome, commotio cordis, sudden cardiac death

### Sudden cardiac death – epidemiology & causes

**Definition** — death from a cardiovascular cause **within 1 hour of symptom onset**

**Incidence** — **0.76–13 per 100,000** (Orthobullets) · up to **1 in 40,000** young athletes (OB) · **2.8×** commoner in adolescent / teenage athletes than non-athletes

**Mechanism** — arrhythmia → ventricular ectopy → sustained **VT / VF**; usually a **pre-existing condition**

| Age group | Usual cause |
|---|---|
| Birth – 13 yr | **Congenital abnormality** |
| 14 – 24 yr | **HCM**, ARVC, congenital coronary anomalies, WPW, Marfan |
| Young athletes (TO) | **HCM + ARVC > 50%**; congenital coronary anomalies + premature coronary atherosclerosis **20%** |
| Structurally normal heart (TO) | **Ion channelopathies** — congenital long QT, Brugada, CPVT |
| **> 35 yr** | **Atherosclerotic CAD** — **> 80%** of SCD (TO) |

**Most common cause, < 35 yr** — **hypertrophic cardiomyopathy** ("cardiomyopathy" in TO)\
✗ Wrong options: CAD · Brugada · channelopathy · commotio cordis · aortic rupture · myocarditis · long QT · WPW

| Cause at autopsy (OB, Garrick) | Share |
|---|---|
| **HCM** | **36%** |
| Coronary anomalies | 19% |
| Increased cardiac mass | 10% |
| Ruptured aorta (Marfan) | 5% |
| Dilated cardiomyopathy | 3% |
| Mitral valve prolapse | 2% |

**HCM share** — **36%** (OB, Garrick) · **20.6%**, then idiopathic LVH 13.4%, coronary anomalies 12.0% (OB illustration) — read the options

![Causes of sudden death in athletes](../../MCQ/attachments/q215478-illa-causes-for-sudden-death-in-athletes-diagram.jpg)

**2nd commonest cause** — **coronary artery disease** (Orthobullets; mostly older) · coronary anomalies 19% (OB, Garrick) · **anomalous origin of left main coronary** → compression → ischaemia

**Risk factors** — **male**, **African-American (black)** race, **cardiomyopathy**, **contact sports**, **basketball** (OB) · false: "commoner in white American males" = the incorrect statement (TO)

**High-risk sports (TO)** — **basketball (most deaths)**, American football, soccer

**Age trend** — SCD **commoner in adolescent than adult athletes** (true statement, TO) · risk **increases with age** (Orthobullets) — read the options

**High-risk markers (TO)** — **unheralded syncope**, **LVH > 30 mm**, **non-sustained VT**, **family history of SCD**, **↓ BP in response to exercise**

**Prognosis** — **> 90% mortality**; early recognition + **AED** ↑ survival

**Survival (TO)** — **time from arrest to defibrillation** = single most important factor · **one shock → immediate CPR**

**Sequence (TO)** — **C-A-B** = chest compressions, airway, breathing · AHA **30:2** compression : ventilation (OB)

**AED** — primarily for **ventricular fibrillation**; may ↑ survival by **90%** (OB)\
✗ Wrong options: AF · bradycardia · tachycardia · asystole

### Genes & lab markers

| Condition | Inheritance / gene / lab |
|---|---|
| **HCM** | **Autosomal dominant**, variable penetrance; mostly **missense** (less often frameshift) |
| HCM genes | **TNNT2** (cardiac troponin T), TNNI3 (troponin I), **MYL2** (regulatory light chain), **MYL3** (essential light chain) |
| Long QT | Mostly **AD** (Orthobullets) · AD or AR (TO) · genetic testing for all, **yield 70%** (TO) |
| Long QT labs | **Basic metabolic panel + magnesium** (electrolytes worsen QT) |
| SCD work-up | **Cardiac enzymes**; genetic testing for **Brugada, LQTS, ARVC** |

### Athlete's heart – physiology

**Well-conditioned heart (OB)** — **↑ wall thickness** → **↑ stroke volume** · **↑ vagal tone** → **↓ resting HR** ✓ · CO = SV × HR, ↑ in exercise\
✗ Wrong options: ↓ SV · ↓ CO · ↓ wall thickness · ↓ vagal tone

**Normal ECG in endurance athletes** — **ventricular hypertrophy**, **1st-degree (primary) AV block**, **nonspecific ST-T changes in lateral leads**, **resting sinus bradycardia 40 bpm**

**Athletic heart syndrome** — physiological **LVH may mimic HCM** ✓ (mild phenotypes overlap) · asymptomatic, normal exam · **no extra SCD risk**

## 2 Mechanism

### Commotio cordis – mechanism

**Mechanism** — **blunt blow to anterior chest (precordium) during ventricular repolarization** → **upstroke of T wave** → aberrant depolarization → **ventricular fibrillation**\
✗ Wrong options: during depolarization

**Vulnerable window** — T-wave upstroke = **1%** of cardiac cycle (Orthobullets) · **< 1%** (OB)

**Physics** — energy **> 50 J** → myocardial membrane stretch → ion channels via **mechanical-electrical coupling** · **smaller balls ↑ risk** (smaller impact area) · higher energy → structural damage rather than isolated VF

**Heart structure** — **no structural damage at autopsy** ever found · = sudden death **without structural cardiac damage** (OB)\
✗ Wrong options: "often structural damage"

![Commotio cordis: T-wave upstroke risk window](../../MCQ/attachments/q211821-illa-screen-shot-2020-03-23-at-11-57-48-am.jpg)

**Epidemiology** — **< 30 cases / yr**; **average age 15**; **M:F 20:1**; contact sports, hard-ball sports (baseball, lacrosse), karate punch, Little League batter hit by pitch

**Screening** — **not detectable on pre-season physical**\
✗ Wrong options: ; unlike HCM

**Prevention** — **chest protectors / pads not shown to prevent** (baseball, hockey) · soft-core baseballs ≈ standard balls for fatal risk (OB, Janda) · educate coaches + **AED access**

## 3 Presentation

### Red flags & syncope

**Best screening tool** — **history + physical exam** (high-school athletes)

**Symptoms** — **chest pain**, **palpitations**, **syncope** with exercise

**Murmurs** — **diastolic murmur → work-up**; **II/VI systolic murmur**

**Syncope** — **red flag for serious cardiac disease** in a young athlete · annual incidence **7%**, F > M · risk: cardiac disease, dehydration, female

**Vasovagal (neurocardiogenic)** — **50%** of syncope; vasodilation + **bradycardia** (↓ sympathetic, ↑ parasympathetic); prolonged standing, heat

**Syncope RTP** — **medical evaluation before return** to sport

### Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy – clinical features

**Pathology** — abnormal thick / thin myofilament proteins → **asymmetric LV wall + septal hypertrophy** → **LVOT obstruction**, diastolic dysfunction, ↑ SCD · **interventricular septum in 66%**

**Epidemiology** — **1 in 500**; present from birth, diagnosed **20–30 yr**; **M:F 1:1** (females more often symptomatic); risk: hypertension, genetics

**Symptoms** — dyspnoea on exertion, chest pain, dizziness, presyncope / syncope · **> 50% minimal or no symptoms** (OB)

**Murmur** — **II/VI systolic crescendo-decrescendo** · obstruction at rest **25%**, with provocation **70%**

**Murmur ↑** — **↓ preload: Valsalva, standing, diuretics** → ↓ venous return → asymmetric septum obstructs LVOT

**Squatting / handgrip** — ↑ preload / afterload; Orthobullets lists them with the provocative manoeuvres (wording inconsistent) — the tested fact is **murmur ↑ with standing / Valsalva**

**Other signs** — **double carotid pulse**, **double apical pulse**, arrhythmia, family history

![Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy specimen](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3121-60077869-m1720649-hypertrophic-cardiomyopathy-spl.jpg)

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Test | Finding / role |
|---|---|
| **Echocardiogram** | **Nondilated, thickened LV wall** vs age-matched · **80%** accuracy · outflow obstruction, valves · **confirms HCM**; best test with **FHx of SCD** (OB) |
| **Cardiac MRI** | **Gold standard for HCM** — when echo inconclusive |
| **ECG** | **Most sensitive diagnostic test** (Orthobullets) · **LVH + right atrial enlargement** highly suggestive · Q waves inferior + lateral, **left axis deviation**, inverted T |
| Ambulatory ECG (Holter) | **All HCM**, 24–48 h → ventricular arrhythmias, SCD risk |
| Exercise stress test | Preferred over pharmacological · **LVOT gradient → risk stratification** · does not diagnose HCM (OB) |
| CAD | **Coronary angiography + MR angiography** = gold standard |
| Commotio cordis | ECG → **ventricular fibrillation** |
| Long QT | **ECG gold standard** (Orthobullets) · long QT on resting ECG in **\~60%** (TO) |

**SCD work-up** — cardiac MRI, coronary angiography, echo, exercise stress test, **electrophysiology testing**, ECG

**Best HCM test** — **ECG most sensitive** (Orthobullets) · **echo most effective screen / confirms** (OB) · **cardiac MRI gold standard** (Orthobullets) — match the stem's wording

### Pre-participation screening (history, ECG, echocardiogram)

**AHA** — careful **personal + family history + physical exam** = best, most practical screening at any age

**ECG not routine in US** — **cost**; **98.8%** of mass-ECG cost spent on **false positives** (O'Connor) · H&P more cost-effective · ECG **can** detect SCD causes\
✗ Wrong options: "cannot detect" = the EXCEPT answer

**Most cost-effective HCM screen** — **history of chest pain, syncope, FHx of early cardiac death**\
✗ Wrong options: echo · ECG · auscultation (may be normal at rest) · stress test

**Screening echo cost** — **> $500,000** to diagnose one HCM case

**Black male basketball player, no history** — **routine history + physical** · ECG only if personal / family cardiac history\
✗ Wrong options: ECG · stress test · Hb electrophoresis · lung diffusion

**2 relatives died playing sport (AD trait)** — **echocardiogram**\
✗ Wrong options: ECG · renal US · PFT · cervical MRI

```
Pre-participation evaluation
├─ All athletes → history + physical exam
├─ FHx SCD, exertional syncope / chest pain, murmur → ECG ± echocardiogram
└─ Echo inconclusive → cardiac MRI
```

### Diagnostic criteria

| Condition | Criteria |
|---|---|
| **SCD** | Cardiovascular death **< 1 h** from symptom onset |
| **HCM** | **Echo + ECG + genetic testing + family history** |
| **Commotio cordis** | **Witnessed chest blow → collapse + ECG VF** |
| Long QT, symptomatic (TO) | **QTc > 440 ms (male)**, **> 460 ms (female)** |
| Long QT, no symptoms / FHx (TO) | **QTc > 500 ms** · or shorter QT + exercise paradoxical QT prolongation, polymorphic VT, or long QT in first-degree relative |
| Long QT extras (TO) | FHx positive in **40%** · **Holter** for polymorphic VT |

### Long QT syndrome

**Definition** — prolonged ventricular action potential (QT) → arrhythmia · congenital (ion-channel genes) or acquired (electrolytes, K-channel blockers → ↑ intracellular positive charge)

**Prevalence** — **1 in 2,500–10,000** (Orthobullets) · **1 in 2,500**; long QT in **0.42% of elite athletes** (TO)

**Who** — diagnosed **< 20 yr** · rarer in females (Orthobullets) · **SCD mostly in females < 40 yr** (TO)

| Type | Gene / chromosome | Channel | Share | Key fact |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **LQT1** | **KCNQ1, chr 11** | K⁺ efflux (loss of function) | **45% — commonest** | **Adrenergic surge** → swimming, diving into cold water (TO) |
| **LQT2** | **KCNH2, chr 7** | K⁺ | 25–40% | **Most dangerous — most SCDs (females)** (TO) |
| LQT3 | SCN5A, chr 3 | **Na⁺** | 5–10% | — |

**LQT1–3 (TO)** — **\~95%** of cases; **12 types** (LQTS-1 to 12)

**Acquired drugs** — antiarrhythmics (**sotalol, amiodarone**), **macrolides, fluoroquinolones**, antipsychotics (haloperidol, olanzapine), cisapride (TO)

**Presentation (TO)** — palpitations, dizziness, **unheralded syncope** (polymorphic VT) · brief **myoclonic jerks → misdiagnosed as epilepsy** · most asymptomatic (Orthobullets)

**Torsades de pointes** — polymorphic VT → VF → SCD · 50% asymptomatic · **Romano-Ward syndrome** (abnormal repolarization → torsades, VT, SCD)

## 5 Management

### Return-to-play criteria

| Condition | Return to play |
|---|---|
| **HCM** | **Absolute contraindication to vigorous exercise + competitive sport** · mild–moderate activity allowed |
| **Commotio cordis** | **No restrictions** · precautions against future chest blows |
| **Long QT** | By genotype, presentation, pacemaker need; intermittent ECG; **no exercise restriction** (Orthobullets) · **avoid all sports with adrenergic surges** (TO) — read the options |
| **Syncope** | **Medical evaluation first** |
| Cardiac condition found | **Avoid vigorous exercise + sport** (OB) |

### Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy – treatment

**Lifestyle** — asymptomatic, mild LVH, no obstruction → **observation**, repeat echo if symptoms change, **hydration**, **avoid vigorous exercise**

**Medical** — symptomatic → **beta-blocker (metoprolol) first line** (non-vasodilating) · calcium-channel blockers · **symptom relief only**

**Prognosis** — **1–2% annual mortality**

### Coronary artery disease

**Features** — older patients; risk: hypertension, family history; mostly **asymptomatic** (chest pain, palpitations, syncope)

**Treatment** — lifestyle · β-blocker, aspirin, ACE inhibitor, nitroglycerin, CCB · angioplasty + stenting, CABG

### Commotio cordis – AED & defibrillation

**Treatment** — **immediate cardiac defibrillation** · CPR alone inferior\
✗ Wrong options: IV epinephrine · pericardial needle · rescue breathing · fluid bolus

**Collapsed, apnoeic, pulseless pitcher** — **protect airway, start CPR, prepare to cardiovert**\
✗ Wrong options: smelling salts · shade + reverse Trendelenburg · seizure precautions · slow move to spine board

**Prognosis** — mortality **59%** (Orthobullets); **time to defibrillation predicts survival** ✓

```
Athlete collapses after chest blow
├─ Unresponsive, pulseless → CPR (C-A-B) + AED now
│      └─ VF on AED → immediate defibrillation
└─ Survives → return to sport, no restriction
```

### Long QT syndrome – management (TO)

**All patients** — **beta-blocker** unless contraindicated · avoid sports with **adrenergic surges**

**ICD** — **resuscitated cardiac arrest**, **β-blocker-resistant symptoms**, β-blocker contraindicated · ± asymptomatic high-risk

## 6 Complications

**HCM** — SCD, arrhythmias, CHF, stroke

### Anticoagulation-related wound complication

**Toe lesions 9 days after aortic valve replacement, on anticoagulation** — **emboli from cardiac surgery**; no gangrene / infection / sepsis → **observation ± late debridement**\
✗ Wrong options: reverse anticoagulation + debridement / forefoot amputation · urgent arteriogram · 40 °C water bath

![Embolic toe lesions after valve replacement](../../MCQ/attachments/q6779-a-046a.jpg)

## X vs Y

| | **Athletic heart syndrome** | **HCM** |
|---|---|---|
| LVH | Physiological — **may mimic HCM** | Pathological, asymmetric septal |
| **LV cavity** | **Maintained** | **Diminished / nondilated** |
| Ventricle | **Left** (✗ Wrong options: right) | Left |
| SCD risk | **No extra risk** | **Most common cause < 35 yr** |
| Clinical | Asymptomatic, normal exam | Exertional symptoms, FHx SCD, systolic murmur |
| ECG | Sinus bradycardia, 1st-degree AV block, nonspecific ST-T | **Lateral T-wave inversion** = pathology; LVH + RA enlargement |

| Pathophysiology in options | Condition |
|---|---|
| Dynamic outflow obstruction by enlarged LV | **HCM** |
| Plaque blocking coronary arteries | CAD (angina / MI, unlikely in the young) |
| **Direct impact during ventricular repolarization** | **Commotio cordis** |
| Lengthening of QT interval | Long QT → VT, torsades |
| AV-node dysfunction | Heart block |

## How it's asked

**"Most common cause of sudden death in athletes (TO)?"** → **cardiomyopathy**

**"Incorrect statement on SCD (TO)?"** → **commoner in white American males** (✓ black athletes)

**"Most important factor for SCD survival (TO)?"** → **time from arrest to defibrillation**

**"Most common SCD cause, 20–30 yr?"** → **hypertrophic cardiomyopathy**

**"High-school basketball death — autopsy?"** → **HOCM**

**"16-yr-old collapses on court — commonest cause?"** → **HCM**

**"Pre-participation ECG — all true EXCEPT?"** → **ECG cannot detect causes of SCD**

**"FHx of relatives dying in sport — best test?"** → **echocardiogram**

**"Most cost-effective HCM screen?"** → **history of chest pain, syncope, FHx early cardiac death**

**"Black freshman basketball player, no history?"** → **routine history + physical**

**"Seen in a well-conditioned athlete's heart?"** → **decreased resting heart rate**

**"Athletic heart syndrome vs HCM — true?"** → **LVH may mimic HCM**

**"Commotio cordis — treatment?"** → **immediate cardiac defibrillation**

**"AED is for which rhythm?"** → **ventricular fibrillation**

**"Pitcher hit by line drive, apnoeic, pulseless?"** → **airway, CPR, prepare to cardiovert**

**"MMA blow to precordium — true?"** → **time to defibrillation predicts survival**

**"Helmet to chest, AED shows VF — pathophysiology?"** → **direct impact during ventricular repolarization**

**"Highest SCD risk after blunt trauma?"** → **22-yr-old African-American male football player**

**"Embolic toe lesions after valve replacement?"** → **observation, possible late debridement**

**"Long QT > 480 ms — most dangerous type (TO)?"** → **type 2**

**"Commotio cordis — return to sport?"** → **no restriction**

**"HCM diagnosed — sport?"** → **absolute contraindication to vigorous exercise**
