# Exertional Compartment Syndrome

*Knee & Sports · 13 / 61*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology

**Definition** — exercise-induced, **reversible ischaemia** of muscle within a compartment

**Rank** — **2nd commonest exercise-induced leg syndrome** after **medial tibial stress syndrome**

**Who** — **males > females**, **3rd decade**, **runners** · **long-distance runners + military recruits** (TO) · **paediatric CECS → more common in females** in running sports (OB)

**Sites** — **leg** commonest, then thigh + forearm (OB) · forearm 2nd (another OB) · **volar forearm** with repetitive gripping

| Leg compartment involved | Share |
|---|---|
| **Anterior** | **\~70% — most common** (Orthobullets) · **anterior (TO)** |
| Anterior + lateral | 10% (Orthobullets) · **95% of CECS in anterior + lateral** (TO) |
| **Posterior** | **Less predictable surgical outcome**; more often linked to **PAES** (accessory medial gastrocnemius head) |

### Pathophysiology

**Mechanism** — local metabolism can't clear metabolic waste · **↑ muscle volume in a non-compliant closed compartment** (TO)

**Muscle swelling (OB)** — fibres swell up to **20× resting size** → **20% ↑ muscle volume and weight**

**Aetiology (OB)** — **limited osseofascial expansion**, **↑ fascial hernias**, **arteriole dysregulation** · **↓ capillary density** vs asymptomatic

**Fascial hernias** — **40% of ECS vs 5% asymptomatic** (Orthobullets) · **palpable in 40–60%** (TO) · near the **intermuscular septum of anterior + lateral compartments, where the SPN exits**

### Risk factors – anabolic steroids (TO)

| Risk factor (TO) | Why |
|---|---|
| **Anabolic steroids** | ↑ muscle volume |
| **Creatine** | ↑ muscle volume |
| **Eccentric exercise** | **↓ fascial compliance** |
| **Rear-foot landing / over-pronation** | Differential loading of individual muscle groups |

**Traps (TO)** — ✗ Wrong options: inversion + supination of foot on landing · "most recover with activity modification, eccentric exercise + stretching" · posterior + medial calf pain as commonest symptom

## 3 Presentation

**Symptoms** — **aching / burning** or cramping leg pain · **paraesthesias over dorsum of foot** · **reproduced by exercise, relieved by rest** · often **bilateral** (vs acute CS)

**Timing** — starts at a **predictable time / distance / intensity** · **\~10 min into exercise**, resolves **\~30–40 min** after stopping · patient can predict how long pain lasts

**Exam at rest** — **most likely normal**\
✗ Wrong options: Tinel at fibular neck · firm compartments · 1st web numbness · weak dorsiflexion

**Exam findings when present** — **↓ sensation 1st web space**, **↓ active ankle dorsiflexion** (drop foot)

**Exam after provoking exercise (TO)** — tender muscles, **pain on passive stretch**, **firm compartments**, palpable fascial hernia

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Study | Use |
|---|---|
| Radiographs | **Normal** — exclude other pathology |
| MRI | **Not diagnostic** — excludes others: **marrow oedema = tibial stress fracture**, **ring-enhancing collection = abscess** |
| Near-infrared spectroscopy | Muscle deoxygenation; **normal within 25 min** of stopping |
| **Compartment pressures** | **Required / gold standard** |

### Diagnosis – pressure measurement protocol & thresholds

**Protocol** — limb **relaxed + consistent position** · **rest, 1 min post-exercise, 5 min post-exercise** (± **15 min**, some authors)\
✗ Wrong options: 30 min continuous · rest + 1 min only · 60 min continuous

**Stryker catheter (TO)** — commonest device · TO protocol = **pre-exercise + 5 min** after exercising to severe symptoms

**Forearm (OB)** — **simulated grip activity → forearm compartment pressures**\
✗ Wrong options: CTA · MRA · EMG / NCS · angiography

![Compartment pressure measurement sites](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3106-technique-moved.jpg)

### Diagnostic criteria

| Time point | Orthobullets / OB | Pedowitz (OB, TO) | TO (FNB) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Rest (pre-exercise)** | **> 15 mmHg** | **≥ 15** | **> 15** (✗ Wrong options: > 10) |
| **1 min post-exercise** | **> 30 mmHg** | **≥ 30** | **> 30** (✗ Wrong options: ≥ 20) |
| **5 min post-exercise** | **> 20 mmHg** | **≥ 20** | **> 20** |
| 15 min post-exercise | **> 15 mmHg** (some authors) | — | — |

**How many criteria** — listed with "and" (Orthobullets) · **"one or more"** (Pedowitz, TO) · "or" / "and/or" (OB) — **any one value above threshold = consistent** in the MCQs

| Values in the options | Verdict |
|---|---|
| **Rest 10 · 1 min 35 · 5 min 25** | **Normal rest, ↑ 1 + 5 min** (OB) |
| **17 · 32 · 21 · 16** (rest, 1, 5, 15 min) | **Confirms ECS** (OB) |
| **5 min = 25** | **Most consistent** (✗ Wrong options: rest 10 · 1 min 15 / 25 · 5 min 15) (OB) |
| **Anterior 25 at 5 min** | **Positive** (✗ Wrong options: superficial posterior 10 at 15 min) (OB) |
| **Pre 18 · 1 min 58 · 5 min 33** | **All point to CECS** (TO) |

## 5 Management

```
Exertional compartment syndrome
├─ First line, ≥ 3 months → activity modification, NSAIDs, rest / ice / stretching
│      (leg: rarely effective; works only if activity stopped completely)
├─ Forearm, no treatment yet → NSAIDs + PT (stretching, endurance)
└─ Refractory → fasciotomy of involved compartments
```

**Nonop success** — **forearm PT \~80%** vs **leg PT < 50%** (OB) · all CECS get a **nonop trial first**

**Definitive (TO)** — **fasciotomy** in non-responders · mainstay = **subcutaneous fasciotomy** (OB)

### Fasciotomy – recurrence & SPN risk

| Incision | Releases | Landmark |
|---|---|---|
| **Lateral** | **Anterior + lateral** | **12–15 cm above lateral malleolus** · protect **SPN** · may see hernia |
| Medial | Posterior compartments (if pressures ↑) | Mid-tibia, **posterior border** |
| Endoscopic | Same | Smaller incisions, **similar complications** |

**Structure at risk (single anterolateral incision)** — **superficial peroneal nerve** crossing near the anterior / lateral fascial border\
✗ Wrong options: sural (posterolateral) · saphenous (medial) · peroneal artery (deep) · posterior tibial artery (posteromedial)

**Success** — **anterior > 80%** · **deep posterior \~60%** · not a "home run" (multifactorial) · no op vs nonop trials

**Recurrence / failure** — **up to 20%** at mean **2 yr** · **fibrosis / scar** · risk = **isolated (anterior + lateral) release** · **four-compartment fasciotomy ↓ reoperation**

**Not risk factors (OB)** — age < 23 (better success + satisfaction), higher pre-op pressures, bilateral involvement

### Forearm exertional compartment syndrome

**Sports** — **motocross, rock climbing, gymnastics, rowing** (repetitive gripping) · **volar compartment** commonest

**Features** — pain, tightness, weakness · **numbness / paraesthesia rare** (vs leg)

**Gymnast, 5-min pressures 25 + 32, untreated** — **NSAIDs, activity modification, PT**\
✗ Wrong options: endoscopic / open fasciotomy · fasciectomy · observation

### Differential – popliteal artery entrapment

**Posterior leg pain, diminished pulses on resisted plantarflexion, sural paraesthesia** — **PAES** (accessory slip of medial gastrocnemius on MRI)

**Provocation** — **passive dorsiflexion + active resisted plantarflexion**

**Traps** — ✗ Wrong options: intraseptal SPN entrapment (gives CECS-type symptoms) · flexor digitorum accessorius longus → **tarsal tunnel** · popliteal thromboembolism

![Accessory medial gastrocnemius head (M, 3, L)](../../MCQ/attachments/q219557-illa-screenshot-2024-04-02-at-12-11-46-pm.jpg)

## 6 Complications

| Complication | Fact |
|---|---|
| **Nerve injury** | **SPN — most common** |
| DVT | — |
| **Recurrence** | **Up to 20%**, mean 2 yr — **fibrosis**; isolated compartment release |

## X vs Y

| | **CECS** | **Acute CS** |
|---|---|---|
| Side | Often **bilateral** | Unilateral |
| Course | **Resolves with rest** | Progressive |
| First line | Activity modification | **Emergent fasciotomy** |

| | **CECS** | **Tibial stress fracture** | **PAES** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Exam | **Normal at rest** | **Focal bony tenderness** | ↓ pulses on PF / DF |
| Pain | Resolves with rest | Progresses to **pain at rest** | Claudication |
| Test | **Pressures** | **MRI marrow oedema** | Arteriogram |
| Compartment | **Anterior / lateral** | — | **Posterior** |

## How it's asked

**"Resting pressure NOT a criterion (TO)?"** → **> 10 mmHg** (criterion is > 15)

**"Most common compartment (TO)?"** → **anterior**

**"Pre 18, 1 min 58, 5 min 33 (TO)?"** → **all the above point to CECS**

**"NOT a criterion (TO)?"** → **1 minute postexercise pressure greater than or equal to 20 mm Hg** (criterion is ≥ 30)

**"Bilateral leg pain, dorsal foot paraesthesia, normal X-ray — next?"** → **post-exercise compartment pressure measurement**

**"Pressure most consistent with CECS?"** → **5 min post-exercise 25 mmHg**

**"Optimal diagnostic protocol?"** → **rest, immediately post-exercise, 5 min post-exercise**

**"Normal rest, elevated 1 + 5 min?"** → **rest 10, 1 min 35, 5 min 25**

**"Set that confirms ECS?"** → **17 / 32 / 21 / 16 mmHg**

**"Exam in clinic?"** → **normal examination**

**"Positive confirmatory test, normal X-rays?"** → **anterior compartment 25 mmHg at 5 min**

**"Recurrence risk after fasciotomy?"** → **isolated anterior + lateral release**

**"Structure at risk, anterolateral incision?"** → **superficial peroneal nerve**

**"Forearm CECS, untreated, pressures 25 / 32?"** → **NSAIDs, activity modification, PT**

**"Rock climber, volar forearm pain after 20 m?"** → **simulated grip then forearm compartment pressures**

**"Posterior leg pain, ↓ pulses on resisted PF, MRI accessory head?"** → **popliteal artery entrapment syndrome**

**"Post-exercise CS — true (TO)?"** → **anabolic steroids are a risk factor**

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Exertional Heat Illnesses

*Knee & Sports · 14 / 61*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology & risk factors

**Cause** — **heat gain > heat loss** (production > dissipation)

**When / who** — ↑ in warmer months · highest in **American football**, running, cycling, adventure races

**Risk factors** — **poor heat acclimatisation**, **dehydration**, sleep deprivation, **prior heat illness** · conditioning, body composition, **protective equipment**, environment

**Heat stroke** — **3rd leading cause of death in athletes** (after cardiac disorders + cervical trauma)

**Football preseason** — **\~74% of EHI = heat cramps** · peak in **first 14 days** (esp. first 7) · risk ↑ when **WBGT ≥ 82°F (27.8°C)**

**Spectrum** — mild (heat oedema, rash, **cramps**, **syncope**) → moderate (**exhaustion**) → severe (**heat stroke**) · heat cramps **don't** predispose to heat stroke

## 3 Presentation

### Spectrum of exertional heat illness

| Condition | Key features | Treatment |
|---|---|---|
| **Heat syncope** | **Transient LOC**, peripheral vasodilation, ↓ cardiac output, **normal temperature** · rapid recovery supine | **Supine + legs elevated**, fluids |
| **Heat cramps / EAMC** | Painful spasm of large muscles · ↓ hydration, **↓ Na + Cl** · random muscles, spasms **1–3 min** | **Stretching** + oral fluids with **NaCl** |
| **Heat exhaustion** | Can't continue · **core < 39°C (102.2°F)** · **no CNS dysfunction** · **profuse sweat**, pale moist skin, nausea, vomiting, headache, weakness | Cooling, **oral** rehydration, monitor · loosen clothes, ice axilla + groin, cool sprays |
| Hypernatraemic heat exhaustion | **Inadequate water replacement** · thirst, oliguria | Water |
| **Heat stroke** | **Hyperthermia + CNS dysfunction + loss of thermoregulation** ± multi-organ failure | **Immediate cold-water immersion** |
| **Exertional rhabdomyolysis** | Muscle pain, weakness, **↑ CK**, **myoglobinuria** | Cooling, **aggressive IV fluids**, ± dialysis |

| Condition | Key features | Treatment |
|---|---|---|
| **Exercise-associated collapse (EAC)** | **Conscious** collapse **after finishing** · **loss of venous return** on stopping · **normal mental status, temp, vitals** · **most common in marathon tent (59–85%)** | Rule out serious causes → **oral fluids + Trendelenburg** → discharge |
| Exercise-associated postural hypotension | **SBP falls ≥ 20 mmHg** standing vs supine | |
| **Exercise-associated hyponatraemia** | **Na < 135 + mental status change** · dilutional | Severe → **EMS + IV hypertonic saline** |

**Heat exhaustion core temp** — **< 39°C (102.2°F)** (Orthobullets, OB) · **98.6–104°F** (OB211458) — read the options

### Heat stroke — definition, CNS dysfunction & cold-water immersion

**Key discriminator from heat exhaustion** — **CNS dysfunction** ✓\
✗ Wrong options: temp > 38°C · hypotension / dehydration · cramps + dizziness (dizziness isn't CNS dysfunction) · profuse sweating

**CNS signs** — confusion, disorientation, **bizarre behaviour (often first sign)**, staggering / **ataxia**, seizures, delirium, coma · **CNS depression = most important marker**

| Heat stroke core temperature | Source |
|---|---|
| **> 40°C (104°F)** | Orthobullets (printed "> 102.2°F or 40°C"), ACSM / AFP (OB) |
| **> 41°C (105.8°F)** | OB1428, OB8705 |
| **> 105°F** | OB211458 |
| May reach **106–110°F (41.1–43.3°C)** | OB8735 |

**Sweating** — classic teaching: **anhidrosis, hot dry skin** (OB "hallmark") · **exertional** heat stroke **often sweat-soaked, pale** (OB219812) · sweating is an **inconsequential criterion** (OB211458) · dry, flushed skin → **non-exertional / classic** heat stroke

**Temperature** — **rectal = only reliable** · not oral, temporal, axillary, auricular (ACSM — don't use for diagnosis)

**Trigger for immediate cooling** — **collapse + mental status change** · rectal > 40°C + any CNS change → cool\
✗ Wrong options: temporal temp 39.4°C · anhidrosis · tachypnoea · hypotension (both conditions can show collapse, hypotension, tachypnoea)

![Heat stroke (dry hot skin, very high temp) vs heat exhaustion (moist clammy skin)](../../MCQ/attachments/q4840-illa-heatexhaustion-heatstroke.jpg)

## 5 Management

```
Athlete collapses in heat
├─ Mental status change (± rectal > 40°C) → heat stroke → cold-water immersion ON SITE
│      └─ then IV fluids + EMS → cool to 39°C first, transport second
├─ Alert, sweating, temp < 39°C → heat exhaustion → shade, cool, oral fluids
├─ Conscious, normal temp + vitals after finishing → EAC → oral fluids, Trendelenburg
└─ Endurance event, heavy water intake, confused → hyponatraemia → hypertonic saline
```

### Heat stroke treatment

**First step** — **rapid cooling: ice / cold-water immersion** = most effective whole-body method\
✗ Wrong options: IV fluids first · acetaminophen (antipyretic COX block, no effect) · supine + leg elevation (heat syncope) · IV antibiotics · benzodiazepines / Valium (may worsen) · electrolyte or low-osmolarity drinks (cramps / syncope) · warm bath · reverse Trendelenburg

**Cooling target** — **0.2°C / min** to **39°C** · other: cooling blankets, evaporative (fans, sprays), ice to large muscle groups, internal cooling, cool IV fluids

**Cool first, transport second** — whole-body cooling **on site before evacuation** unless other medical emergency (Orthobullets, ACSM, OB219812) · OB8735 key = **transport to ED** with IV fluids + cooling **during transfer** — choose immersion if offered

**Outcome** — **cooling within 1 h of onset → usually full recovery** · on-site immersion safe (half-marathon: 68% treated on site, 59% discharged home)

**Then** — IV resuscitation + treat complications · EMS activation

**RTP after heat stroke** — **no evidence-based guideline** · **rest 7 days** → gradual increase over **next month** → cleared when **heat tolerance returns** (medically supervised)

### Exercise-associated hyponatraemia & collapse

**Ironman, 60°F, heavy bottled-water intake, collapses** — **hyponatraemia**\
✗ Wrong options: hypernatraemia · hypothermia · subendocardial MI · berry aneurysm

**Cause** — **excess free (hypotonic) water** not replacing **sweat Na losses** · usually **> 3 h** ingestion · endurance athletes (triathlon, ultra-marathon, marathon)

**Na thresholds** — **< 135** (Orthobullets prints mg/dl; OB mEq/L) · up to **10% of ultradistance athletes ≤ 135 mEq/L** (mostly asymptomatic) · symptomatic usually **\~125 mEq/L**

**Presentation** — **mental status change**, peripheral paresthesias, **extremity swelling**

**Correction** — **severe acute → rapid correction** (avert **brainstem herniation**) · mild → **oral salty solutions** · **too-rapid correction → central pontine myelinolysis** (OB) — read the options

**Prevention** — fluid balance · **sports drinks (4–8% carbohydrate) for exercise > 1 h** · water best early; electrolyte drinks later prevent dilutional hyponatraemia

**Soldier collapses after 2-mile run, conscious, rectal 37.9°C, BP drop 10 mmHg, recovers** — **exercise-associated collapse**\
✗ Wrong options: heat stroke (no CNS change) · cramps · postural hypotension (needs ≥ 20 mmHg) · hyponatraemia

### Prevention — wet bulb globe temperature

**Wet bulb temperature** — thermometer **wrapped in a wet cloth** → reflects **evaporative (sweat) cooling** capacity in heat + humidity

| Wet bulb temperature | Action |
|---|---|
| **82–86°F** | **1:1 practice : rest** |
| **> 86°F** | **Postpone / cancel** participation (✗ Wrong options: > 75 · > 83 · > 90 · > 93°F) |

**Other prevention** — **heat acclimatisation** (gradual ↑ activity in heat), hydration, activity modification, **immersion tub on site at high-risk events**

## X vs Y

| | **Heat exhaustion** | **Heat stroke** |
|---|---|---|
| CNS | **Intact** | **Dysfunction** (confusion, ataxia, seizure) |
| Core temp | **< 39°C** | **> 40°C** (OB also > 41°C) |
| Skin | Profuse sweat, pale, moist | Classic dry hot · exertional often sweaty |
| Treatment | Cool, oral fluids | **Cold-water immersion on site** |

| | **Heat syncope** | **EAC** |
|---|---|---|
| Timing | During heat exposure | **After finishing** |
| Mechanism | Vasodilation, ↓ cardiac output | **Loss of venous return** on stopping |
| Treatment | Supine, legs up, fluids | Oral fluids, Trendelenburg |

## How it's asked

**"Hyperthermia + CNS dysfunction + loss of thermoregulation?"** → **heat stroke**

**"41°C, dry skin, disoriented, convulsing — most important step?"** → **rapid cooling with ice immersion**

**"Best description of heat stroke?"** → **hyperthermia, CNS dysfunction, loss of thermoregulatory function**

**"Collapsed, confused, not sweating, 80°F + 80% humidity?"** → **transportation to the emergency department**

**"Ataxic, disoriented, 105°F, no sweat?"** → **heat stroke**

**"Lineman confused, 104.5°, no sweat — initial step?"** → **rapid ice water immersion**

**"Finding that mandates immediate cooling in collapsed soldier?"** → **mental status changes**

**"Feature differentiating heat stroke from exhaustion?"** → **presence of CNS dysfunction**

**"Ironman collapse after drinking lots of water?"** → **hyponatraemia**

**"Conscious collapse after 2-mile run, normal temp?"** → **exercise-associated collapse**

**"Wet bulb threshold to postpone sport?"** → **greater than 86 degrees Fahrenheit**

# Femoral Neck Stress Fractures

*Knee & Sports · 15 / 61*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology

**Incidence** — **0.04%** of military personnel (**20 / 10,000 recruits / yr**) · **9%** of lower-limb stress fractures

**Share of sport stress fractures** — **3–5%** (Orthobullets) · **3%** (TO)

**Who** — age 16–56 · **female > male** (TO: higher in female athletes) · most often caucasian

**Sports (TO)** — **marathon / long-distance running**, basketball, gymnastics, ballet · Orthobullets: track + cross-country, military recruits

### Risk factors

| Group | Risk factors |
|---|---|
| Demographic | **Female**, caucasian, older age · **poor baseline fitness** (TO) |
| Activity | **Military**, track / cross-country, high training volume · **sudden ↑ training intensity** (TO) |
| Medical | **Low BMI**, **↓ BMD**, **energy deficiency** (expenditure > intake), tobacco · **acute weight loss** (independent, Armstrong) |
| Anatomical | **FAI**, **coxa vara**, abnormal running gait · **coxa profunda + acetabular retroversion** (pincer) (TO) |
| Triad | **Female athlete triad** (TO + Orthobullets) |

**FAI association** — present in **50%** of FNSF · **cam 42%**, **pincer 78%**

**Female athlete triad** — **amenorrhoea + eating disorder + osteoporosis** · **2–4× risk** · consider in every female athlete with a stress fracture

**Triad mechanism** — HPG-axis dysregulation → **↓ oestrogen** (needed for osteoblast maturation) → **↑ osteoclast relative to osteoblast activity** · OCP ↑ BMD

**Workup** — endocrine / metabolic bone workup · training, diet + menstrual history

### Genes & lab markers

**3× risk if absent** — **calcitonin receptor C allele** · **vitamin D receptor C-A haplotype**

### Anatomy & biomechanics

**Proximal femur** — neck-shaft angle **130 ± 7°** · anteversion **10 ± 7°** · **calcar** = strongest part (dense posteromedial bone)

**Head blood supply** — **MFCA predominant** · LFCA · artery of ligamentum teres (~**10%**) · ↑ displacement → ↑ vascular disruption

**Capsular ligaments** — iliofemoral (Y of Bigelow, AIIS → IT line) resists **ER + extension** · ischiofemoral resists IR · pubofemoral resists abduction + ER in extension

**Load** — **3–5× BW jogging** · **8.4× BW running**

**Mechanism** — repetitive loading > elastic limit → microfractures outpace osteoblastic repair · **fatigued hip muscles lose shock absorption** (Boden)

**Muscle strain** — abductors → highest compressive strain **superolateral** neck · extensors / flexors → tensile vs compressive strain posterior / anterior cortex · rectus femoris (knee extensor) loads neck least

| | **Compression side** | **Tension side** |
|---|---|---|
| Site | **Inferomedial** neck (calcar) | **Superolateral** neck |
| Propagation | Starts inferomedial → superiorly · **45°** → **stable oblique** | Starts superolateral → inferiorly · **90°** to cortex → **unstable transverse** |
| Cause | Compressive load | Bending; **abductors fatigue** |
| Risk | Low (if < 50%) | **High — displaces** |
| Rx | Nonop if < 50% | **Screw fixation** |

![Compression (inferomedial) vs tension (superolateral) stress zones](../../MCQ/attachments/q214803-illa-femoral-neck-illustration.jpg)

## 3 Presentation

**History** — **overuse** running, **recent ↑ training**, high-impact activity

**Symptoms** — **insidious thigh / groin pain** ± radiates to knee · ↑ with weight-bearing, ↓ with rest · pain while running → then walking · completion → crack / pop + can't bear weight

**Exam** — groin tenderness (**62%**), pain at extremes of motion (**79%**), antalgic gait · pain with **SLR, log roll, axial load**, passive IR

**No mechanical symptoms** — no impingement signs (vs FAI / labral tear)

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Study | Findings | Facts |
|---|---|---|
| **Radiographs** | AP pelvis, AP hip, cross-table lateral | **90% initial normal** · 50% normal at 4–6 wk repeat · changes take **6–8 wk** |
| X-ray late signs | **Grey cortex sign**, **linear lucency**, endosteal callus, **sclerotic line** (inferior neck = compression) | Visible line → MRI not needed, ↑ chance of displacement |
| **MRI** | Oedema on **STIR / fat-sat T2** · **low-signal line on T1** · hip effusion | **Study of choice** if X-ray negative · **sens 100%, spec 100%** · defines side + % width |
| MRI effusion | Hip effusion | **8× risk of propagation** |
| **Bone scan** | ↑ uptake (remodelling) | Only if **MRI contraindicated** · sens 93–100%, spec 76–95%, **false + 32%** |

**Gradual groin pain in runner, normal X-rays** — **MRI hip**\
✗ Wrong options: IA steroid (labral tear) · oral steroids · EMG / NCS (radiculopathy) · CT for sports hernia

**Slender runner, inferior neck sclerosis on X-ray** — **urgent MRI** to define extent\
✗ Wrong options: aspiration · immediate fixation before extent known · protected WB with 2-wk films · return to running

**Normal X-ray, superolateral marrow oedema on MRI** — **tension-side fracture** (alpha 45°, LCEA 30° = normal → no FAI surgery)

![Compression-side stress fracture on MRI](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3110-mri-fn-stress-compression-side.jpg)

### Fullerton–Snowdy & Shin classifications

| Fullerton–Snowdy (X-ray + bone scan) | Shin (X-ray + MRI) |
|---|---|
| **Type I — tension** (superolateral) (TO numbering) | Compression: **oedema, no fracture** |
| **Type II — compression** (inferomedial) | Compression: **line < 50%** |
| **Type III — displaced** | Compression: **line > 50%** |
| — | Tension-sided |
| — | Displaced |

**Fullerton–Snowdy (TO)** — **most commonly used** classification · Orthobullets lists the three groups unnumbered

**MRI compression subgroups (TO)** — no line · line **< 50%** · line **≥ 50%** of neck width

### Rohen-Quinquilla & Steele classifications

| Rohen-Quinquilla (MRI) | Steele (X-ray + MRI) |
|---|---|
| **Low grade I** — endosteal oedema **≤ 6 mm** | Compression: oedema, no fracture |
| **Low grade II** — oedema **> 6 mm**, no fracture | Fracture **< 50%, no effusion** |
| **High grade III** — fracture **< 50%** neck width | Fracture **< 50% with effusion** |
| **High grade IV** — fracture **> 50%** neck width | Fracture **> 50% with effusion** |
| — | Tension-sided |
| — | Displaced |

### High- vs low-risk stress fracture sites

| **High risk (TO)** | **Low risk (TO)** |
|---|---|
| **Femoral neck — tension side** | **Femoral shaft** |
| **Patella — tension side** | Medial tibia |
| **Anterior tibial cortex** | Ribs |
| **Medial malleolus** | Ulnar shaft |
| **Talar neck** | **1st–4th metatarsals** |
| **Dorsal navicular cortex** | — |
| **5th MT proximal metaphysis** | — |
| **Great toe sesamoids** | — |

**High risk (TO)** — tension sites → progression to complete fracture, delayed / nonunion, refracture, worse if diagnosis delayed

**Low risk (TO)** — compressive side · favourable natural history · respond to activity modification · rarely recur or go to nonunion

### Differential diagnosis

| Condition | Clue |
|---|---|
| Early OA | Older, ↓ IR, joint-space narrowing + sclerosis |
| Labral tear | Snapping, young, FAI · MRA |
| Chondral defect | Overlaps labral / FAI / dysplasia · MRI |
| Rectus strain | Sudden onset, AIIS tenderness, pain on resisted flexion / extension |
| Osteonecrosis | Steroids, alcohol, sickle cell · crescent sign, head flattening |
| Osteoid osteoma | Night pain, relieved by NSAIDs, nidus |
| Lumbar disc | Positional pain, back pain, paraesthesia, positive SLR |

## 5 Management

```
Femoral neck stress fracture on MRI
├─ Compression, line < 50%, no effusion → NWB / protected WB + activity restriction
├─ Compression, line > 50% or effusion or progression → percutaneous cannulated screws
├─ Tension side → percutaneous screw fixation (urgent)
│      (MRI stress change without line → nonop per Orthobullets)
└─ Displaced → urgent ORIF, anatomic reduction
```

### Operative criteria

| Finding | Treatment |
|---|---|
| **Compression, line < 50%** | **Nonoperative** — NWB / protected WB, activity restriction |
| Tension-side stress change, **no line** (MRI) | Nonoperative (Orthobullets) |
| **Tension-side fracture** | **Cannulated screw fixation** |
| **Compression, line > 50%** | **Cannulated screws** (or strict NWB + weekly X-rays — Shin) |
| **Compression + hip effusion** | **Cannulated screws** (8× progression) |
| **Progression** of compression fracture | Cannulated screws |
| **Displaced** | **ORIF** — cannulated screws or sliding hip screw |

| TO treatment table | Incomplete (< 50% width) | Complete (> 50% width) |
|---|---|---|
| **Compression** | **Conservative** — unless significant pain or **can't straight-leg raise** | Surgical — **cannulated screws or DHS** |
| **Tension** | Surgical — **DHS** | Surgical — **DHS** |
| **Displaced** | — | **Immediate reduction + fixation** — DHS ± derotation screw |
| Atypical tension | Conservative | Surgical — DHS |

**Implant choice conflict** — **3 cannulated screws, inverted triangle** (Orthobullets, OB) · **DHS** for tension-side (TO table) — read the options

### Initial management – protected weight bearing

**Compression < 50% — NWB vs protected WB** — **NWB + activity modification** (TO; ✗ Wrong options: protected WB with crutches · screws · bed rest + calcium / vitamin D) · **crutches with protected WB** (OB; ✗ Wrong options: skeletal traction · calcium · ORIF · pulsed US) — read the options

**Nonop protocol (Orthobullets)** — **NWB 6 wk** → ↑ **25% BW per week** to painless FWB → **unrestricted at 3–4 months** · **75–100%** heal and return

**OB protocol** — protected WB + close observation **6–8 wk** · cessation of running until healed

**Nonop cons** — refracture (case reports), progression

**No role** — corticosteroids, calcium, pulsed ultrasound, skeletal traction in acute FNSF

**Low-risk stress fracture (TO)** — relative rest + activity modification · not healed or symptoms > **4–6 wk** → immobilisation + NWB or surgery

**High-risk incomplete fracture (TO)** — **immobilisation + strict NWB** · worsening / progression → fixation · complete high-risk fracture → strongly consider surgery

### Tension vs compression side – surgical fixation

**Tension side** — high risk of displacement → **percutaneous screws as soon as diagnosed** (Shin & Gillingham) → avoids displacement + harms of bedrest, allows early crutch WB\
✗ Wrong options: cam / pincer arthroscopy · NSAIDs + ↓ mileage · PWB

**Nonop tension side** — rare reports: strict NWB with **3+ wk bed rest** then prolonged crutches

**Compression > 50%** — **percutaneous screw fixation**\
✗ Wrong options: oncology referral · valgus intertrochanteric osteotomy · arthroscopic labral repair · I&D

**Recruit, 10% weight loss, compression line 60%** — **compression-sided; surgical fixation**\
✗ Wrong options: complete fracture · crutches only · tension-sided

**Deciding factor** — **compression vs tension side + % width** · bone density **not** important

**Displaced fatigue fracture** — **urgent surgery**

**Screw technique** — **three 7.0 / 7.3 mm cannulated screws, inverted triangle** (> two): **inferior calcar** (highest load to failure), posterosuperior, anterosuperior · start **at or above lesser trochanter** (avoid stress riser) · parallel, maximal spread · threads in head, not across fracture · washer vs GT penetration · percutaneous or mini-open lateral

**Screw outcomes** — prevent displacement · outcomes ≈ nonop lower-risk FNSF · **more military separation**

**Screw complications** — cutout, **varus collapse**, implant failure, neck shortening, nonunion

### ORIF of displaced FNSF

**Principle** — **anatomic reduction** + **early surgery** ↓ AVN

| Approach | Interval | Note |
|---|---|---|
| **Smith-Petersen (anterior)** | Superficial **TFL (SGN) / sartorius (femoral)** · deep **gluteus medius (SGN) / rectus femoris (femoral)** — internervous | Best view of whole neck · implants via separate lateral incision |
| **Watson-Jones** | **TFL / gluteus medius** (both SGN) — intermuscular | Same incision for reduction + implant · poor subcapital view |

**Reduction aids** — K-wire / Schanz joysticks, pointed clamps, **medial buttress plate** · fix with cannulated screws or DHS · autograft vs nonunion

**ORIF complications** — infection, AVN, nonunion, cutout, malreduction, implant failure · lower return to activity than nondisplaced

## 6 Complications

| Complication | Incidence | Risk factors | Treatment |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Progression / completion** | **14%** | **Hip effusion (8×)**, delayed diagnosis | Cannulated screws |
| **AVN** | Rare if incomplete · **5–42% displaced** | Delay to surgery, initial displacement, **varus malreduction** | Pre-collapse: core decompression / vascularised fibula · collapse: arthroplasty |
| **Nonunion** | Rare if incomplete · **9–44% displaced** | Delay, noncompliance | **Valgus intertrochanteric osteotomy** |
| **Varus malunion** | **5–33%** | — | Revision ORIF + graft, arthroplasty |
| Refracture | Case reports | After nonop | — |

**Fracture size and progression** — size **not** associated with progression (Orthobullets complications) · **> 50%** width → **high risk of progression** (OB, Robertson & Wood) — read the options

### Prognosis

| Outcome | Nonoperative | Operative |
|---|---|---|
| Return to military | **~75%** | **~40–45%** |
| Return to sport | **30–100%** | **48–56%** |

**Negative predictors** — displacement, high athletic demand (vs recreational), delayed treatment · natural history → progression + displacement

## X vs Y

| | **FNSF** | **FAI / labral tear** |
|---|---|---|
| Pain | Insidious, **weight-bearing / axial load** | Flexion, sitting, **FADIR** |
| Mechanical symptoms | **None** | Click, catch, lock |
| Imaging | **MRI oedema / line** | Alpha angle, crossover, MRA tear |
| Rx | Rest / screws | Arthroscopy |

## How it's asked

**"Marathoner, groin pain, normal X-rays, pain on SLR + IR?"** → **MRI of the hip**

**"Slender XC runner, inferior neck sclerosis — next?"** → **urgent MRI**

**"Marathoner, compression fracture > 50% on MRI?"** → **percutaneous screw fixation**

**"Superolateral neck oedema, normal X-ray?"** → **percutaneous screw placement**

**"Marine recruit, compression line 60%?"** → **compression-sided fracture; surgical fixation**

**"Athlete, limp, compression line < 50% (TO)?"** → **non-weight-bearing + activity modification**

**"Same TO stem, repeated paper?"** → **NWB + activity modification**\
✗ Wrong options: protected WB with crutches

**"Recruit, compression fracture ⅓ neck width on MRI?"** → **crutches with protected weight-bearing**

**"Low-risk stress fracture (TO)?"** → **femoral shaft**\
✗ Wrong options: tension patella · anterior tibial cortex · medial malleolus

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Femoroacetabular Impingement (FAI)

*Knee & Sports · 16 / 61*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology & natural history

**Definition** — abnormal femur–acetabulum contact → **labral damage**, chondral injury, progressive hip pain

**Who** — deformity common + often asymptomatic · symptomatic with **extreme ROM** (ballet, gymnastics, martial arts) · **hockey goalies, gymnasts, dancers** → labral tears (repetitive FADIR)

**Onset** — active young adult, slow-onset groin pain, often after minor trauma

**Elite athletes** — prone to **cam** → linked to ↑ training intensity during skeletal development

**Natural history** — early hip dysfunction + arthritis · **FAI = most common cause of OA in nondysplastic hips**\
✗ Wrong options: Perthes · traumatic labral tear · repetitive high-impact sport · dashboard injury

### Anatomy (capsule, labrum, ligamentum teres)

**Acetabulum** — ilium + ischium + pubis · femoral head anteverted **15°** relative to condyles

**Capsular ligaments** — **iliofemoral (Y ligament of Bigelow)**, ischiofemoral, pubofemoral

**Iliofemoral** — **strongest** capsular ligament → restrains **extension + ER** → key restraint vs **microinstability** · **cut during capsulotomy**

**Labrum** — horseshoe fibrocartilage, joined inferiorly by **transverse acetabular ligament** · ↑ acetabular volume + **suction seal**

**Labral seal** — negative intra-articular pressure, retains synovial fluid · no labrum → ↑ cartilage loading + ↓ resistance to distraction

**Ligamentum teres** — cotyloid fossa → femoral head · **negligible** blood supply in adult

**Ligament arrangement** — iliofemoral, pubofemoral + ischiofemoral form a **helical spiral** around the proximal femur

### Cam vs pincer pathomorphology & chondrolabral damage patterns

| | **Cam** | **Pincer** |
|---|---|---|
| Side | **Femoral** | **Acetabular** |
| Typical patient | **Young athletic males** | **Active middle-aged women** |
| Site | Anterolateral neck / anterosuperior head-neck junction | Anterosuperior acetabular rim |
| Morphology | **↓ head-neck ratio**, **aspherical head**, **↓ femoral offset**, **femoral neck retroversion** | **AS rim overcoverage**, **acetabular retroversion**, **protrusio**, **coxa profunda** |
| Mechanism | Aspherical head (radius > acetabulum) jams in → **shear at chondrolabral junction** | Normal neck abuts abnormal rim → **crushes labrum** |
| Labrum | **Chondrolabral separation** — outside-in detachment, anterosuperior | **Intrasubstance tear** / contusion (coup) |
| Cartilage | **Delamination** anterosuperior — **precedes** labral tear | **Contrecoup posteroinferior** acetabulum (head levered back) |
| X-ray | **Pistol grip**, ↑ alpha angle, ↓ offset ratio | **Crossover sign**, posterior wall sign, ↑ LCEA |

**FAI (TO)** — **dynamic conflict**: early abutment of proximal femur on acetabulum · intra-articular impingement = cam or pincer

**Cam (TO)** — aspherical **antero-superior head-neck junction** rotates in → compression + shear at chondrolabral junction → **chondrolabral separation**, labral degeneration, **cartilage detached from subchondral bone**

**Pincer (TO)** — **excessive acetabular wall** → labrum compressed between femoral neck and rim

**Mixed cam + pincer** — **most common** type (up to **80%**)

**Only one lesion needed** — cam or pincer alone can cause symptoms

**SCFE** — epiphysis displaced posteriorly → prominent anterior neck, ↓ IR → **cam / pistol grip** (variable patterns) · externally rotated limb

**After PAO** — acetabulum may become **retroverted** → pincer

**FAI labral tears** — **anterosuperior, 10–2 o'clock**

**Pathologic anatomy of cam** — **morphologic abnormality of femoral head** · impingement in **flexion**\
✗ Wrong options: acetabular retroversion (pincer) · posteroinferior labral tears · femoral anteversion · osteonecrosis

**Cam pathology in a gymnast** — **↓ femoral head-neck offset**\
✗ Wrong options: AS rim overcoverage (pincer) · neck anteversion (cam = retroversion) · ↑ head-neck ratio · spherical head

**Horizontal growth plate sign** — pistol grip + **epiphyseal scar extending lateral to the best-fit circle** of the head → cam

![Bilateral pistol grip deformity (cam)](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3130-bilateralpistolgrip.jpg)

| Lesion pattern seen | Means |
|---|---|
| **AS cartilage delamination → then labral tear at chondrolabral junction** | **Cam** ✓ |
| AS labral tear at labral-capsular junction → then delamination | Wrong order |
| **Posteroinferior acetabular cartilage lesion** | **Pincer contrecoup** |
| Posterosuperior acetabular eburnation · posterosuperior head fraying | OA |
| **Non-spherical head + AS chondral-labral separation** (scope) | **Cam** ✓ |
| **Anterior labral contusion + intrasubstance tear + posteroinferior chondral softening** | **Pincer** ✓ |
| Lateral acetabular cartilage softening + partial labral detachment | Cam |
| Posteriorly oriented acetabulum + AS labral tear | Retroversion (pincer) |
| Non-hemispherical acetabulum + **hypertrophic labrum** | **Dysplasia** |
| Tense iliopsoas + anterior labral tear · tear at **4 o'clock** + bursal inflammation | **Internal (iliopsoas) impingement** |
| AS femoral head cartilage flap + haemarthrosis | AVN |
| Gluteal enthesopathy + bursal scarring | GTPS (lateral pain, abductor weakness) |
| Ligamentum teres tear + patulous anterior capsule | Hip instability |

## 3 Presentation

### Symptoms

**Pain** — activity-related **groin pain ↑ with hip flexion** · worse with **prolonged sitting / driving** · better standing / lying · deep groin pain on squatting

**Mechanical** — clicking / popping (may be absent) · gluteal or trochanteric pain from aberrant gait

**Byrd C sign (TO)** — patient cups hip: **index finger over anterior hip + thumb over posterior trochanteric region** → **intra-articular hip pathology**\
✗ Wrong options: knee · ankle · shoulder

**Intra-articular injection relief** — temporary relief confirms intra-articular source

### Clinical examination – FADIR & loss of internal rotation

**Hallmark** — **↓ flexion + ↓ internal rotation** (flexion < 90°, IR < 5°)\
✗ Wrong options: ↑ ER · ↑ abduction · ↓ ER · ↓ adduction

**Anterior impingement test (FADIR)** — **supine, hip passively flexed 90°, adducted, internally rotated** → deep groin pain\
✗ Wrong options: abduction / ER combinations

**Posterior impingement test** — **extension + ER** → occasionally positive

**FADIR in FAI** — most sensitive exam · positive in cam, pincer and AS chondrolabral tears

![Anterior impingement test (flexion, adduction, internal rotation)](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3130-anterior-impingement.jpg)

| Exam finding | Points to (not FAI) |
|---|---|
| **Stinchfield** — groin pain on resisted hip flexion | Femoral neck fracture, **iliopsoas tendonitis**, abscess |
| **FABER** pain | Lumbar spine, iliopsoas, **sacroiliitis** (not routine for FAI) |
| Trendelenburg gait | Abductor weakness |
| **Half sit-up pain + pubic ramus tenderness** | **Athletic pubalgia** |
| Greater trochanter tenderness | Trochanteric bursitis |
| Pain with axial loading, female athlete triad | **Femoral neck stress fracture** |
| Symphysis tenderness, resisted adduction / abduction | Osteitis pubis |
| Pain on resisted hip flexion / sit-up | Adductor / hip flexor strain |

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| View / study | Technique | Use |
|---|---|---|
| **AP pelvis + true lateral** | Lateral with hip in **15° IR** | Recommended routine views (Orthobullets) |
| **Frog-leg lateral** | — | Alpha angle (Orthobullets method) |
| **45° modified Dunn (TO)** | Supine, **45° flexion, 20° abduction, neutral rotation** | **Best view for cam** (✗ Wrong options: frog-leg · cross-table lateral · 90° sitting view) |
| **Cross-table lateral (TO)** | Hip in **10° IR** | Anterior head-neck offset ratio |
| **90° Dunn / seated (TO)** | Seated, **90° flexion, 20° abduction, neutral rotation** | Beta angle |
| **False profile (faux profil)** | **Standing**, affected hip on cassette, ipsilateral foot parallel, **pelvis rotated 65°** | **Anterior coverage** (DDH + FAI) → ACEA (✗ Wrong options: AP · inlet · outlet · frog lateral) |
| CT ± 3D | — | Adjunct for structure · **femoral version** (anteversion / retroversion) |
| **MRI / MR arthrogram** | In-line with femoral neck | **Best for labrum + cartilage** · fraying, tears, chondral damage, subchondral cysts |
| Bone scan | — | **Not** for FAI (used for FNSF when MRI impossible) |

**MRA triad (Kassarjian)** — **AS labral tear + AS cartilage lesion + ↑ alpha angle**

**Cam on MRI** — axial T2: **loss of sphericity of anterior head-neck** (alpha angle measurable, e.g. 80°) · fluid-sensitive images: **labrum separated from chondral surface** · coronal T1: no neck oedema\
✗ Wrong options: stress fracture

**Alpha angle method (other OB views)** — best-fit circle on head + line along neck axis → angle where head exits the circle · AP, frog-leg lateral or Dunn

**False profile extra** — shows cam deformity + **os acetabuli** (small ossicles beside acetabular roof)

**Cam lesion views (TO)** — frog-leg, cross-table lateral, 45° modified Dunn · anterolateral cam best on lateral + Dunn · quantified by **alpha angle + head-neck offset ratio**

### Radiographic measurements

| Measurement | How | Normal | Abnormal |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Alpha angle** | Line head centre → neck centre + line head centre → point where head **loses sphericity** (anterolateral head-neck junction) | **< 42°** (Orthobullets) · **≈ 42°** (TO) · **< 55°** (one OB) | **> 42°** head-neck offset deformity · **> 50–55° cam** · **> 50° diagnostic** (one OB) |
| **Head-neck offset ratio** | (anterior head line − anterior neck line) ÷ head diameter | **> 0.17** | **< 0.17 cam** · **< 0.15 → 95% PPV for FAI** (TO, Beaulé) |
| **Beta angle (TO)** | Acetabular rim–head centre line vs head centre–loss of sphericity line (Wyss, open MRI) | — | **< 30° → impingement** (cam, pincer, mixed) (Brunner) |
| **LCEA (Wiberg)** | AP pelvis / hip | **Under 40°** (Orthobullets) · **25–45°** (one OB) · **25–39°** (another OB) | **> 35°** overcoverage → poor arthroscopy outcome · **< 25°** inadequate coverage · **< 20°** dysplasia |
| **ACEA (Lequesne)** | False profile | **Over 20°** (Orthobullets) · **25–50°** (OB) | **< 20°** structural instability / dysplasia |
| **Tönnis (roof) angle** | Acetabular index (inclination) | **Above 0°** (Orthobullets) · **0–10°** (OB) | **< 0° ↓ inclination → overcoverage (pincer)** · **> 10° ↑ inclination → dysplasia** |
| Head to ilioischial line | Medial head | **< 10 mm** lateral | **> 10 mm lateral → dysplasia** · medial to line → protrusio |

**Alpha angle (Nötzli, MRI)** — **74°** symptomatic vs **42°** asymptomatic → ↓ head-neck concavity in impingement

**Tönnis grading (TO)** — **OA grade → cannot quantify FAI**\
✗ Wrong options: alpha angle · beta angle · anterior head-neck offset ratio

![Alpha angle on frog-leg lateral](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3130-angle.jpg)

### Pincer/acetabular retroversion – crossover sign

**Normal AP pelvis** — anterior rim runs medially + distally, **diverging** from the more vertical posterior rim · walls meet superolaterally

**Crossover sign** — **anterior rim line crosses posterior rim line** ("figure 8") → crossing occurs more distally → **acetabular retroversion** → **pincer** · the two wall lines **should never cross** normally (two OB explanations word it as the **posterior wall crossing over the anterior**)

**Seen on** — **AP pelvis / AP hip** · hip AP normal head-neck + popping in gait extension → **AP pelvis shows crossover**\
✗ Wrong options: normal film · ↑ femoral offset · SI sclerosis · coxa valga

**Crossover = ?** — **excessive acetabular retroversion**\
✗ Wrong options: low acetabular index · deficient anterior column · labral detachment · pelvic discontinuity

**Crossover in FAI** — **retroversion in pincer**\
✗ Wrong options: retroversion in cam · protrusio (head medial to ilioischial line) · femoral anteversion / retroversion (CT)

**Other pincer signs** — posterior wall sign, protrusio, coxa profunda, ↑ LCEA, negative Tönnis angle

**Pincer AP pelvis** — bilateral **acetabular over-coverage + cephalad retroversion** · bilateral cam + crossover = mixed

![Crossover sign — anterior (red) and posterior (blue) rims](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3130-crossover.jpg)

### FAI types

| Type | Defect | Classic sign |
|---|---|---|
| **Cam** | Femoral head-neck (aspherical) | Pistol grip, alpha > 50–55° |
| **Pincer** | Acetabular overcoverage / retroversion | Crossover sign |
| **Mixed** | Both — **most common (up to 80%)** | Both |

### Differential diagnosis

| Condition | Discriminator |
|---|---|
| **Femoral neck stress fracture** | Female athlete triad / energy deficiency · **axial-load pain**, no impingement · **linear lucency / sclerosis of inferior or posteromedial neck** · T2 oedema |
| **DDH** | **Shallow acetabulum, ↓ anterolateral coverage**, low CEA, hypertrophic labrum |
| **Os acetabuli** | Unfused secondary ossification centre of **anterosuperior acetabulum** |
| Iliopsoas tendonitis / internal impingement | Resisted hip flexion pain · Rx tenotomy |
| Sacroiliitis | Pain with prolonged standing, low back, **FABER** · **SI joint sclerosis** + T2 signal |
| Osteitis pubis | Central pain, symphysis tenderness · **peri-symphyseal sclerosis + osteolysis** · NSAIDs, rest |
| AVN | Insidious, pain with impact / inclines, risk factors, not end-range |
| Displaced femoral neck fracture | ORIF (young) · arthroplasty (old) |
| Other | Ischiofemoral impingement, adductor strain, athletic pubalgia, lumbar radiculopathy, hip instability |

**Compression-sided FNSF (distractor)** — NWB if **< 50%** neck · percutaneous screws if **> 50%**

## 5 Management

```
Symptomatic FAI
├─ Minimal symptoms, no mechanical symptoms → activity modification, PT, NSAIDs
├─ Failed nonop, mechanical, non-arthritic → arthroscopic osteoplasty + labral repair
│      (no viable labrum → labral reconstruction)
├─ Large femoral deformity (residual SCFE / Perthes) → open surgical hip dislocation (Ganz)
├─ Significant acetabular retroversion / dysplasia → periacetabular osteotomy
└─ Arthritic, end-stage → THA (resurfacing vs THA controversial)
```

### Operative criteria

| Procedure | Indication |
|---|---|
| **Nonoperative** | Minimal symptoms, **no mechanical symptoms** — always first |
| **Arthroscopic osteoplasty** | Symptomatic + **mechanical**, **failed nonop**, **non-arthritic** |
| **Labral repair** | Viable tissue, tear at **chondrolabral junction** (vascular acetabular margin) |
| Labral debridement | Intrasubstance tears |
| **Labral reconstruction** | No viable labral tissue |
| **Open surgical dislocation** | Previous gold standard · **preserved cartilage, correctable deformity, reasonable expectations** · **residual SCFE / Perthes** |
| **PAO** | **Acetabular deformity with significant retroversion** · dysplasia |
| THA | Arthritic, end-stage |
| **Hip preservation contraindicated** | **Tönnis grade 2–3** or **< 2 mm joint space** (Harris) |

### Treatment – nonoperative first

**Initial Rx** — **activity modification + NSAIDs** + PT (core / hip strengthening, kinetic chain) — even pro athlete with labral tear on MRI

**PT to ↑ passive IR** — avoid: worsens impingement + chondrolabral damage

**Untreated cam FAI, pro footballer** — **activity modification + NSAIDs**\
✗ Wrong options: open dislocation (more morbidity) · isolated labral repair (leaves cam) · PAO (no acetabular deformity)

**Hockey player, 3 wk pain, no mechanical symptoms** — **activity modification, PT, NSAIDs**\
✗ Wrong options: bone scan · any surgery yet

### Arthroscopic osteoplasty with labral repair

**Failed 12 wk PT + NSAIDs, cam + labral tear** — **arthroscopic labral repair + femoral osteoplasty**\
✗ Wrong options: more PT · surgical dislocation (slower return) · debridement · PAO (no dysplasia)

**Arthroscopic over open** — less soft-tissue trauma, **↓ AVN risk**, **quicker return** · **equivalent results** to open

**Repair vs debridement** — repair / reconstruction → **better PROMs (HHS, SF-12, VAS) + less pain**\
✗ Wrong options: ↑ OA risk · no difference · ↑ pain · ↑ nerve injury

**Always treat bone** — labral debridement or repair **alone** → suboptimal; address cam / pincer as well · femoral osteotomy no role · minimal arthritis → arthroplasty not indicated

**Pincer + AS chondrolabral tear, failed 6 mo PT** — **pincer osteoplasty + labral repair**\
✗ Wrong options: cam osteoplasty (no cam) · labral reconstruction · more PT + isolated repair

**Cam + labral detachment, minimal arthritis** — **femoroacetabular osteoplasty + labral repair**\
✗ Wrong options: debridement · femoral osteotomy · hemi / THA

**Mixed FAI, wrestler with Trendelenburg + IR 10°** — **hip arthroscopy + osteoplasty**\
✗ Wrong options: abduction orthosis · DEXA · bone scan · osteotomy

**Hockey goalie, failed PT + iliopsoas bursa injection** — **FADIR reproduces pain → femoroacetabular osteoplasty**\
✗ Wrong options: PAO (dysplasia) · iliopsoas tenotomy (internal impingement / snapping) · FABER · loose body removal

**Technique** — **capsulotomy** to reach peripheral compartment (cam) · trim head-neck · rim trim → **labral refixation if destabilised** · debridement vs repair vs reconstruction

**Evidence caveat** — long-term halt of arthritis unproven (Gerdeman)

### Open surgical hip dislocation (Ganz)

**Approach** — lateral decubitus, **Kocher-Langenbeck** incision, gluteus maximus split → **digastric trochanteric flip** (fragment mobilised anteriorly) → capsulotomy → **anterior dislocation**, ligamentum teres transected

**Advantage** — wide exposure, **preserves external rotators + MFCA** → **no ↑ AVN**

**Cam + anterior labral tear (classic)** — **anterior transtrochanteric dislocation + labral repair + femoral osteoplasty**\
✗ Wrong options: THA · arthroscopic debridement alone · arthroscopic repair alone · posterior dislocation

**Alternative** — direct anterior approach → limited posterior acetabular view · combined arthroscopic + limited open → early results promising

**Complication** — **trochanteric hip pain**

### Return to sport

**Adolescent, open treatment** — **median 7 months**\
✗ Wrong options: 4–6 wk · 3 mo · 12 mo · 16 mo

**Pro athlete, no OA** — return at same level **> 85–90%** (Orthobullets) · **> 85%** (OB) · ≈ **90%** after arthroscopy · **> 90%** (OB, athletes)

**With concomitant microfracture** — **92%** return (Byrd and Jones) · 5 transient neurapraxias, all resolved

### Poor prognostic factors – arthritis & subchondral cysts

| Poor outcome after FAI surgery | NOT a poor prognostic factor |
|---|---|
| **Preop OA** / Outerbridge III–IV cartilage | Age < 20 / adolescent (mean 17.6 yr — good results) |
| **Tönnis ≥ 1** (Orthobullets) · **subchondral cyst = Tönnis ≥ 2** (OB) | **Professional athlete** · football player |
| **Subchondral cysts / oedema on MRI** | **Alpha angle > 60°** (treated by osteoplasty) · cam prominence |
| **Age > 45** | Posterior labral tear |
| **BMI > 25–30** (one OB) · **BMI > 30** (another OB) — weaker than OA | Labral tear **repaired** |
| **LCEA > 35°** (overhang hard to modify arthroscopically) | LCEA 25° (normal) |
| **Relative femoral retroversion (anteversion < 5°)** | — |
| Labral **debridement** without repair | — |

**OA on AP** — **lateral joint-line sclerosis + acetabular subchondral cyst** · MRI chondromalacia + **subchondral oedema**

**OA + FAI** — inferior results + **earlier conversion to THA** → counsel on limited benefit · symptomatic care → eventual THA

## 6 Complications

| Complication | Fact |
|---|---|
| **Femoral neck fracture** | Femoroplasty · limit resection depth **< 30% of neck diameter** + multiple fluoroscopy views |
| **Heterotopic ossification** | — |
| **Residual deformity** | Commonest cause of revision · prevent with multiple fluoroscopy views |
| **Capsular deficiency** | No capsular closure → instability |
| Neurapraxia | Traction (see hip arthroscopy) |

### Failed arthroscopy – residual FAI & subspine impingement

**Revision rate** — **5–10%** · causes: **under-resection**, over-resection, capsular deficiency, cartilage injury, unaddressed dysplasia

**Commonest cause of revision** — **residual FAI** (persistent structural deformity)\
✗ Wrong options: progressive OA · HO · iatrogenic soft-tissue injury · AVN

**Persistent FADIR pain, cartilage intact** — **alpha angle 60°** = residual cam\
✗ Wrong options: Tönnis angle 8° · LCEA 25° · ACEA 27° · head 7 mm from ilioischial line (all normal)

**Commonest arthroscopy complication** — **iatrogenic chondral injury** · commonest neurological → **traction pudendal** palsy

**Revision options** — repeat arthroscopy, arthroscopy + limited open capsulorrhaphy, PAO, surgical dislocation, arthroplasty

**Revision success** — complete cam resection on **Dunn 45°** (alpha angle, anterior offset, head-neck ratio) → better outcome

**Subspine (AIIS) impingement** — large AIIS spur → **pain on passive hip flexion > 90° in neutral rotation** · overhang below AIIS must be addressed

**Subspine imaging** — **AIIS spur** on AP + frog-leg lateral (abuts femur in frog-leg position) · MRI: spur **dark, tendon-like on T1** + **head-neck oedema** on T2

**Postop alpha 47° + periportal capsulotomy, still painful** — **residual subspinous impingement**\
✗ Wrong options: inadequate cam (47° < 50–55°) · OA beyond Tönnis 2 (needs large cysts + joint-space loss) · plication (none done) · microinstability (no interportal capsulotomy)

**Periportal capsulotomy** — small separate portal openings, not joined → no repair or plication needed

### Postoperative capsular deficiency

**Cause** — iliofemoral ligament incised in capsulotomy → **close after interportal + T-capsulotomy**

**Risk factors** — no capsular closure, unaddressed laxity, trauma, overaggressive rehab, ignoring ROM limits

**Imaging** — Dunn view: no residual cam / pincer · **T2 FS coronal MRI: superior capsular defect + extracapsular fluid**

**Presentation** — pain **different from preop**, **pain / apprehension with hip extension + ER**, positive dial test, **loss of recoil on log roll**, gross instability

**Treatment** — **capsular repair** · **capsular reconstruction** (autograft / allograft) for large irreparable defects

| Postop exam finding | Diagnosis |
|---|---|
| **Pain with extension + ER** | **Capsular deficiency** ✓ |
| Pain with FADIR | Residual impingement |
| Pain on resisted hip flexion at 90° | Iliopsoas tendonitis |
| Pain on passive flexion > 90° in neutral rotation | Subspine impingement |
| Loss of motion | Capsular adhesions |

### Microinstability – iliofemoral ligament & hyperlaxity

**Strongest restraint** — **iliofemoral ligament**\
✗ Wrong options: ischiofemoral · ligamentum teres · pubofemoral · inguinal

**Causes** — bony / DDH, connective tissue disorder (Beighton), post-traumatic, microtrauma, iatrogenic, idiopathic

**Pathology** — abnormal head translation → microtrauma, capsular stretch, ↑ shear

| Test | Sensitivity | Specificity |
|---|---|---|
| **Abduction-hyperextension-ER** | **80.6%** | 89.4% |
| Prone instability | 33.9% | **97.9%** |
| Hyperextension-ER | 71% | 85.1% |

**Imaging** — **cliff sign** (loss of head sphericity; in **> 89%** of microinstability) · anterior recess widening, thin anterior capsule, capsular defect, labral tear (MRA)

**Gymnast, Beighton 5, cliff sign after labral repair** — **PT first** (core + hip stabilisers) → **> 70% resolve**\
✗ Wrong options: steroid injection · PAO · capsular plication / repair (only after failed PT)

![Cliff sign of hip microinstability](../../MCQ/attachments/q219852-illa-cliff-sign.jpg)

### Ischiofemoral impingement

**Mechanism** — **lesser trochanter abuts lateral ischium** · **↓ native femoral offset predisposes** ✓

**Presentation** — **deep posterior hip pain**, worse after long runs · no relief from hip-joint injection

**IFI test** — **passive hip extension in contralateral decubitus** · pain on extension + ER\
✗ Wrong options: FADIR (that is FAI)

**MRI** — **↓ ischiofemoral space** · **quadratus femoris** signal (nerve to quadratus femoris, not femoral nerve)\
✗ Wrong options: iliofemoral

**Treatment** — stretching, steroid injection into ischiofemoral space → recalcitrant: **partial lesser trochanter resection** · MRI: ↑ signal around quadratus femoris tendon

## X vs Y

| Postop / hip pain | Provocation |
|---|---|
| **FAI / residual cam** | **FADIR** |
| **Ischiofemoral impingement** | **Extension (+ ER), contralateral decubitus** |
| Capsular deficiency / microinstability | Extension + ER, AB-HEER |
| Subspine impingement | Passive flexion > 90°, neutral rotation |
| Iliopsoas | Resisted hip flexion (Stinchfield) |

| | **FAI** | **Dysplasia** |
|---|---|---|
| Coverage | Normal / ↑ (LCEA > 35°, Tönnis angle negative) | ↓ (LCEA < 20°, Tönnis angle > 10°) |
| Labrum | Tear, crushed / separated | **Hypertrophic** |
| Surgery | Osteoplasty ± labral repair | **PAO** ± labral repair |

## How it's asked

**"Commonest cause of OA without DDH?"** → **FAI**
