# Plicae

*Knee & Sports · 43 / 61*

## 1 Basics

### Embryology & pathology

**Origin (TO)** — embryonic knee divided by **synovial folds** → absorbed by **3–4 months** → single cavity · **partial resorption** → persistent septations = **synovial plicae** (visible at arthroscopy)

**Mediopatellar plica (TO)** — not a septum remnant (Ogata & Uhthoff) · linked to **lateralised patella** = remnant of mesenchyme that was larger medially

**Prevalence** — medial patellar plica in **5–55%** of people; **few symptomatic** (OB) · mere presence ≠ symptoms (TO)

**Plica syndrome** — painful impairment from **thickened, inflamed synovial fold (usually medial)** · **50%** give history of **blunt anterior knee trauma** · repetitive sport, direct trauma, other knee pathology

**Inflamed plica (TO)** — **fibrotic, thickened, inelastic** → snaps over **femoral condyle** or under **patella** → pain, **synovitis of condylar margins**, **chondromalacia patellae** / cartilage wear

### Plica anatomy, types & symptomatic plica

| Plica | Course | Key fact |
|---|---|---|
| **Infrapatellar = ligamentum mucosum** | **Intercondylar notch → infrapatellar (Hoffa) fat pad**; narrow femoral origin (anterior notch), widens, **anterior to ACL** | **Most common / most frequently seen at arthroscopy**; **rarely symptomatic** |
| **Suprapatellar** | **Anterior femoral metaphysis → medial joint wall** (TO) · → posterior quadriceps tendon above patella (TO) · medial → lateral wall of suprapatellar space (Orthobullets); between joint + suprapatellar bursa | **Most persistent**; impinges **superomedial trochlea in flexion**; usually asymptomatic |
| **Medial (mediopatellar, Lino band, shelf)** | **Medial joint wall → synovium over infrapatellar fat pad** (from suprapatellar origin) | **Most common cause of plica syndrome**; **most significant / most symptomatic**; abrades **MFC** |
| **Lateral** | Lateral wall, **lateral patellar facet → infrapatellar region**; thin, 1–2 cm lateral to patella | **Least common**; existence controversial; asymptomatic |

**Frequency order (TO)** — **infrapatellar > suprapatellar > mediopatellar** · Orthobullets: ligamentum mucosum most common

**Conflict** — **infrapatellar = most common plica** (TO, Orthobullets) vs **medial = most common type + most symptomatic** (TO FNB 2023) — read the options; **most symptomatic = medial** in all sources

**Multiple plicae** — not uncommon in one patient

![Synovial plicae of the knee — medial (shelf) plica is the symptomatic one](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3027-plica.jpg)

## 3 Presentation

**Symptoms** — **anterior / anteromedial knee pain**, **snapping, popping, clicking** with repetitive flexion–extension, **buckling**, **pain on sitting**, crepitus, swelling · worse with **poor quadriceps tone / muscle imbalance**

**Cyclist (TO)** — tenderness on **undersurface of medial patellar facet**; **deep flexion + full extension painful**

**Exam** — **medial parapatellar tenderness** · **painful palpable cord** rolled + popped under the finger · band painful with flexion–extension **with tibia internally rotated** · **McMurray negative**

**Provocative test** — knee held in **full extension**, examiner flexes against resistance, then **pushes patella medially** palpating medial border → **pain ± click = positive**

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Modality | Finding |
|---|---|
| **Clinical** | **Diagnosis is primarily clinical** |
| **MRI** | Detects plica but **low sensitivity**, non-specific · order only **after failed conservative** treatment |
| MRI symptomatic plica (TO) | **Thick ± synovitis** (normal plica thin) |
| MRI Sakakibara C (TO) | Axial FS PD **hypointense band covering anterior MFC** |
| MRI suprapatellar (TO) | **Sagittal**: band-like low-signal structure **posterior to patella** |
| **Arthroscopy** | **Gold standard for diagnosis + treatment**; arrow on PF view from inferolateral portal = **plica** (✗ Wrong options: loose body · meniscus · retinaculum · OCD) |
| Arthroscopy, suprapatellar (TO) | **Almost never diagnosed** — complete plica suggested only by a **short suprapatellar pouch** (or needle enters bursa) |

![MRI — thickened medial plica (Sakakibara type C) covering the anterior MFC](../../MCQ/attachments/TO17119465-1.png)

### Sakakibara classification of medial plica

| Type (TO) | Arthroscopic appearance | Symptoms |
|---|---|---|
| **A** | **Cord-like elevation** in synovial wall | Unlikely |
| **B** | **Shelf-like**, does **not** cover anterior MFC | Unlikely |
| **C** | **Large shelf covering anterior surface of MFC** | **Trapped between MFC + patella** → thick, hard → **cartilage damage** |
| **D** | **Fenestrated** (central defect) | Trapped → cartilage damage |

**Types C + D (TO)** — impinge **medial patellar facet cartilage in flexion** + **MFC cartilage in extension** → **arthroscopic resection = treatment of choice**

**Untreated medial plica syndrome (TO)** — can cause **Outerbridge grade IV** chondral lesions (subchondral bone exposed)

![Plica locations and Sakakibara types A–D](../../MCQ/attachments/TO19356188-3.png)

### Misfiled TO items (Brodie abscess, carpal tunnel tests)

| Brodie abscess (TO) | Fact |
|---|---|
| Site / age | **Proximal tibia**, children + young adults |
| Course | **Indolent**; mild–moderate local tenderness; intermittent pain **worse at night**, > 2 wk; low-grade fever |
| Labs | WBC, ESR, CRP **normal or mildly ↑** |
| X-ray | **Cavity at metaphyseal–epiphyseal junction + rim of reactive new bone** |
| Diagnosis / organism | **Bone biopsy** confirms · **S. aureus** commonest |
| **Treatment** | **Curettage + bone grafting under an anti-staphylococcal antibiotic cover** (✗ Wrong options: curettage alone · observation · needle aspiration) |

| Carpal tunnel syndrome (TO) | Fact |
|---|---|
| **Not a CTS test** | **Murphy** (✗ Wrong options: Phalen · Durkan · Semmes-Weinstein) |
| Pathology | Carpal tunnel pressure **> 30 mmHg** |
| Commonest causes | **Idiopathic > hypothyroidism > pregnancy** |
| Risk factors | Female, obesity, pregnancy, hypothyroid, RA, trauma (distal radius fracture / malunion, carpal dislocation), repetitive motion, acromegaly, age, menopause, CRF, space-occupying lesion, OCP, CHF, diabetes, smoking, alcohol, mucopolysaccharidosis (children) |
| Symptoms | Pain, numbness, paraesthesia **first 3 digits + lateral half of 4th**; thenar atrophy |
| **Riche-Cannieu anastomosis** | Deep ulnar → recurrent motor median branch → **thenar strength preserved** |
| Sensory tests | 2PD (fails at **5 mm**), **Semmes-Weinstein = most sensitive (early)**, vibrometry, texture, **hand diagram = most specific** |
| **Phalen** | Dorsal hands together **30–60 s** |
| **Durkan** | Thumb pressure over carpal tunnel just distal to wrist crease → symptoms **within 30 s** |
| Tinel | Tapping over nerve → tingling |
| **CTS-6** | Score **> 12 → 80%** probability |
| Imaging | X-ray limited · MRI if mass · **US CSA > 10 mm²** at pisiform / proximal TCL |
| **NCV** | **Distal sensory latency > 3.5 ms**, **motor latency > 4.5 ms**, CV **< 52 m/s** (less specific than latencies) |
| EMG | Traditional gold standard (AAOS: limited recommendation) · ↑ insertional activity, sharp waves, fibrillations, fasciculations, complex repetitive discharges |

## 5 Management

```
Plica syndrome
├─ First line → ACTIVITY MODIFICATION + PHYSICAL THERAPY (hamstring stretching), NSAIDs
│      └─ persistent → INTRAPLICAL CORTICOSTEROID INJECTION (TO)
├─ Failed conservative → MRI (limited sensitivity)
└─ Refractory / Sakakibara C–D → ARTHROSCOPIC PLICA EXCISION
```

### Diagnosis & initial management

**Most cases** — **nonop**: activity restriction, NSAIDs, PT · avoid **repetitive jumping, squatting, lunging** · usually successful

**PT content** — **moist heat**, **hamstring stretching** · **resistive strengthening avoided early** (Orthobullets) · **quadriceps strengthening** part of rehab (OB) — read the options

**Weightlifter, palpable painful anteromedial fold, McMurray −** — **activity modification & physical therapy**\
✗ Wrong options: diagnostic arthroscopy · MRI · PRP · patellar stabilising brace

**Arthroscopic resection** — **rare**: plica band syndrome **not responding to nonop** · **thickened Sakakibara C plica (TO)** → **arthroscopic excision**

## X vs Y

| | **Infrapatellar (lig. mucosum)** | **Medial (shelf)** | **Suprapatellar** | **Lateral** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Frequency | **Most common** | Common (most common type — one TO) | Most persistent | **Least common** |
| Symptoms | **Rare** | **Most symptomatic** | Usually none | None |
| Course | **Notch → fat pad** | **Medial wall → fat pad** | **Femoral metaphysis → medial wall** | Lateral facet → infrapatellar |

| | **Sakakibara A / B** | **Sakakibara C / D** |
|---|---|---|
| Covers anterior MFC | No | **C: yes**; D fenestrated |
| Cartilage damage | Unlikely | **Yes** |
| Treatment | Nonop | **Arthroscopic resection** |

## How it's asked

**"Arrow on arthroscopic PF view (inferolateral portal)?"** → **plica**

**"Anteromedial fold, popping on squats — next step?"** → **activity modification & physical therapy**

**"Infrapatellar plica (TO)?"** → **runs from intercondylar notch to fat pad**\
✗ Wrong options: medial wall → fat pad = medial · metaphysis → medial wall = suprapatellar

**"Most common symptomatic plica (TO)?"** → **medial**

**"Most significant plica causing knee pain (TO)?"** → **medial**

**"NOT true about synovial plica (TO)?"** → **"Type C Sakakibara medial plica is not associated with articular cartilage damage"** (it is)

**"Cyclist, hypointense band covering anterior MFC (TO)?"** → **Sakakibara type C synovial plica — arthroscopic excision**\
✗ Wrong options: type B ablation · MPFL cyst

**"Brodie abscess — treatment (TO)?"** → **curettage + bone grafting under an anti-staphylococcal antibiotic cover**

**"Not a test for carpal tunnel syndrome (TO)?"** → **Murphy**

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Popliteal Artery Entrapment Syndrome

*Knee & Sports · 44 / 61*

## 1 Basics

**Definition** — **constriction of the popliteal artery** by adjacent muscles, tendons or fibrous tissue · usually an **underlying anatomic abnormality**

**Demographics** — **male 4:1** · age **25–40 years**

**Pathophysiology** — ↓ flow distal to the popliteal fossa → symptoms **mimic compartment syndrome**

**Classic pathoanatomy (OB)** — compression by the **medial head of gastrocnemius** · **accessory slip of medial gastrocnemius head** on MRI

### Modified Whelan classification

| Type | Anatomy | Image |
|---|---|---|
| **I** | **Normal medial gastrocnemius head**, popliteal artery takes an **aberrant course** | ![Type I](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3107-popliteal-artery.jpg) |
| **II** | **Medial gastrocnemius head located laterally**, artery not deviated | |
| **III** | **Abnormal muscle bundle from medial gastrocnemius head** surrounds + constricts artery | |
| **IV** | Artery entrapped by **popliteus** | |
| **V** | **Popliteal vein entrapped with the artery** in any of the above | |

## 3 Presentation

**Symptoms** — **intermittent claudication** (OB) · **limb swelling often the primary complaint** · foot numbness, **toe tingling after vigorous exercise** · **calf cramping even after light exercise** as it worsens · may be asymptomatic

**Exam** — **diminished pulses with active plantarflexion or passive dorsiflexion** · cool posterior calf, paraesthesias · **easily confused with posterior compartment syndrome**

**Sensory pattern (OB)** — dysaesthesia in the **sural** distribution (posterolateral leg / foot)

**Runner with pain, coolness, tingling (OB)** — **pain with passive dorsiflexion + plantarflexion**, **compartment pressures normal** → **PAES**\
✗ Wrong options: exertional compartment syndrome · tibial stress fracture · lumbar radiculopathy · piriformis

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Study | Finding / use |
|---|---|
| Radiographs | **Usually normal** |
| **Doppler US** | Less invasive; detects **pulse change during active plantarflexion / passive dorsiflexion** |
| **Arteriogram** | **Confirms diagnosis**, **\~100% sensitivity** → **stenosis, obliteration, post-stenotic dilation** |
| MRA / CTA | Usefulness still under study · MRA can show **segmental popliteal occlusion** (OB) |

![Arteriogram — popliteal artery entrapment](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3107-popliteal-artery-entrapment-syndrome-image.jpg)

### Differential of exertional leg pain (OB)

**Exertional compartment syndrome** — needs **↑ compartment pressures**: rest > 15 · 1 min post > 30 · 5 min post > 20 · 15 min post > 15 mmHg (Orthobullets) → normal pressures exclude it

**Lumbar radiculopathy / piriformis** — neuropathy does not explain a **cool, crampy leg**

**Tibial stress fracture** — **not relieved by running**, no claudication signs

**Other differentials** — CECS, medial tibial stress syndrome, tibial / fibular stress fracture, fascial defects, nerve entrapment, **vascular claudication (atherosclerotic or PAES)**, lumbar disc herniation

## 5 Management

```
Popliteal artery entrapment
├─ Mild symptoms, rigorous exercise only → activity modification + observation
└─ Refractory / artery or vein damage → vascular surgery
       ├─ release the entrapping structure (depends on type)
       └─ damaged artery → saphenous vein bypass vs endarterectomy
```

**Natural history** — **most patients eventually need surgery**

**Approach** — **posterior → better exposure** · **medial → used when bypass needed**

## 6 Complications

| Complication | Rate |
|---|---|
| **Surgical failure (entrapment returns)** | **30%** → can need **amputation** |
| Wound infection | **< 5%** |
| **DVT** | **\~10%** |
| **Asymptomatic after surgery** | **70–100%** |

## X vs Y

| | **PAES** | **Exertional compartment syndrome** |
|---|---|---|
| Compartment | **Posterior** leg | **Anterior** (± lateral) commonest |
| Mechanism | Artery compressed (medial gastrocnemius) | ↑ pressure in a closed compartment |
| Key sign | **↓ pulses with active plantarflexion / passive dorsiflexion**, cool leg | Normal exam at rest |
| Nerve symptoms | Sural distribution | **Dorsum of foot / 1st web** |
| Confirm | **Arteriogram** (Doppler US screen) | **Compartment pressures** |

## How it's asked

**"Claudication, coolness, tingling; pain on passive DF + PF; normal pressures?"** → **popliteal artery entrapment syndrome**

**"Confirmatory test?"** → **arteriogram (stenosis, obliteration, post-stenotic dilation)**

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Posterolateral Corner Injury

*Knee & Sports · 45 / 61*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology

**Incidence** — **7–16%** of knee ligament injuries (Orthobullets) · **10–15%** (OB) — read the options

**Isolated** — **only 28%** isolated (Orthobullets) · **< 5%** isolated (OB) — read the options · usually combined with cruciate (**PCL > ACL**)

**Missed PLC** — **common cause of ACL (and PCL) reconstruction failure** · often missed (subtle exam + imaging)

### Anatomy – LCL, popliteus, biceps femoris

**3 major static stabilisers** — **LCL (FCL)**, **popliteus tendon**, **popliteofibular ligament (PFL)**

**Other static** — lateral capsule thickening (meniscofemoral + meniscotibial ligaments) · arcuate ligament (variable) · fabellofibular ligament (variable)

**Dynamic** — **biceps femoris**, popliteus muscle, **ITB**, **lateral head of gastrocnemius**

**Lateral quadruple complex (TO)** — **ITB + LCL + popliteus tendon + biceps femoris**\
✗ Wrong options: fabellofibular ligament · popliteofibular ligament

**Arcuate complex** — LCL + arcuate ligament + popliteus tendon

**Full PLC (OB, Covey)** — ITB, LCL, popliteus complex (dynamic MTU; static PFL, popliteotibial + popliteomeniscal fascicles), middle third lateral capsular ligament, fabellofibular, arcuate, posterior horn LM, lateral coronary ligament, posterolateral capsule

| Structure | Femur | Fibula / tibia | Key fact |
|---|---|---|---|
| **LCL** | LFC, **proximal + posterior to popliteus** | **Most anterior** on fibular head · **anterolateral to PFL** | Runs **superficial to popliteus** · primary varus restraint |
| **Popliteus tendon** | **Distal + anterior to LCL** (✗ Wrong options: proximal) · through LM hiatus | Muscle → posteromedial tibia | **Dynamic internal rotator of tibia** |
| **PFL** | Popliteus musculotendinous junction | **Medial to LCL** on fibular head (✗ Wrong options: lateral) | Anterior + posterior divisions |
| **Biceps femoris** | — | **Posterior to LCL** on fibular head | Long head: direct arm posterolateral, anterior arm lateral to LCL · short head: superior fibular head, medial + posterior to LCL |

**Fibular insertion order (anterior → posterior)** — **LCL → PFL → biceps femoris**

**Biceps–capsulo-osseous ITB confluence** — injury correlates with **positive Lachman** (Terry & LaPrade)

**PMC distractors** — **posterior oblique ligament** (from adductor tubercle region, posterior + proximal to MCL) + deep MCL (meniscofemoral + meniscotibial) → **medial**; no positive dial

![Lateral knee: popliteus, PFL, FCL (LCL), fibula](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3012-plc-anatomy.jpg)

| Lateral layer | Structures |
|---|---|
| **I** | Iliotibial tract, biceps |
| — | **Common peroneal nerve between layers I and II** |
| **II** | Patellar retinaculum, patellofemoral ligament |
| **III superficial** | **LCL**, fabellofibular ligament |
| — | Lateral geniculate artery between superficial + deep III |
| **III deep** | Arcuate, coronary ligament, **popliteus tendon**, **PFL**, capsule |

### Function

**Popliteus + PCL** — synergistic → control **external tibial rotation, varus, posterior tibial translation**

**Popliteus primary action** — **internal tibial rotation** · popliteus complex reinforces posterior third of lateral capsule; restrains ER, posterior translation, varus\
✗ Wrong options: ER · posterior / anterior translation

**Popliteus + PFL** — maximal **in flexion** → resist ER

**LCL** — primary varus restraint at **5° (55%)** and **25° (69%)** (PLC note) · **5° and 30°** (LCL note) — read the options

## 2 Mechanism

**Mechanisms** — **blow to anteromedial knee** · **varus blow to flexed knee** · contact / non-contact **hyperextension** · **ER twisting** · knee dislocation · varus force + ER component (OB)

**Associated** — **common peroneal nerve 15–29%** (Orthobullets etiology) · **\~25%** (Orthobullets exam) · vascular injury

## 3 Presentation

**Symptoms** — instability **in full extension** · difficulty reciprocating stairs, pivoting, cutting · lateral + posterior pain

**Gait** — standing **varus**, **varus thrust** or hyperextension thrust

**Peroneal nerve** — ↓ sensation dorsum of foot + weak ankle dorsiflexion

**PLRI** — **lateral tibial plateau moves posterior to femoral condyle** on rotational stress → **posterolateral rotatory instability** · instability named by tibial position relative to femur\
✗ Wrong options: AMRI · ALRI · PMRI

### Diagnosis – dial test, reverse pivot shift & PLRI

| Test | Technique | Positive means |
|---|---|---|
| **Varus stress 30°** | — | **LCL** |
| **Varus stress 0°** | — | **LCL + cruciate (ACL or PCL)** |
| **Dial test** | Prone or supine; 30° + 90° | **> 10° ER at 30° only → isolated PLC** · **30° + 90° → PLC + PCL** |
| **ER recurvatum** | Supine, suspend legs by great toes | ↑ recurvatum + varus + **anterior** tibial translation → **ACL + PLC** |
| **Posterolateral drawer** | Hip 45°, knee 80°, foot ER 15° · posterior drawer + ER | ↑ posterolateral translation (lateral tibia ER on LFC) |
| **Reverse pivot shift** | Tibia ER + valgus | Clunk of reduction → **PLC** (PLRI) |
| Posterior drawer | 90° | PCL (negative in isolated PLC) |

**Dial threshold** — **> 10°** (Orthobullets) · **10–15°** (OB) · **15°** (TO supine description) — read the options

**Supine dial (TO)** — leg over bed edge, stabilise distal femur, hold heel, max ER at 30° → **tibial tuberosity position** vs other side → repeat at 90°

**Prone dial (TO)** — both feet rotated at 30° then 90° · angle between **sagittal plane of sole and body** (thigh–foot angle)

**Prone preferred (Jung)** — dial more clearly positive when **posterior sag is reduced**

**Other PLC tests (TO)** — ER recurvatum · posterolateral drawer · reverse pivot shift

**Dial ↑ at 30° + 90° — least likely torn?** — **posterior oblique ligament** (medial)\
✗ Wrong options: FCL · popliteus · PCL · arcuate

**+ Reverse pivot, negative posterior drawer** — PLC without PCL → expect **10° ↑ ER at 30°**\
✗ Wrong options: at 90° · valgus opening · anterior drawer

**LaPrade (OB)** — reverse pivot shift ↔ injury to **LCL, popliteal components, mid-third lateral capsular ligament** · LCL injury ↔ ↑ ER at 30° · **Gollehon: sectioning LCL → 20° ↑ ER at 30°**

| Pivot tests (TO) | Movement | Instability |
|---|---|---|
| **Pivot shift (Macintosh)** | **Extension → flexion**, tibia **IR** + valgus · tibia relocates posteriorly | **Anterolateral** (ACL + lateral capsule / ALL) |
| **Jerk test (Hughston & Losse)** | **Flexion → extension** (reverse of pivot shift) | **Anterolateral** |
| **Reverse pivot shift** | Tibia **ER** + valgus; clunk | **Posterolateral** (PLC, ± PCL) |

**Reverse pivot direction** — start at **90°**, ER + valgus, **extend** → tibia reduces from posterior subluxation at **\~20°** (ITB flexor → extensor) (Orthobullets) · knee moved **extension → flexion** to feel clunk (TO) — read the options

![Reverse pivot shift: valgus and external rotation](../../MCQ/attachments/TO17119513-2.jpg)

**ER recurvatum (TO)** — historically relative hyperextension + ER of tibial tubercle + varus · **LaPrade: sensitivity 7.5%** in 134 PLC patients · X-ray showed **anterior** tibial translation · **all positives = ACL + PLC** · doesn't identify which PL structures (multiple inferred)\
✗ Wrong options: posterior

**ER recurvatum yield** — identifies **\~10%** of PLC injuries (Orthobullets) · **7.5% sensitivity** (TO) — read the options

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Study | Finding / use |
|---|---|
| **AP X-ray** | **Arcuate sign** = fibular head avulsion (LCL / biceps) · or femoral condyle avulsion |
| **Varus stress X-ray** (bilateral, **20° flexion**) | Side-to-side **2.7–4 mm → isolated LCL** · **> 4 mm → PLC** |
| **Long-leg standing** | **Chronic** PLC → **triple varus**, mechanical axis, need for **proximal tibial osteotomy** |
| **MRI** | LCL, popliteus, biceps · acute **bone bruise MFC + medial tibial plateau** · **coronal oblique thin slices through fibular head** best |
| MRI (sagittal T2) | Posterolateral tibia oedema + **oedema posterior to popliteus tendon** |
| MRI (coronal) | ITB, LCL, popliteus torn · coronary ligament torn from tibia → displaced LM |

**Arcuate sign (OB)** — **highest association with PLC**\
✗ Wrong options: Segond (ALL + ACL) · Schatzker II lateral plateau (varus PLC injury → **medial** plateau injury commoner) · PCL avulsion · tibial spine avulsion (ACL)

**Fragment size (Lee)** — arcuate ligament / PFL → **small styloid** avulsion · LCL / biceps → **larger** fragment · **Huang**: fibular head avulsion predicts **PLC + PCL** injury (13 pts; LCL > popliteus)

![Arcuate fracture (fibular head avulsion)](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3012-arcuate-fracture.jpg)

| Varus alignment (chronic) | Cause |
|---|---|
| **Primary** | Tibiofemoral malalignment |
| **Secondary** | LCL deficiency → ↑ lateral opening |
| **Triple** | Remaining PLC deficient → varus recurvatum |

![Bilateral varus stress radiographs](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3012-plc-stress-radiograph.jpg)

### Modified Hughston classification

| Grade | Examination | Findings |
|---|---|---|
| **I** | **0–5 mm** lateral opening on varus stress | Sprain, no tensile failure |
| **II** | **6–10 mm** opening · **6–10°** rotational instability on dial | Partial, moderate disruption |
| **III** | **> 10 mm**, **no endpoint** | Complete disruption |

## 5 Management

```
PLC injury
├─ Grade I or isolated midsubstance grade II → brace locked in extension 4 wk → rehab
├─ Isolated acute grade II avulsion, reducible → repair (augment with graft if tenuous)
├─ Grade III midsubstance / irreparable avulsion → reconstruction (± repair hybrid)
└─ Combined cruciate injury → PLC reconstruction with or before ACL / PCL
       └─ Varus mechanical axis → valgus HTO first (long-leg films)
```

### Operative criteria

| Option | Indication |
|---|---|
| Nonop (brace in extension 4 wk, quad rehab, RTS 8 wk) | **Grade I** · **isolated midsubstance grade II** |
| **Repair** | **Isolated acute grade II avulsion** · structures **anatomically reducible to attachment** |
| Fibular head avulsion | Screws or suture anchors |
| **Reconstruction (hybrid)** | **Grade III midsubstance** · avulsion not repairable / poor tissue |
| **PLC + ACL / PCL reconstruction** | Acute + chronic **combined** injuries |
| **Valgus HTO** | **Varus mechanical alignment** (else ACL + PLC reconstruction fail) |

### Treatment – reconstruction over repair

**Repair → higher failure (OB)** — **midsubstance repair 40% failure** (Orthobullets) · **Levy: 40% repair vs 6% (1 pt) reconstruction** · **Stannard: 37% vs 9%** (57 PLC injuries; both "near-normal" IKDC)\
✗ Wrong options: quicker ROM · better stability · higher short-term scores · worse kinematics

**After revision of failed repair** — outcome scores ≈ primary reconstruction

**ACL reconstruction + PLC repair vs reconstruction** — **33% vs 88%** IKDC A / B

**Outcomes** — operative > nonop · **early treatment better** · anatomic reconstruction restores **rotatory but not all varus** stability

| Reconstruction | Graft / technique | Rebuilds |
|---|---|---|
| **Fibular-based (Larson)** | Soft-tissue graft (semitendinosus / Achilles) through fibular head tunnel → **figure-of-eight** → single femoral tunnel | **LCL + PFL** |
| **Transtibial double-bundle** | Split Achilles to femoral epicondyle isometric point · fibular limb + tibial limb | Fibular limb **LCL** · tibial limb (posterior tibia) **PFL** |
| **LaPrade anatomic** | **Two grafts** · graft 1 femoral LCL site → fibular head (lateral → medial) · graft 2 popliteus site · both dock in tibial tunnel posterior → anterior | **LCL + PFL + popliteus** |

![LaPrade anatomic PLC reconstruction](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3012-laprade-anatomic-plc-reconstruction.jpg)

### Alignment correction & combined ACL revision

**Sequence** — PLC reconstruction **same time or before** ACL / PCL → prevents early cruciate graft failure

**Missed PLC + ACL graft (OB)** — 3+ Lachman, varus laxity at 30°, dial + at 30° dissipating at 90°, varus thrust → **revision ACL + PLC reconstruction** · unrecognised PCL → medial + PF OA without lateral laxity / thrust\
✗ Wrong options: PCL · PMC · hamstring autograft alone

**ACL + PLC + varus + MM tear (OB)** — HTO + ACL + meniscus + PLC reconstruction; **NOT PCL reconstruction** (quad active negative, normal ER at 90°) · untreated varus thrust → ACL graft fails

**Noyes (staged HTO + ACL, 41 pts, \~50% PLC)** — 4 yr: pain ↓ **71%**, giving way gone **85%**, light recreation **66%**

**Grade 3 PLC laxity + varus after ACL + PCL reconstruction (OB)** — **long-leg alignment films first** → primary varus → **medial opening-wedge proximal tibial osteotomy before PLC reconstruction**\
✗ Wrong options: straight to fibular / tibial-fibular reconstruction · PT · bracing

**Untreated PLC ↑ PCL graft force** — Harner (↑ posterior translation, ER, in-situ graft force) · LaPrade (grade 3 PLC)

### Rehabilitation

**After PLC reconstruction (hybrid)** — hinged brace, **NWB 6 wk** · ROM varies (passive 0–90° at once vs 2 wk immobilisation) · at 6 wk WB + closed-chain · **RTS 6–9 months**

**After combined reconstruction** — immobilise + **protected WB 4 wk** (long leg cast controls ER better than brace) · **passive ROM at 4 wk** (avoid arthrofibrosis) · **avoid active hamstring** · full active extension allowed

**PCL reconstruction + PLC repair (OB)** — **no active flexion, active extension 90→0°** · hinged brace, crutches, partial WB first 4 wk · quad sets + SLR\
✗ Wrong options: patellar tendon repair · ACL ± MCL · ACL + PLC

## 6 Complications

**Missed PLC** — failure of ACL or PCL reconstruction

**Peroneal nerve injury** — **15–29%**

**Arthrofibrosis**

## X vs Y

| | **PLC** | **PMC** |
|---|---|---|
| Key static | LCL, popliteus, PFL | **POL**, deep MCL, sMCL |
| Exam | **Dial**, varus, reverse pivot | Valgus, AMRI |
| Instability | **PLRI** | AMRI / PMRI |

| | **Dial + at 30° only** | **Dial + at 30° and 90°** |
|---|---|---|
| Lesion | **Isolated PLC** | **PLC + PCL** |
| Posterior drawer | Negative | Positive |

| | **Repair** | **Reconstruction** |
|---|---|---|
| Indication | Acute grade II **avulsion**, reducible | Grade III midsubstance, chronic, combined |
| Failure | **37–40%** | **6–9%** |

## How it's asked

**"Lateral quadruple complex EXCEPT (TO)?"** → **fabellofibular ligament**

**"NOT part of lateral quadruple complex (TO)?"** → **popliteofibular ligament**

**"LCL relationship in PLC?"** → **inserts anterolateral to PFL on fibula, superficial to popliteus**

**"Normal anatomy of region injured (dial + at 30°)?"** → **biceps femoris inserts posterior to LCL on fibular head**

**"Function of structure at probe tip (popliteus)?"** → **internal tibial rotation**

**"Use of test in image (TO)?"** → **detect PLC injury (dial test)**

**"Positive reverse pivot shift (TO)?"** → **PLC injury**

**"Positive ER recurvatum test (TO)?"** → **combined PLC + ACL injury**

**"Lateral plateau moves posterior to femoral condyle on rotation?"** → **posterolateral rotatory instability**

**"↑ ER at 30° + 90° — least likely torn?"** → **posterior oblique ligament**

**"Finding indicating PLC + PCL?"** → **dial + at 30° and 90°**

**"+ Reverse pivot, negative posterior drawer — other finding?"** → **10° ↑ ER at 30°**

**"Radiograph most associated with PLC injury?"** → **arcuate sign (fibular head avulsion)**

**"Post-op: no active flexion, active extension 90→0°?"** → **PCL reconstruction + PLC repair**

**"ACL tear, dial + at 30° only, varus axis, MM tear — EXCEPT?"** → **PCL reconstruction**

**"Grade 3 PLC laxity + varus after ACL + PCL reconstruction?"** → **long-leg alignment films**

**"Failed ACL graft, varus laxity, dial + at 30° only?"** → **revision ACL + PLC reconstruction**

**"Acute PLC repair vs reconstruction?"** → **higher rate of failure**

# Prepatellar Bursitis (Housemaid's Knee)

*Knee & Sports · 46 / 61*

## 1 Basics

**Frequency** — **most common bursitis of the knee**

**Anatomy** — bursa **anterior to patella** · potential space → gliding of tissue over patella

**Risk factors** — **excessive kneeling** · **wrestlers** → **concern for septic bursitis**

**Septic vs aseptic** — **20% septic**

**Organism** — **S. aureus** = most common in septic prepatellar (and olecranon) bursitis

## 3 Presentation

**Symptoms** — pain, swelling over patella, history of kneeling · **warm**, especially if septic

**Septic vs aseptic** — hard to tell clinically · **extra-articular** → no septic-arthritis pain on knee motion

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

**Aspiration + Gram stain + culture** — **rarely indicated** · use only to separate septic from aseptic

## 5 Management

```
Prepatellar bursitis
├─ Aseptic → compressive wrap, NSAIDs, ± aspiration, immobilisation 1 wk
└─ Septic (fever, spreading erythema) → open bursectomy + IV antibiotics
```

**Aseptic** — compression, NSAIDs, padding, ice, ± aspiration, **1 wk immobilisation** · corticosteroid **controversial**

**Septic** — **complete bursectomy + systemic (IV) antibiotics** = standard · antibiotics + aspiration / percutaneous drain succeed in some

**Recurrent after aspiration, fever 101.7°F, spreading erythema** — **perform open bursectomy + start intravenous (IV) antibiotics**\
✗ Wrong options: oral antibiotics 7 days · re-aspirate + steroid · padding + ice

**Bursal resection** — rarely needed · open or **arthroscopic**

## How it's asked

**"Febrile, erythematous prepatellar bursa after aspiration?"** → **open bursectomy + IV antibiotics**

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Proximal Tib-Fib Dislocation

*Knee & Sports · 47 / 61*

## 1 Basics

**Incidence** — **rare**, rarer isolated · **often missed** · 2nd–4th decades

**Anatomy** — fibular head articulates with facet on lateral tibial cortex (**separate from knee joint**) · anterior + posterior ligaments of fibular head · **common peroneal nerve** distal to joint, **posterolateral fibular neck**

## 2 Mechanism

**Mechanism** — **high energy** (horseback riding, parachuting) · fall on **flexed, adducted knee** · twisting of flexed knee in athletes (OB)

**Associated** — **posterior hip dislocation** (knee + hip flexed) · **open tibia–fibula fractures** · other knee / ankle fractures

## 3 Presentation

**Symptoms** — **lateral knee pain mimicking lateral meniscal tear** · instability · lateral prominence · popping / catching · dorsal foot numbness (peroneal)

**Exam** — fibular head tenderness · **compare both knees** (landmarks)

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Study | Finding / use |
|---|---|
| **AP + lateral of both knees** | **Contralateral comparison views essential** |
| CT | Clearly confirms / excludes dislocation |
| MRI | Normal cruciates + menisci → points away from intra-articular cause |

### Ogden classification

| Type | Fact |
|---|---|
| Subluxation | — |
| **Anterolateral dislocation** | **Most common** |
| Posteromedial dislocation | **Peroneal nerve injury usually with posterior** dislocations |
| Superior dislocation | — |

**4 types (Sekiya & Kuhn)** — subluxation + 3 dislocations · diagnosis frequently missed

![Ogden classification of proximal tibiofibular joint injuries](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3014-shows-the-ogden-classification-of-proximal-tibiofibular-join.jpg)

## 5 Management

```
Proximal tib-fib dislocation
├─ Acute → closed reduction (knee flexed 80–110°)
│      (failed closed reduction → open reduction)
└─ Chronic pain / instability / missed / malreduced → surgery
       └─ soft-tissue (capsule) reconstruction, ORIF + pinning, arthrodesis, fibular head resection
```

**Closed reduction** — **flex knee 80–110°** (relaxes **LCL + biceps femoris**) → **pressure on fibular head opposite to dislocation** · usually successful

**Post-reduction** — immobilisation in extension vs early ROM → **controversial**

**2 wk after rugby fall, popping + dorsal foot numbness, normal MRI (OB)** — **closed reduction**\
✗ Wrong options: meniscal arthroscopy · ACL reconstruction · OCD arthroscopy · EMG for anterior tarsal tunnel (deep peroneal entrapment)

## 6 Complications

**Recurrence**

**Common peroneal nerve injury** — usually **posterior** dislocations · **> 35%** with concomitant proximal tibia fracture

**Arthritis** — rare, minimally symptomatic

## How it's asked

**"Lateral knee pop, catching, dorsal foot numbness, X-ray dislocation, normal MRI — next?"** → **closed reduction of dislocation**

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Proximal Tibiofibular Joint Ganglion Cysts

*Knee & Sports · 48 / 61*

## 1 Basics

**Incidence** — **< 1%** of knee MRIs for pain · age 20–76 · **median 67** (Orthobullets) · "middle-aged males" (OB) · **male 90%** · right leg \~70%

**Location** — **extraneural 80%** > intraneural 20%

**Cause** — repetitive microtrauma · **degeneration** · associated **OA**

**Pathology** — **mucin-filled, synovial-lined sac without true epithelial lining** · collagen walls · viscous (hyaluronic acid, mucopolysaccharides)

**Anatomy** — fibular head in groove behind lateral tibial ridge · capsule stabilisers: anterior + posterior tibiofibular ligaments, LCL, popliteus, biceps · **anterior tibial artery** just distal to PTFJ · **common peroneal nerve** around fibular neck → superficial + deep branches

**Biceps femoris** — long head (posterior on fibular head) → **tibial nerve** · short head (anterior) → **common peroneal nerve**

**PTFJ function** — anterolateral / posteromedial sliding → ↓ ankle torsion, disperses axial load

| Classification | Site |
|---|---|
| **Intraneural** | Within epineurium |
| **Extraneural** | Outside epineurium |

## 3 Presentation

**Symptoms** — usually asymptomatic · pain, **mobile palpable mass distal to lateral joint line**, fullness, **paraesthesias dorsum of foot**

**Neuro** — **foot drop** from common peroneal compression

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Study | Finding / use |
|---|---|
| X-ray (AP + lateral knee / tibia) | Usually normal · ± sclerosis lateral proximal tibia |
| **MRI** | **Diagnostic tool of choice** · well-circumscribed homogeneous cyst · **T1 low, T2 high** |
| US | If MRI not possible · thin-walled fluid cyst |
| EMG / NCS | Extent of sensory + motor deficit |
| Histology | **Confirms** · no true epithelium, no atypia / mitoses |

![PTFJ ganglion, T2 MRI](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob423108-t2-ptfj-cyst.jpg)

**Differential** — ABC, chondroblastoma, GCT, juxta-articular myxoma, **lumbar disc herniation**, PVNS, solid nerve tumours

## 5 Management

```
PTFJ ganglion
├─ Asymptomatic / first presentation → observation, NSAIDs, aspiration
├─ Neuro symptoms / failed nonop / progressive → decompression + marginal excision
└─ Recurrence after first excision → PTFJ arthrodesis (or fibular head resection)
```

| Treatment | Recurrence |
|---|---|
| **Aspiration alone** | **80%** (Orthobullets) · **25–80%** (OB) |
| **Decompression + excision** (hockey-stick lateral approach, neurolysis, excise stalk) | **8–25%** · 1st excision success 7 / 8; repeat excision 3 / 3 recurred (Miskovsky) |
| **PTFJ arthrodesis** (6.5 mm partially threaded cancellous screw) | **< 5%** — lowest |

**Painful growing mass + dorsal foot paraesthesias (OB)** — **surgical decompression + cyst excision**\
✗ Wrong options: NSAIDs / activity change · aspiration · arthrodesis / fibular head resection (reserved for recurrence)

**Recurrent cyst** — Gulati: proximal fibular excision, good results in 9 · arthrodesis (Miskovsky: 2 fused, no recurrence)

## 6 Complications

**Peroneal nerve dysfunction** — **up to 50%** · risk: **intraneural** · decompression; tendon transfer if complete palsy · prolonged neuro symptoms = poor prognosis

**Recurrence** — **5–80%** · aspiration only highest

**Other** — neurapraxia, perineural fibrosis · hardware irritation after arthrodesis

## How it's asked

**"PTFJ ganglion with peroneal paraesthesias — reduce recurrence?"** → **surgical decompression and excision of cyst**

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Pulmonary Conditions in Athletes

*Knee & Sports · 49 / 61*

## 1 Basics

### Airway disorders

**Asthma** — **transient lower airway obstruction** from hypersensitivity · **expiratory** · exercise trigger in an asthmatic = **exercise-induced asthma (EIA)**

**EIB** — **no underlying asthma** (usually; can coexist, OB) · transient **lower**-airway, **expiratory** obstruction after exertion

**EIB risk factors** — **winter / cold-weather sports** (drying + cooling of mucosa → oedema + constriction) · **endurance athletes** · high minute ventilation\
✗ Wrong options: warm weather

**EILO** — transient **upper**-airway obstruction: paradoxical vocal-cord closure or supraglottic narrowing · **inspiratory** · prominent **psychological** component · types: **supraglottic, glottic (vocal cord dysfunction), mixed**

**Exercise-induced anaphylaxis** — rare, life-threatening **type I (IgE)** reaction to exertion · after a food allergen = **food-dependent EIA (FDEIA)**

### Chest wall & vascular conditions

**Pneumothorax causes** — **spontaneous** (smoking, male, FHx, Marfan, Ehlers-Danlos) · **traumatic** (**contact athletes — ice hockey highest risk**) · **iatrogenic** (interscalene block)

**Haemothorax** — **most common result of major chest-wall trauma** · **30%** of rib fractures → pneumohaemothorax

**PE in athletes** — ↑ risk: musculoskeletal injury ± immobilisation, repetitive motion (pitchers, tennis), long-distance travel

## 3 Presentation

**Asthma** — wheeze, SOB, **chest tightness**; **expiratory wheeze**, accessory muscles

**EIB** — **triad: wheeze + SOB + cough** with exercise · "poor performance", fatigue, headache · exam often normal

**EIB timing** — onset **10–15 min after exertion**, resolves with **20 min rest** (Orthobullets) · 10–15 min after the **start** of exercise; resolves **15–20 min** after stopping (OB) — read the options

**Pseudo-asthma syndrome** — exercise-induced hyperventilation; no bronchoconstriction

**EILO** — **rapid onset**, **throat tightness / choking**, "breathing through a straw" · **inspiratory stridor** · **fails bronchodilators** (often misdiagnosed as EIA / EIB)

**Exercise-induced anaphylaxis** — onset **within 30 min** of exercise · pruritus, urticaria, GI cramps, SOB, throat tightness

**Pneumothorax** — sudden unilateral **pleuritic pain**, SOB · ↓ / absent breath sounds, **hyperresonant** · tension adds **tracheal deviation + JVD**, cardiopulmonary compromise

**Tension pneumothorax on the field** — dyspnoea, anxiety, **deviated trachea**, **hypertympanic** chest, ↓ breath sounds\
✗ Wrong options: spontaneous (closed, no deviation) · haemothorax (dull) · EIB · HCM

**Haemothorax** — less pleuritic pain · **hypotension** if large · **dull to percussion**

**PE** — sudden pleuritic pain, SOB, syncope · tachycardia, **hypoxia**, hypotension if large

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Condition | Test | Finding |
|---|---|---|
| Asthma | PFT ± **methacholine** challenge | **↓ FEV1, ↓ FEV1/FVC**, reverses with bronchodilator |
| **EIB** | **Eucapnic voluntary hyperventilation (EVH)** = gold standard | **> 10% ↓ FEV1**; baseline PFT may be normal |
| **EILO** | **Continuous exercise laryngoscopy (CEL)** = gold standard | PFT: **blunted inspiratory loop** |
| Pneumothorax | **Ultrasound** | **Absent lung sliding** |
| Pneumothorax | **CXR** | **Pleural line**, absent lung markings · tension: **mediastinal shift + depressed hemidiaphragm** |
| Haemothorax | CXR | **Opacified hemithorax** |
| PE | **Helical CT** first line · **pulmonary angiography** gold standard | V/Q scan alternative |
| HAPE (TO) | CXR | Patchy opacities, often **right middle lobe** |

![Tension pneumothorax: mediastinal shift, depressed hemidiaphragm](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3122-cxrtensionptx.jpg)

### Pneumothorax types

| Type | Communication | Pleural pressure |
|---|---|---|
| **Closed** (e.g. bleb rupture) | None | **< atmospheric** → no tracheal deviation |
| **Open** | Air in on inspiration, out on expiration | **= atmospheric**, no tension |
| **Tension** | **One-way: in, cannot exit** | **↑ > atmospheric** → lung + mediastinal compression — **emergency** |

## 5 Management

### Return-to-play criteria

| Condition | Return to play |
|---|---|
| **Asthma / EIB** | As tolerated · **active wheezing = contraindication** |
| **Exercise-induced anaphylaxis** | After **allergist review + comprehensive action plan** · always carry **EpiPen**, exercise with a partner |
| **Pneumothorax** | **Radiographic resolution, \~4 weeks** · pain limits RTP · **no flying 2–4 weeks** |
| **PE** | **\~6 months** · on anticoagulation controversial · **contact athletes withheld** during anticoagulation |

### Exercise-induced bronchospasm

**Acute** — rest, controlled breathing, **inhaled β2-agonist (albuterol q15 min PRN)** · severe / refractory → **epinephrine + ER** · moderate–severe asthma maintenance → **inhaled glucocorticoids**

**Prevention** — **warm-up**, environmental control · **"refractory period"** (deliberate induction) works in **\~50%** · **albuterol 2 puffs 15–20 min before** activity

### EILO & anaphylaxis

**EILO** — nonoperative mainstay: **laryngeal control therapy** (lower-abdominal breathing, controlled exhalation through mouth), speech therapy, SSRIs, botulinum toxin · surgery (debulking) rare

**Anaphylaxis prevention** — no exercise **4–6 h after eating**, avoid temperature extremes, **avoid aspirin / NSAIDs** before exercise · acute: **epinephrine**, antihistamines, steroids

### Tension pneumothorax – diagnosis & needle decompression

**ATLS 10th, adults** — **standard 14-gauge 4.5 cm angiocatheter (5 cm needle), 4th / 5th ICS mid-axillary line** ✓

**Children** — **2nd ICS mid-clavicular line** still recommended

**Why changed** — 5 cm needle at 2nd ICS **fails in 22–50%** (chest-wall thickness) · risk to internal thoracic, subclavian, heart · no original evidence

**Site evidence** — 5th ICS accurate **100%** vs just over **50%** at 2nd ICS (Inaba cadaver study) · either site reasonable, **2nd ICS slightly better in obese** (Azizi) — read the options

**Site wording** — "2nd or 5th ICS" acceptable (Orthobullets, OB)

**Failed decompression** — check catheter patency → **longer 8.25 cm needle** → **finger thoracostomy**\
✗ Wrong options: any of these first

**Traps** — ✗ Wrong options: chest tube first (hospital; supplies rarely on hand) · digital thoracostomy first · long needle first (visceral injury)

```
Pneumothorax
├─ Tension → needle decompression 5th ICS MAL → chest tube in ED
├─ > 20% (lung edge > 2 cm) → chest tube + admit
└─ < 20% (< 2 cm), stable → O2, observe 4–6 h, repeat CXR
```

**Observation arm** — O2 ↑ pleural air absorption · repeat CXR before discharge + at **12–48 h**

### Haemothorax & pulmonary embolism

**Haemothorax** — **chest tube** first · repeat CXR at **4–6 h** and **24 h**

| VATS / thoracotomy criterion | Threshold |
|---|---|
| Initial chest-tube output | **> 1500 cc** |
| Ongoing output | **> 200 cc/h for 3+ h** |
| Haemothorax size | Increasing |
| Despite **2 chest tubes** | Incomplete resolution |

**PE** — anticoagulation **3–6 months (minimum 3)** · IV heparin / Lovenox → oral

### High-altitude pulmonary oedema (TO)

**High-altitude illness (TO)** — **AMS, HACE, HAPE** · usual threshold **8000 ft (2500 m)** with rapid ascent

**HAPE (TO)** — **noncardiogenic** oedema; lowlanders ascending rapidly to **> 2500–3000 m** · **exaggerated hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction**, ↑ PA pressure → capillary **"stress failure"** leak

**Onset (TO)** — **2–5 days** after arrival · rare below 2500–3000 m or after **1 week** of acclimatisation

**Symptoms (TO)** — dry cough, exertional dyspnoea, ↓ performance → rest dyspnoea, orthopnoea · **gurgling + pink frothy sputum = advanced** · cyanosis, tachycardia, temp ≤ **38.5 °C** · rales over middle lung fields

**Prevention (TO)** — **nifedipine, tadalafil, dexamethasone, acetazolamide** (500–1000 mg/day 8–12-hourly or 125 mg BD, start **24 h before ascent**)

**Treatment (TO)** — **supplemental oxygen = first line / first step** ✓ · **immediate descent = definitive** · portable hyperbaric chamber · nifedipine 30 mg SR 12-hourly\
✗ Wrong options: descent by 1000 ft · hypoxia chamber · oral nifedipine first

**Remote, no medical care (TO)** — **descent has first priority** — read the stem's setting

## X vs Y

| | **EIA / asthma** | **EIB** | **EILO** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Underlying asthma | **Yes** | **No** | No |
| Airway | Lower | **Lower** | **Upper (larynx)** |
| Phase | Expiratory | Expiratory | **Inspiratory** |
| Key symptom | Chest tightness, wheeze | Wheeze + SOB + cough | **Throat tightness, choking, stridor** |
| Spirometry | ↓ FEV1, ↓ FEV1/FVC | **> 10% ↓ FEV1** | Blunted inspiratory loop |
| Bronchodilator | Improves | Improves | **No response** |
| Gold-standard test | PFT ± methacholine | **EVH** | **CEL** |

| | **Simple pneumothorax** | **Tension pneumothorax** | **Haemothorax** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Percussion | Hyperresonant | **Hypertympanic** | **Dull** |
| Trachea | Midline | **Deviated + JVD** | Midline |
| CXR | Pleural line | **Mediastinal shift, low hemidiaphragm** | Opacification |
| First step | O2 / observe or chest tube | **Needle decompression** | **Chest tube** |

## How it's asked

**"Tension pneumothorax — ATLS next step?"** → **standard 14-G 5 cm needle, 5th ICS mid-axillary line**

**"Helmet to chest, deviated trachea, hypertympanic?"** → **tension pneumothorax**

**"Occasional β2-agonist for exertional wheeze — true?"** → **FEV1 reduction on spirometry**

**"Pink frothy sputum at altitude — first step (TO)?"** → **oxygen**

**"Throat tightness, stridor, inhaler fails?"** → **EILO → continuous exercise laryngoscopy**

**"Pneumothorax — return to play?"** → **radiographic resolution, \~4 weeks**

# Quadriceps Contusion

*Knee & Sports · 50 / 61*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology

**Who** — **M:F 2:1** · **football, soccer, rugby** · competition > practice

### Pathophysiology

**Mechanism** — **direct blow** compresses anterior thigh muscles **against the femur**

**Sequence** — small fibre tears → haemorrhage + swelling in **anterior compartment** → myonecrosis + **haematoma** → scar → muscle regeneration

| Thigh compartment | Muscles |
|---|---|
| **Anterior** | Sartorius, rectus femoris, vastus lateralis / medialis / intermedius |
| Medial | Gracilis, adductor magnus / longus / brevis |
| Posterior | Biceps femoris, semitendinosus, semimembranosus |

### Muscle healing biology (immobilisation, losartan)

**Prolonged immobilisation** — **↑ granulation tissue** (→ myositis ossificans) · scar contraction · poor organisation · **↓ tensile strength + ↓ tensile stiffness**\
✗ Wrong options: ↑ stiffness · better tensile strength · ↓ MO

**Brief immobilisation → mobilisation** — **parallel penetration of muscle fibres through (limited) scar**, in line with native muscle · ↑ myotubules, early nerve regeneration, ↓ inflammation\
✗ Wrong options: lymphocyte / macrophage infiltrate · ↓ nerve fibrils · narrowed myotubes

**Immobilisation alone** — **disorganised** fibre penetration through **dense** scar

**Losartan (angiotensin II receptor blocker)** — **↑ muscle regeneration, ↓ fibrosis** (animal model) · ↓ apoptotic cascade · thought to act via **insulin-like growth factor** blockade (OB)\
✗ Wrong options: aspirin · allopurinol · acetaminophen · bupropion

**PRP + losartan** — ↑ angiogenesis + **follistatin**, **↓ phosphorylated Smad2/3**, ↓ fibrosis

## 3 Presentation

**History** — collision / direct blow in competition

**Pain** — **worsens over first 24–48 h** · worse with dynamic movement + **knee flexion**

**Exam** — swelling, **ecchymosis, point tenderness** · compare firmness + circumference · palpable defect = partial / complete tear · ± knee effusion · **↓ knee flexion** · antalgic gait

**Straight leg raise** — tests **extensor mechanism** · able (even with pain) → extensor mechanism intact

**Distal NV exam** — screen for **thigh compartment syndrome**

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Study | Use / finding |
|---|---|
| X-ray | Only for severe injury with **suspected fracture** · chronic → **myositis ossificans ("egg-shell" calcification)** |
| **MRI** | Concern for **extensor mechanism disruption** · best for soft tissue · **oedema / haematoma in anterior compartment** |
| CT for arterial extravasation | not part of initial work-up (no signs of active bleeding) |

### Jackson and Feagin classification

| Grade (knee ROM at 24 h) | Knee flexion |
|---|---|
| **Mild** | **> 90°** |
| **Moderate** | **45–90°** |
| **Severe** | **< 45°** |

## 5 Management

```
Quadriceps contusion
├─ All acute → 120° FLEXION 24–48 h + ICE + NSAIDs → PT
├─ Compartment syndrome → THIGH FASCIOTOMY
└─ Myositis ossificans → OBSERVE
       └─ painful, ↓ ROM / strength, MATURE (6–12 mo) → RESECT
```

### Treatment — immobilisation in 120° flexion

**Immobilise** — **knee in 120° flexion** (ace wrap / hinged brace) **immediately**, **24–48 h** · cryotherapy, NSAIDs · start at once to **limit haematoma**

![Immobilisation of the knee in 120° flexion](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3103-quad-contusion-moved.jpg)

**Then** — stretching + active ROM, protected WB with crutches → sport-specific rehab once full pain-free ROM

**17-yr, firm thigh, SLR with pain, NV intact** — **cryotherapy, NSAIDs, hyperflexion splinting** (overnight → ↓ MO)\
✗ Wrong options: CT for extravasation · emergent fasciotomy · **splint in extension** · MRI for extensor rupture

**Knee immobilised in 120° flexion → injury?** — **quadriceps contusion**\
✗ Wrong options: iliac crest contusion (hip pointer leg in **extension**) · lesser trochanter avulsion · hamstring rupture · sports hernia

**MRI next day** — **haematoma in anterior compartment of thigh**\
✗ Wrong options: meniscal, MCL, ACL, hamstring tears

| Study | Cohort | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Jackson | Midshipmen, **24 h hyperflexion** | RTP **3.5 days** · MO 1 / 23 |
| Aronen | 47 military, **120° for 24 h** | RTP **3.5 days** |
| Ryan | 117 West Point cadets | Disability **mild 13 d, severe 21 d** · **MO 9%** |

**Disability time** — **3–13 days** across studies · **13 days for mild** (Orthobullets) · time to return ∝ initial severity

### Thigh fasciotomy

**Indication** — **compartment syndrome** (from rupture of **deep perforating branches of deep femoral vessels**)

**Approach** — **single anterolateral incision** → incise **fascia lata** (anterior) → retract **vastus lateralis medially** → incise **lateral intermuscular septum** (posterior) · ± **medial incision** for adductor compartment

![Mid-thigh cross-section: anterior, medial and posterior compartments](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3103-diagram-showing-cross-section-at-mid-thigh-level.jpg)

## 6 Complications

### Myositis ossificans

**Incidence** — **9–18%** (Orthobullets) · **5% and 9%** (OB study series) — read the options · more with **severe** contusions

**Risk factors (Ryan)** — **knee motion < 120°**, injury in football, **previous quadriceps injury**, **delay in treatment > 3 days**, **ipsilateral knee effusion**

**Timing** — develops **2–4 wk** after injury · serial X-rays → **"egg-shell" calcification**

**Treatment** — **observation** usually · **resect only mature lesions** (no growth, **6–12 months**) with pain + ↓ strength / ROM

**Compartment syndrome** — **thigh fasciotomy**

**Prognosis** — self-limited, but prolonged disability without treatment · early treatment speeds RTP

## X vs Y

| | **Quadriceps contusion** | **Hip pointer** |
|---|---|---|
| Site | Anterior thigh | Iliac crest |
| Position | **Knee flexed 120°** | **Leg in extension** (maximum stretch) |
| Chronic risk | **Myositis ossificans** | — |

| | **Brief immobilisation → mobilisation** | **Prolonged immobilisation** |
|---|---|---|
| Fibres | **Parallel** penetration, limited scar | **Disorganised**, dense scar |
| Tissue | ↓ inflammation, early nerve regrowth | **↑ granulation tissue** |
| Mechanics | Better strength | **↓ tensile strength, ↓ stiffness** |

## How it's asked

**"Immobilised in 120° knee flexion — diagnosis?"** → **quadriceps contusion**

**"Thigh contusion, SLR with pain, NV intact — next step?"** → **cryotherapy, NSAIDs, hyperflexion splinting**

**"MRI after flexion immobilisation shows?"** → **haematoma in the anterior compartment of the thigh**

**"Drug that improves muscle regeneration + ↓ fibrosis (animal)?"** → **losartan**

**"Prolonged immobilisation after contusion leads to?"** → **increased granulation tissue production**

**"Histology at 4 wk with early mobilisation?"** → **parallel penetration of muscle fibre through scar tissue**
