# The Female Athlete

*Knee & Sports · 58 / 61*

## 1 Basics

### Physiologic sex differences

**Women have** — **↑ body fat %** · **↓ maximal O₂ consumption + haemoglobin** · **↓ cardiac output** · **↓ muscle mass + strength**

**↑ incidence in women** — **patellofemoral disorders**, **stress fractures**, **ACL injuries**

### Triad definition & components (low energy availability, amenorrhoea, BMD; thyroid not included)

| Component | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Low energy availability** | **± disordered eating** (with or without an eating disorder) · insufficient caloric intake for demand |
| **Menstrual dysfunction** | **Amenorrhoea** / oligomenorrhoea · energy imbalance, low body fat, hypothalamic–pituitary axis changes |
| **↓ Bone mineral density** | **Osteoporosis** (often presents as a **stress fracture**) |

**NOT components** — **stress fracture (TO)** — a consequence, not a component · **thyroid dysfunction** (OB) · ligamentous laxity, ↑ ACL tears, hirsutism (OB)

**Dysmenorrhoea in the options (TO)** — counted under menstrual dysfunction → not the answer; **stress fracture** is the odd one out

**Eating disorder** — both "LEA **with** an eating disorder" and "LEA **without** an eating disorder" = components (OB)

**Names** — **female athlete triad**, coined **1992 by ACSM** (adolescent + young female athletes) · **anorexia athletica** = synonym (Orthobullets, OB) · **RED-S** (relative energy deficiency in sport) — **IOC 2014**, broader, **includes males**

**Anorexia athletica criteria (OB)** — **GI complaints**, **restriction of caloric intake**, **weight loss**, **menstrual dysfunction** · **requires ABSENCE of an affective disorder** (e.g. depression) · true osteoporosis relatively **uncommon** in female athletes → traditional triad criteria rarely met\
✗ Wrong options: presence of affective disorder

**Thyroid (OB)** — **not a core component**; **may occur secondarily** to the metabolic + hormonal disruption\
✗ Wrong options: core component · unrelated · primary cause · always present / needed for diagnosis

**Associated (OB)** — amenorrhoea, osteoporosis, **↓ immune function**, insufficient caloric intake · not **low LDL** → actually **↑ LDL** (hypoestrogenism)

**Cardiovascular (OB)** — **endothelial dysfunction** (nitric oxide imbalance between vasodilators + vasoconstrictors) → atherosclerosis, ↑ future CVD · RED-S: **dyslipidaemia + ↑ visceral adiposity → precocious coronary atherosclerosis**

### Pathophysiology – energy deficit & hormones

**Root cause** — **low energy availability (LEA)** = cornerstone of RED-S + triad · drives the hormonal + bone deficits

**Axis** — LEA disrupts **hypothalamic–pituitary–ovarian axis** (OB) · "**hypothalamic–pituitary–adrenal** axis disruption" (OB, RED-S) → reproductive hormonal dysfunction

**Bone** — **↓ BMD from ↑ bone turnover** · loss of **oestrogen / progesterone** protective effect → osteoporosis → stress fractures

**Undernutrition (TO)** — **↓ IGF-1 + ↓ leptin** → ↓ appetite stimulation, menstrual problems, impaired bone remodelling → **osteopenia + stress fractures**

![Hypothalamic–pituitary–ovarian feedback loop](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3125-feedback-loop.jpg)

### Genes & lab markers

| Marker | Direction / value | Why |
|---|---|---|
| **Cortisol** | **↑** | Maintains blood glucose (OB) |
| **Free T3** | **↓** | Lowers basal metabolic rate (OB) |
| **LH** | **↓** | Diverts energy from reproduction (OB) |
| IGF-1, leptin | ↓ | Undernutrition (TO) |
| Oestrogen | ↓ | Hypoestrogenism from menstrual dysfunction |
| **LDL** | **↑** | Hypoestrogenism (OB) (✗ Wrong options: low LDL) |
| Serum calcium | **Normal** | Not a useful next test (OB) |
| 25(OH)D | Normal **≥ 30 ng/mL** · **< 75.8 nmol/L = stress fracture risk** | TO |
| **Iron status** | **Ferritin / transferrin ratio** | TO — see Calcium & iron |
| **COL5A1** | **CC genotype under-represented** in females with ACL rupture | Orthobullets |

**Hormone trap (OB)** — **↑ cortisol, ↓ free T3, ↓ LH**\
✗ Wrong options: ↑ T3 · ↑ LH · ↓ cortisol

### Epidemiology – amenorrhoea & sports

| Amenorrhoea prevalence | Source |
|---|---|
| **Elite runners — nearly 50%** | Orthobullets, OB |
| Vigorous exercisers **10–20%** · elite runners + professional ballet dancers **40–66%** | OB |
| Exercise-induced **5–25%** (by level + sport) | OB |

**Sports** — **running + gymnastics** · aesthetic / endurance (**gymnastics, cross-country**) · higher stress fracture risk: **gymnastics, cross-country, lacrosse** · multiple sports (not a risk factor)

### Pregnancy & Title IX

**Pregnancy** — **↑ estradiol → ligamentous laxity** · ↑ body weight → **16–32% ↑ O₂ consumption**

**Title IX** — no person in the US excluded, denied benefits or discriminated against **on the basis of sex** in any educational programme receiving **federal financial assistance**

## 2 Mechanism

### Sex differences in ACL injury

**Risk** — **2–10× males** (Orthobullets, this topic), esp. **pivoting sports** · ACL Tear chapter: **4.5 : 1** (Orthobullets), **2–9×** (OB), **\~8×** (TO) — read the options

| Factor (Orthobullets) | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Landing biomechanics + neuromuscular control** | **Conditioning + strength = biggest role** · land with knee in **more extension + valgus** (from **hip IR**) |
| Notch | **Smaller notch** |
| Ligament | **Smaller ACL** |
| **Cyclic hormones** | ↑ risk in **first half (preovulatory phase)** of cycle |
| Alignment | Leg alignment |
| **Genetics** | **CC genotype of COL5A1** under-represented in females with ACL rupture |

**Menstrual phase conflict** — **preovulatory** (Orthobullets, this topic) · **no consensus** (ACL Tear chapter) — read the options

**Most significant contributor (OB)** — **neuromuscular coordination + training** — **modifiable**\
✗ Wrong options: ACL size · oestrogen levels · notch size · valgus alignment

**Prevention** — **neuromuscular training (jump training)**, plyometric conditioning ↓ ACL rate · NM training **↑ medial hamstring EMG → ↓ dynamic valgus** on sidecutting (Zebis)

![Valgus medial knee collapse on landing](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3125-valgus-medial-knee-collapse.jpg)

## 3 Presentation

### Stress fracture as presentation – menstrual & nutritional work-up

**First contact** — often presents to the **orthopaedic surgeon / team physician with a stress (osteoporotic) fracture**

**Stress fracture risk** — **amenorrhoeic athletes 2–4× eumenorrhoeic** (TO) · **most frequent site = tibia** (TO) · menstrual irregularity → **more and multiple** stress fractures (Warren)

**Sites (OB)** — **weight-bearing cortical bone: tibia, metatarsals, femur** · not usually trabecular bone

**NOT ↑ in amenorrhoeic athletes (OB)** — femoral head AVN, ACL rupture, labral tear, FAI → answer = **stress fracture**

**Amenorrhoea + Beighton 4 (volleyball)** — at risk of **tension-side femoral neck stress fracture** (Figure A) · Beighton **> 5/9** = hypermobility (OB)

![Tension-side femoral neck stress fracture (arrow)](../../MCQ/attachments/q1468-illa-femoral-neck-stress-fx-arrow.jpg)

**Runner, tibial stress fracture, BMI 16, no menses 7 mo** — **poor caloric intake** = **most common cause of amenorrhoea** in female athletes\
✗ Wrong options: NF1 (tibial bowing, MPNST) · osteofibrous dysplasia (anterior eccentric tibial lesion in child) · EWS-FLI1 (Ewing) · focal fibrocartilaginous dysplasia (abrupt varus at metaphyseal–diaphyseal junction)

**4th MT stress fracture + contralateral 1 yr ago** — **amenorrhoea 12 months**\
✗ Wrong options: daily OCP (helps regulate menses) · multiple sports · T-score −0.5 (normal) · BMI 26 (normal)

**Thresholds (OB)** — **BMI < 21** predictive of stress fracture (some) · **T-score < −1.0 osteopenia**, **< −2.5 osteoporosis**

**Marine recruits (OB)** — **\~5%** of female recruits fracture in **13 wk** basic training · **\~40% femoral / pelvic** (more severe) · biggest risk = **no menses in the previous year** (vs 10–12 menses) · lesser irregularity → trend only\
✗ Wrong options: prior mileage · running frequency · self-rated fitness · race

**Peak bone mass** — hypoestrogenic athletes **may never reach peak bone mass** → possibly **irreversible** deficit · **neither calcium nor ↑ activity compensates** · lost BMD may never be regained

### Runner's nipples (TO)

**Cause (TO)** — **irritation from rubbing against clothing** · **cold-weather sports** (cold → hard, prominent nipples) · common in **cyclists** (cold + perspiration)\
✗ Wrong options: warm weather precipitates

**Treatment (TO)** — **tape nipples** to restrict movement · petroleum jelly, moisturisers, vitamin E soaks · **sports bra**

**Sports bra (TO)** — firm but elastic (room for padding), **non-abrasive**, absorptive · **crossed Y-shaped back, no hooks / fasteners** · **no seams over nipple** · personally fitted

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Diagnostic criteria

| Term | Definition | Source |
|---|---|---|
| **Secondary amenorrhoea** | **Cessation of menses for 6 months after ≥ 1 normal cycle** | Orthobullets, OB |
| Secondary amenorrhoea | Absence of bleeding **6 months** or of **3–6 consecutive cycles** after normal menses (no pregnancy) | OB |
| Amenorrhoea (RED-S) | No menses for **≥ 3 months** | OB |
| Osteopenia / osteoporosis | T-score **< −1.0** / **< −2.5** | OB |
| Hypermobility | Beighton **> 5/9** | OB |

**Amenorrhoea cut-off conflict** — **6 months** (Orthobullets, OB) · **3 months** (OB, RED-S) — read the options\
✗ Wrong options: ↑ volume 3 mo · pain 2 mo · missed month after peak training · ↓ volume 2 mo

**Pre-participation screening (TO)** — triad domains = **eating patterns, menstrual cycles, stress fracture history** · source gives **no key**; the item **outside the triad** = **supplement / drug-abuse history** (inferred)

### Work-up of a female athlete with a stress fracture

**Any female athlete with a stress fracture** — work up for triad: **menstrual history + nutritional consultation + bone density** (OB)

**DEXA** — female athlete with **amenorrhoea + stress fractures** (Orthobullets)

**Runner, femoral neck stress fracture on MRI** — evaluate **osteoporosis + anorexia + secondary amenorrhoea** (all of the above)

**Prior 5th MT stress fracture, now tension-side femoral neck** — fix + **obtain menstrual history**\
✗ Wrong options: never compete again (can return when counselled) · serum calcium (normal) · psychiatry (only if eating disorder diagnosed) · ↓ training (not next step)

**Femoral neck rule (OB)** — **tension side → internal fixation** · compression side → rest, nonop (line > 50% or effusion → screws: Femoral Neck Stress Fractures chapter)

### Key radiology findings

| Study | Finding / use |
|---|---|
| Radiographs | **May be normal or equivocal** · screening films may miss stress reaction |
| X-ray femur | **Smooth cortical thickening, distal medial femoral diaphysis** = femoral stress injury |
| X-ray tibia (lateral) | **Anterior cortical thickening** = stress reaction · **"dreaded black line"** = poor prognosis → surgery |
| **MRI** | **Preferred** · rule out fracture in **high-risk areas (femur, femoral neck)** · **before return to sport** · stress reaction vs impending / complete fracture · STIR oedema (periosteum, cortex, marrow) |
| Bone scan | **Fallen out of favour** — radioisotope + relatively high ionising radiation |
| **DEXA** | BMD — amenorrhoea + stress fractures |

### Hip flexor tightness – modified Thomas test (TO)

**Technique (TO)** — supine, **knees bent over table edge** · hold one knee to chest · test knee should stay at **90°**, hip + posterior thigh flat on table · **pass** = knee stays at 90° · **fail** = knee extends to < 90°

| Finding (TO) | Interpretation | Muscles short |
|---|---|---|
| Thigh **touches** table, knee **stays flexed** | **Negative** | — |
| Thigh **off** table, knee **extends** | **Both one- + two-joint** flexors short | All below |
| **Thigh touches table, knee extends** | **Two-joint hip flexors short** | **Rectus femoris, TFL, sartorius** |
| Thigh **off** table, knee **flexes > 80°** | **One-joint** hip flexors short | **Iliopsoas, pectineus, adductor longus / brevis / magnus** |
| Thigh abducts as hip extends, **lateral patellar deviation**, thigh IR + leg ER | **TFL** short | TFL |
| Hip abduction + flexion + ER and knee flexion (**≥ 3** = tight) | **Sartorius** short | Sartorius |

| Muscle (TO) | Joints crossed | Actions |
|---|---|---|
| Iliopsoas | **1** (hip) | Hip flexion, ER |
| Pectineus | **1** | Hip flexion · abduction (as listed) |
| Adductor longus / brevis | **1** | Adduction, flexion, ER |
| Adductor magnus | **1** | Adductor part: flexion, adduction, ER · hamstring part: extension, IR |
| **Rectus femoris** | **2** (hip + knee) | Hip flexion, **knee extension** |
| **TFL** | **2** | Hip abduction, flexion, IR · knee extension |
| **Sartorius** | **2** | Hip flexion, abduction, ER · **knee flexion** |

## 5 Management

```
Female athlete triad with stress fracture
├─ First / most critical step → multidisciplinary nutrition + psychological counselling
├─ Thigh pain at rest, equivocal X-ray → stop training + MRI
├─ Tension-side femoral neck → fixation + menstrual history
└─ Tibial stress reaction, no fracture line → nonop + review diet and training
```

### Treatment – nutrition rather than bisphosphonates

| Arm | Content |
|---|---|
| **Psychological counselling** | **Core** of treatment (Sabatini) · eating behaviours, self-esteem |
| **Dietary management** | **Establish energy balance** · ↑ weight / food intake, ↓ exercise · ↑ **calcium + vitamin D** |
| **Training** | **↓ intensity + cross-training** (↓ stress fracture risk) · **limit training until menses resume** |
| Supplements | **Calcium + vitamin D** for osteoporosis · consider **cyclic oestrogens / progesterones** for osteoporosis |
| **OCPs** | **May help amenorrhoea** (Orthobullets, OB) · **use OCPs to normalise menses** (TO) |
| Team | Physician, athletic trainer, **sports dietician**, mental-health provider, family, coaches |

**Least appropriate (OB)** — **bisphosphonates**\
✗ Wrong options: calcium + vitamin D · ↑ food intake · ↓ training · counselling

**Advice NOT suitable (TO)** — **"avoid OCPs"** · TO Rx = OCPs to normalise menstruation + **↑ body fat** via diet + prompt stress fracture care\
✗ Wrong options: improve nutrition · increase body fat

**Hormone conflict** — OCPs beneficial (Orthobullets, OB, TO) · **hormone replacement does not fix the energy deficit; no longer primary Rx for functional hypothalamic amenorrhoea** (OB) — read the options

**Most critical initial step (OB)** — **multidisciplinary nutritional + psychological counselling to correct energy deficit**\
✗ Wrong options: NSAIDs + crutches (NSAIDs impair bone healing) · oral oestrogen / progesterone · 6 wk complete rest (deficit persists → recurrence) · DEXA first

**Tibial stress reaction, 3 mo amenorrhoea (15 yr)** — **discuss eating habits + training regimen** · stop the precipitating activity, protected WB rehab\
✗ Wrong options: keep running · CAM walker / casting (stiffness) · IM nail (no clear fracture line → nonop first)

**Hurdler, vegan, BMI 18.1, femoral cortical thickening, pain walking** — **immediate cessation of training + MRI**\
✗ Wrong options: ↓ intensity + PT · dietician only · bone scan + oncology · bone stimulator / US (evidence limited for femoral)

**Natural course** — appropriate diet + moderated exercise → **natural return of menses**

**Recovery rates** — **energy status: days–weeks** (↑ IGF-1, reverses energy conservation) → **menses: months** (oestrogen anti-resorptive) → **BMD: years**

![Triad components recover at different rates](../../MCQ/attachments/q5522-illa-triadrecov.jpg)

**Return to sport** — when properly counselled, may **return to high-endurance sport** (OB)

### Calcium & iron requirements (TO)

**Calcium for stress-fracture prophylaxis (TO)** — **2000 mg/day** + **vitamin D 800 IU/day** (= **200% + 150%** of recommended intake)\
✗ Wrong options: 500 · 1000 · 1500 mg

**Vitamin D (TO)** — intake up to **2000 IU/day** · normal **≥ 30 ng/mL** · **25(OH)D < 75.8 nmol/L** = stress fracture risk · supplementation ↓ stress fracture prevalence · high 25(OH)D → ↓ body fat, ↑ muscle mass

**Vitamin D biology (TO)** — fat-soluble, first found in **cod liver oil** · from diet (fortified dairy, fish oils) or **UV irradiation of 7-dehydrocholesterol** in skin · active **1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D** regulates **> 900 gene variants**

**Stress fractures in athletes (TO)** — prevalence **6.5–9.7%** · **metatarsals + tibia** commonest · risk factors: **female sex, age > 40 or < 18**, high arches, LLD, flat feet

**Best iron test in female athlete (TO)** — **ferritin / transferrin ratio**\
✗ Wrong options: ferritin · transferrin saturation · hepcidin

**Why not ferritin (TO)** — best marker in non-athletes, but an **acute-phase reactant** → ↑ with training inflammation even when stores are low

**Female iron depletion (TO)** — **menstrual loss**, **exercise-induced haemolysis**, **sweat loss**

## X vs Y

| | **Female athlete triad** | **RED-S** |
|---|---|---|
| Origin | **ACSM 1992** | **IOC 2014** |
| Who | Female athletes | **Includes males** |
| Core | LEA ± eating disorder, amenorrhoea, ↓ BMD / osteoporosis | LEA, reproductive dysfunction, low BMD (↑ turnover) |
| Scope | Narrow | **Broader spectrum** (performance, CV health) |

| Part of triad | NOT part of triad |
|---|---|
| **Low energy availability ± eating disorder** | **Stress fracture (TO)** |
| **Menstrual dysfunction** (amenorrhoea; dysmenorrhoea option — TO) | **Thyroid dysfunction** |
| **↓ BMD / osteoporosis** | Ligamentous laxity, ↑ ACL tear, hirsutism, **low LDL** |

## How it's asked

**"Triad includes all EXCEPT (TO)?"** → **stress fracture**\
✗ Wrong options: dysmenorrhoea · LEA · low bone density

**"Same TO stem, repeated papers (2015, 2016, 2019)?"** → **stress fracture**

**"Triad = amenorrhoea + osteoporosis + ?"** → **low energy availability (± disordered eating)**

**"Associated with triad EXCEPT?"** → **low LDL levels** (LDL is raised)

**"Not part of the triad?"** → **thyroid dysfunction**

**"Thyroid and the triad?"** → **not a core component; may occur secondarily**

**"Anorexia athletica criteria EXCEPT?"** → **presence of an affective disorder**

**"Secondary amenorrhoea definition?"** → **absence of menstrual bleeding for 6 months**

**"Hormones in low energy availability?"** → **↑ cortisol, ↓ free T3, ↓ LH**

**"Screening item NOT routinely assessed (TO)?"** → no key given · **supplement / drug-abuse history** (inferred)

**"Advice NOT suitable for triad (TO)?"** → **avoid OCPs**

**"Least appropriate treatment?"** → **bisphosphonates**

**"Most critical initial step, BMI 17, skips meals?"** → **multidisciplinary nutritional + psychological counselling**

**"Runner, femoral neck stress fracture — work-up for?"** → **osteoporosis, anorexia, secondary amenorrhoea (all)**

**"Tibial stress fracture, BMI 16, menses 7 mo ago — aetiology?"** → **poor caloric intake**

**"Amenorrhoea, Beighton 4 — at greater risk of?"** → **femoral neck stress fracture (Figure A)**

**"15 yr runner, tibial stress reaction, 3 mo amenorrhoea?"** → **discuss eating habits + training regimens**

**"Tension-side femoral neck after prior 5th MT fracture — next?"** → **obtain a menstrual history**

**"Marine recruit — biggest femoral / pelvic stress fracture risk?"** → **no menses in the year before training**

**"Recurrent MT stress fractures — likely finding?"** → **amenorrhoea for 12 months**

**"Hurdler, femoral cortical thickening, pain walking?"** → **stop training + MRI**

**"Biggest contributor to female ACL tears?"** → **neuromuscular coordination + training**

**"Daily calcium for stress-fracture prophylaxis (TO)?"** → **2000 mg/day**

**"Best iron-status test in a female athlete (TO)?"** → **ferritin / transferrin ratio**

**"Runner's nipples — EXCEPT (TO)?"** → **warm weather precipitates it** (cold does)

**"Modified Thomas: thigh on table, knee extends (TO)?"** → **two-joint hip flexors short (positive)**

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Tibial Shaft Stress Fractures

*Knee & Sports · 59 / 61*

## 1 Basics

### Epidemiology – tibia most common site

**Most common stress fracture bone** — **tibia** in athletes + military training (OB)\
✗ Wrong options: femur · fibula · navicular · 5th MT

**Most common site (TO)** — **posteromedial tibia, junction of middle + distal third** → answer "**distal third of tibia**"\
✗ Wrong options: 2nd MT · fibula · femoral neck

**Series (Ohta-Fukushima, OB)** — 370 athletes: **tibia 49.1%** > tarsals 25.3% > metatarsals 8.8% · evaluation **> 3 wk** after pain onset → prolonged recovery

**2nd commonest** — **2nd / 3rd MT shaft (March fracture)** (TO) · **tarsals** (OB series) — read the options

**Who** — **runners + military recruits** · after a **change in training routine** · incidence in runners **up to 20%** (OB)

**Risk factors (OB)** — **sudden ↑ training intensity**, **low BMD**, **menstrual irregularities**, **low energy availability** · always evaluate **female athlete triad**

### Stress fracture sites (TO)

| Site | Detail (TO) |
|---|---|
| **Tibia** | **Posteromedial**, **middle–distal third junction** — **most common** |
| **2nd / 3rd MT shaft** | **March fracture** — **2nd most common** |
| Other metatarsals | — |
| Medial malleolus | Jumpers + runners |
| **Distal fibula** | **Runner's fracture** |
| Femoral neck | **Inferomedial (compression) commoner** than superior (tension) · **tension more dangerous** → completes without fixation |
| Femoral shaft | Posteromedial or medial cortex |
| Navicular | **Central third** — runners / sprinters, ballet dancers, football players |
| Calcaneus | **Tuberosity** — military recruits, runners |
| **Ribs** | **Middle ribs (4th–9th)** — **rowers**, **serratus anterior** traction |
| Pubic ramus | **Female long-distance runners** |

![Bilateral distal-third tibial stress fractures (arrows)](../../MCQ/attachments/TO17125615-1.png)

### Pathophysiology

**Mechanism** — **repetitive submaximal loads** → resorption > formation · abrupt ↑ duration / intensity / frequency without rest → **↑ osteoclast activity** (OB)

**Bone type conflict** — **linear microfractures in trabecular bone** (Orthobullets) · **weight-bearing cortical bone, not usually trabecular** (OB, Female Athlete chapter) — read the options

**Healing** — **callus** → **woven bone** → **endochondral bone formation**

**Why the anterior cortex fails** — **tension side** from **posterior muscle force** (Orthobullets) · **tensile forces + relative paucity of blood supply** in anterior midshaft (OB)

### High- vs low-risk sites – tension vs compression

| | **Anterior cortex (tension)** | **Posteromedial cortex (compression)** |
|---|---|---|
| Frequency | **Uncommon** | **Typical / most common** |
| Risk | **High** — delayed union, **nonunion**, **propagation to complete fracture** | **Low** — usually heals |
| X-ray | **"Dreaded black line"** (transverse anterior lucency) | Periosteal reaction, cortical thickening |
| Rx | Often **IM nail** | **Protected WB** |

**Greatest risk of delayed healing / nonunion (OB)** — **anterior cortex of tibia** · 2nd + 5th MT can delay / nonunite (OB)\
✗ Wrong options: posteromedial tibia · distal fibula · inferior femoral neck (compression side) · **3rd MT — heals well**

**High-risk sites (TO, Femoral Neck Stress Fractures chapter)** — tension femoral neck, tension patella, **anterior tibial cortex**, medial malleolus, talar neck, dorsal navicular cortex, 5th MT proximal metaphysis, great-toe sesamoids

**Low-risk sites (TO, same chapter)** — femoral shaft, **medial tibia**, ribs, ulnar shaft, 1st–4th MT · **2nd MT** low risk (TO) vs can delay / nonunite (OB) — read the options

## 3 Presentation

**History** — **change in exercise routine** · **insidious onset** · pain with running first → then **daily activities / walking**

**Exam** — **pain directly over the fracture** · **point tenderness** (TO)

**Army recruit, 6 wk training, posteromedial pain, point tenderness at distal–middle third junction (TO)** — **stress fracture**\
✗ Wrong options: posteromedial tibial stress syndrome · gastrocnemius strain · bone contusion

**MTSS clue (TO)** — **dull pain** ↑ with activity, relieved by rest · **diffuse tenderness along posteromedial tibial border** (vs **point** tenderness in fracture)

**Differential (OB)** — medial tibial stress syndrome (shin splints), periostitis, infection, bursitis, neoplasm, **exertional compartment syndrome**, nerve entrapment

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Study | Findings | Facts |
|---|---|---|
| **Radiographs (AP + lateral)** | **Lateral: "dreaded black line" anteriorly** (tension) · endosteal thickening · periosteal reaction + cortical thickening | Often diagnostic (Orthobullets) · **usually normal early** → later periosteal reaction, new bone, fracture line (OB) |
| **MRI** | **Earliest: T2 periosteal high signal** · marrow oedema · **T1 linear low-signal zone** | **Most sensitive** · **replacing bone scan** · normal X-ray + high suspicion → MRI |
| Tc-99m bone scan | **Focal uptake** in cortical and / or trabecular region | **Highly sensitive, low specificity** (OB) |

**Investigation of choice (TO)** — **MRI** — **best combined sensitivity + specificity even early**; recommended for the whole bone-stress spectrum\
✗ Wrong options: CT · clinical exam · bone scan

**X-ray-negative suspected tibial stress fracture (TO)** — **MRI** — **most sensitive + specific** · beats bone scan + CT for soft-tissue / oedema, **earlier** than bone scan · sensitivity ≈ bone scan but **far more precise for location + extent**

**MRI vs bone scan (OB)** — MRI better (Ishibashi)

**Normal films, high suspicion (OB)** — trial of rest + **serial radiographs** also appropriate

![Lateral tibia — "dreaded black line" of the anterior cortex](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3112-xray-with-dreaded-black-line.jpg)

![MRI — tibial marrow oedema](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3112-mri-shows-edema.jpg)

### MRI grading of bone stress injury (TO)

| Grade (TO) | MRI signs |
|---|---|
| **1** | Mild–moderate **periosteal oedema on STIR**, **no marrow change** |
| **2** | Moderate–severe periosteal oedema on STIR **+ marrow change on T2WI** |
| **3** | Grade 2 **+ marrow change on T1WI** |
| **4** | **Fracture line visible** |

## 5 Management

```
Tibial shaft stress fracture
├─ Posteromedial / X-ray normal, MRI oedema → protected WB 4–6 wk, stop running
├─ Anterior cortex, dreaded black line → reamed IM nail
│      (esp. if the line violates the anterior cortex)
└─ Failed nonop, complete, or fixed deadline → IM nail
```

### Operative criteria

| Indication for IM nail (OB, Orthobullets) |
|---|
| **High-risk fracture — anterior tibial cortex** ("dreaded black line", esp. violating the cortex) |
| **Failure of nonoperative management** (no improvement with rest — Boden) |
| **Complete fracture** |
| **Need to return on a specific timeline** (athletes, military) |

### Posteromedial compression-side fractures – protected weight bearing

**Standard (most cases)** — **activity restriction + protected weight-bearing** · **avoid NSAIDs** (slow bone healing) · consider **bone stimulator** · correct nutritional deficiencies

**Duration (OB)** — **4–6 wk limited WB** → progress to full WB

**Runner, normal X-ray, MRI oedema, positive bone scan** — **protected WB 4–6 wk**\
✗ Wrong options: immediate return · IM nail without nonop trial · long-leg cast · biopsy

**Distance runner, proximal tibial pain now with walking, normal X-ray, MRI +** — **protected WB with crutches**\
✗ Wrong options: switch to elliptical · prescription NSAIDs · arthroscopy · ORIF

**Pneumatic bracing + modified rest (OB, Batt)** — effective, but return to full activity **\~12 months**

**Pulsed ultrasound (OB, Rue)** — **no** effect on healing or return to duty (26 servicemen, 43 fractures)

**Most important factor in an athlete with a stress fracture (TO)** — source gives **no key**; best fit = **correction of biomechanical + training errors** (inferred) (vs immobilisation 8 wk, gradual return, analgesics)

### Anterior cortex tension-side fractures – nonunion risk & intramedullary nailing

**Footballer, 6 mo pain, failed nonop, anterior linear lucency** — **tibial IM nailing** — fastest WB + return\
✗ Wrong options: posterior plate + graft · graft alone · cast · protected WB

**Special-forces soldier, dreaded black line, deploys in 4 mo** — **tibial IM nail**\
✗ Wrong options: activity modification + vitamin D (high-risk site, no deficiency stated) · pulsed US · pneumatic brace NWB (up to 12 mo) · observe + repeat imaging (risk of completion)

**Outcome (Varner, OB)** — 11 athletes, **reamed IM nail** for chronic anterior stress fracture → **return to sport \~4 months**

**Plating** — **anterior tension-band plating** described · **not posterior**

**IM nail caveat (OB)** — allows return to sport but **does not guarantee healing**

## 6 Complications

**Anterior cortex** — **delayed union, nonunion, propagation to complete fracture** · **highest risk of all tibial sites**

**Delayed diagnosis** — evaluation **> 3 wk** after pain onset → longer recovery

## X vs Y

| | **Stress fracture** | **MTSS (shin splints)** | **CECS** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Tenderness | **Point / focal** | **Diffuse posteromedial border** | Normal exam at rest |
| Pain | With running → **walking / rest** | Dull, worse at start, **↓ during training** | Starts \~10 min in, resolves \~30 min after |
| X-ray | **Dreaded black line** (anterior) | Normal · periosteal exostoses (late) | Normal |
| Bone scan | **Focal, intense, all 3 phases** | **Diffuse, longitudinal, delayed phase only** | — |
| Test | **MRI** (oedema / line) | Clinical · bone scan / MRI | **Compartment pressures** |
| NSAIDs | **Avoid** (slow healing) | Used | Used |

## How it's asked

**"Most common site of stress fracture (TO)?"** → **distal third of tibia**

**"Same TO stem, 2014 paper?"** → **distal third of tibia**

**"Bone most often involved in athletes?"** → **tibia**

**"Greatest likelihood of delayed healing / nonunion?"** → **anterior cortex of tibia**

**"Recruit, posteromedial pain, point tenderness (TO)?"** → **stress fracture**\
✗ Wrong options: posteromedial tibial stress syndrome

**"Investigation of choice for stress fracture (TO)?"** → **MRI**

**"X-ray-negative suspected tibial stress fracture (TO)?"** → **MRI**

**"Normal X-ray, MRI oedema, hot bone scan?"** → **protected weight-bearing for 4–6 weeks**

**"Runner, proximal tibial pain with walking, MRI +?"** → **protected weight-bearing with crutches**

**"Failed nonop, anterior linear lucency, quick return?"** → **tibial intramedullary nailing**

**"Soldier, dreaded black line, deploys in 4 months?"** → **tibial intramedullary nail**

**"Most important factor in athlete with stress fracture (TO)?"** → no key given · **correct biomechanical + training errors** (inferred)

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Tibial Stress Syndrome (Shin Splints)

*Knee & Sports · 60 / 61*

## 1 Basics

**Definition** — overuse / repetitive-load injury of the shin → **persistent dull anterior leg pain**

**Incidence** — **10–15% of running injuries** · **60% of leg pain syndromes**

**Location** — **medial (posteromedial) tibial stress syndrome = most common** · anterior (anterolateral) tibial stress syndrome · distal + posteromedial tibia

**Risk factors** — **poor shock absorption** (cement / uneven surfaces, improper shoes) · training errors (sudden ↑ intensity + duration) · **> 20 miles / week** · early-season hill training · previous lower-limb injury · **over-pronation / ↑ internal tibial rotation**

**Pathophysiology** — **traction periostitis** · anterolateral → **tibialis anterior** on tibia + interosseous membrane · posteromedial → **tibialis posterior + soleus**

**Associated** — **female athlete triad** (critical to diagnose + treat) · **tibial stress fracture** — **females 1.5–3.5× risk of progression**

![Pain areas — anterior vs medial tibial stress syndrome](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3108-stress-syndromes.jpg)

## 3 Presentation

**Symptoms** — **vague, diffuse pain along middle–distal tibia** that **↓ with running** (early) · later: pain earlier with more frequent training

**Exam** — **tenderness along posteromedial tibial border**, from **4 cm above medial malleolus** extending **up to 12 cm** proximally · **pes planus**, **tight Achilles**, **weak core**

**Provocative test** — **pain on resisted plantarflexion**

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Study | Shin splints | vs stress fracture |
|---|---|---|
| Radiographs | **Normal first 2–3 wk** · late **periosteal exostoses** | **"Dreaded black line"** |
| **3-phase bone scan** | **Diffuse, longitudinal** uptake along **posteromedial (posterior) tibial cortex**, **delayed phase (3) only** · phases 1 (flow) + 2 (blood pool) normal | **Focal, intense** hyperperfusion + hyperaemia in phases 1 + 2 · **focal, fusiform** uptake in phase 3 |
| MRI | **Periosteal oedema** → progressive marrow involvement · shows other soft-tissue injury | Marrow oedema ± line |

**Distinguishing finding (OB)** — **bone scan: diffuse, longitudinal uptake of posterior tibial cortex = shin splints**\
✗ Wrong options: shin splints scan more intense · more diffuse tenderness in stress fracture · all 3 phases positive in shin splints · pain persists longer after activity in stress fracture

![Bone scan — diffuse longitudinal uptake (shin splints)](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3108-bone-scan-shin-splints.jpg)

![Bone scan — focal uptake (stress fracture)](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3108-stress-fracture-bone-scan.jpg)

### Differential diagnosis – exertional leg pain

| Condition | Characteristics | Tissue |
|---|---|---|
| Anterior tibial stress syndrome | Diffuse anterolateral pain, worse at start, **↓ during training** | Periosteum |
| **Medial tibial stress syndrome** | Diffuse middle–distal pain, worse at start, **↓ during training** | Periosteum |
| Tibial / fibular stress fracture | Pain with running, **point tenderness**, **dreaded black line** | Bone |
| Exertional compartment syndrome | Starts **10 min** into exercise, **resolves 30 min** after · sensory / motor loss · ↑ anterior compartment pressure | Muscle + fascia |
| Leg tendinopathy | Achilles, peroneal, tibialis posterior | Tendon |
| Sural / SPN entrapment | Dermatomal symptoms | Nerve |
| Lumbar radiculopathy | Worse sitting (lumbar tension position) | Nerve |
| Popliteal artery entrapment | Vascular studies | Vessel |

## 5 Management

**Nonop — first line, works in vast majority** — **NSAIDs**, rest, activity modification

**Activity modification** — **↓ running distance, frequency + intensity by 50%** · low-impact cross-training · stretching + strengthening · synthetic track · avoid hills, uneven / hard surfaces

**Shoes** — change every **250–500 miles** (lose shock absorption) · **orthotics** for pes planus

**Therapy** — strengthen **invertors + evertors of the calf** · **local phonophoresis with corticosteroids** may help

**Operative (failed nonop)** — **deep posterior compartment fasciotomy + release of painful periosteum** · variable results, complete relief unlikely

## 6 Complications

**Recurrence** — common after resuming heavy activity

## X vs Y

| | **Shin splints (MTSS)** | **Tibial stress fracture** | **CECS** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Pain with running | **↓ during training** (early) | ↑ → walking | **↑**, starts \~10 min in |
| Tenderness | **Diffuse posteromedial** | **Point** | Normal at rest |
| Bone scan | **Diffuse longitudinal, phase 3 only** | **Focal, all phases** | — |
| Cause | Traction periostitis | Bone fatigue | ↑ compartment pressure |

## How it's asked

**"Bone scan finding separating shin splints from stress fracture?"** → **diffuse, longitudinal uptake of posterior tibial cortex in shin splints**

<div style="page-break-after: always;"></div>

# Visceral Blunt Trauma

*Knee & Sports · 61 / 61*

## 1 Basics

### Overview

**Injuries** — **abdominal wall**, **spleen**, **liver**, **kidney**, **testicular + penile**

**Risk factors** — **contact sports**, **direct blows** · **deceleration** → spleen, liver, kidney

**Commonest sport injuries (Rifat, OB)** — **abdominal wall muscle, liver, spleen** · **spleen = most frequent intra-abdominal organ** in sport

| Organ | Rank / fact | Clue |
|---|---|---|
| **Spleen** | **Most common** abdominal organ in blunt trauma · **most common cause of death** from abdominal / visceral injury | **LUQ pain**, **Kehr's sign** (left shoulder), N/V · **mononucleosis** |
| **Liver** | **2nd most common** | **RUQ pain → right shoulder** |
| **Kidney** | Contact athletes, **blow to flank** | **Haematuria** (may be absent), flank ecchymosis |
| Abdominal wall | Commonest sport injury | **Localised pain**, pain on **trunk flexion / rotation** |

## 3 Presentation

**Warning pattern** — **localised pain → diffuse pain** = visceral injury

**Spleen** — **nausea + vomiting** · **sharp LUQ pain → dull + diffuse** · shoulder pain (**Kehr's sign**) **before hypotension + collapse**

**Signs (Juyia, OB)** — **referred shoulder pain**, **umbilical or flank ecchymosis**, **guarding / rebound** · high index of suspicion with any abdominal pain

### Diaphragmatic spasm

**"Wind knocked out"** — **direct blow to epigastrium** → diaphragmatic spasm → **observe**, resolves, **may continue play**

### Abdominal wall injury / rectus sheath haematoma

**Cause** — **epigastric or intramuscular vessel** injury

**Clinical** — **acute localised pain** · **mimics surgical abdomen** (N/V) · **pain on active trunk flexion or rotation**

**Treatment** — **ice + rest** (most) · **surgical decompression** if progressive + persistent pain

## 4 Diagnosis & Classification

### Key radiology findings

| Study | Use |
|---|---|
| **CT abdomen (± pelvis)** | **Diagnosis for all organs** — wall haematoma, spleen, liver, kidney, bowel · grades severity, guides treatment |
| **Scrotal ultrasound (urology)** | **Testicular rupture / haematocoele** |
| Pelvic X-ray | Fracture → posterior urethral injury |

![Splenic injury on CT](../../Orthobullets/attachments/ob3123-splenomegaly.jpg)

## 5 Management

### Hypotension after abdominal trauma – work-up

```
Athlete with abdominal blow
├─ Haemodynamically unstable → fluid resuscitation + transfer to ER → abdominal CT
├─ Stable, abdominal pain / haematuria → ER + CT abdomen / pelvis
└─ Epigastric blow, transient breathlessness → observe, may play
```

**Footballer, BP 70/48, vomiting ×2, resolving arm numbness** — **fluid resuscitation + transfer to ER for abdominal CT**\
✗ Wrong options: antiemetics + sideline rest · non-emergent C-spine MRI (**stinger** resolved) · RTP when arm settles · head CT (no concussion signs)

**RTP (Juyia, OB)** — varies with organ + grade · **up to 6 months** after severe spleen / liver injury

### Splenic rupture – mononucleosis risk

**Mono** — **acute splenomegaly** → ↑ rupture risk with trauma

**Spread** — **oral mucosa**: **sharing water bottles**, kissing ✓\
✗ Wrong options: creatine · spider bite (skin necrosis) · alcohol · NSAIDs on empty stomach (gastric irritation)

**Timing** — **4–21 days after infection** (OB, this topic) · **first 3 weeks** / within a month (infectious diseases chapter)

**Farley (OB)** — **> 8,000** IM patients → **9 splenic ruptures** (\~0.1%; 0.1–0.5% in infectious diseases chapter)

**17-yr-old, mono 1 month ago, tackled, LUQ pain, tachycardia** — **splenic rupture**\
✗ Wrong options: fractured kidney · ruptured appendix · ruptured gallbladder · pneumothorax

**RTP after mono** — **no contact sport until splenomegaly resolved** · radiographic surveillance **debated**

**Spleen treatment** — **observation** in most · **splenectomy only for severe** injury · range: observation → **laparotomy + packing ± splenectomy**

### Liver injury

**Treatment** — **conservative: monitoring + rest**

### Renal contusion – haematuria

**Pattern** — **males**, **single blow to the flank** · flank / abdominal pain, **ecchymosis**, **haematuria**

**Lacrosse, right flank blow, pink urine > 5 RBC/hpf, stable** — **renal laceration**\
✗ Wrong options: splenic laceration (left side, intra-abdominal bleed not haematuria) · haemothorax (chest pain + dyspnoea) · liver laceration (no haematuria) · posterior urethral injury (**displaced pelvic fracture**)

**Work-up** — **CT abdomen + pelvis ASAP** (risk of instability with continued bleeding)

**Treatment** — **stable → nonop** · small subset → exploration, partial / complete nephrectomy · **urgent surgery: extensive bleeding with renal fracture or vascular pedicle injury**

**Patel (OB)** — grade III / IV: **contact sports + skiing / snowboarding** commonest · sports injuries more **isolated**, ↓ instability + mortality

**Dangle (OB)** — paediatric: **low-grade injuries in males** commonest · few need surgery

**McAleer (OB)** — **< 5%** of testicular / renal injuries from team sports, no organ loss → **solitary kidney / testis may not need restriction**

![Right renal laceration with subcapsular haematoma (CT)](../../MCQ/attachments/q215510-illa-right-renal-laceration-ct.jpg)

### Penile & testicular injuries

**Pudendal nerve neuropraxia** — **cyclists** · **penile shaft numbness** · **seat modification**

**Testicular rupture** — usually a **kick** → **tunica albuginea rupture** → **haematocoele** · **painful firm scrotal mass, doesn't transilluminate** · **urology ultrasound** · observation

## X vs Y

| | **Splenic injury** | **Liver injury** | **Renal injury** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Side / blow | **Left**, LUQ | **Right**, RUQ | **Flank** |
| Referred pain | **Left shoulder (Kehr)** | **Right shoulder** | — |
| Urine | Normal | Normal | **Haematuria** |
| Bleeding | Intra-abdominal | Intra-abdominal | **Haematuria** |
| Treatment | Observe · **splenectomy if severe** | **Conservative** (monitor + rest) | Nonop if stable · surgery for renal fracture / **pedicle injury** |

## How it's asked

**"Splenic rupture risk factor in hockey player?"** → **sharing water bottles (mono)**

**"Mono 1 month ago, tackled, LUQ pain + tachycardia?"** → **splenic rupture**

**"Peak window for splenic rupture after mono?"** → **4–21 days**

**"BP 70/48 + vomiting after hit — next step?"** → **fluid resuscitation + ER transfer for abdominal CT**

**"Flank blow, pink urine, > 5 RBC/hpf — CT shows?"** → **renal laceration**

**"Most common cause of death from abdominal injury?"** → **splenic injury**

**"Cyclist with penile numbness?"** → **pudendal neuropraxia — seat modification**

**"Kicked, firm non-transilluminating scrotal mass?"** → **haematocoele (tunica albuginea rupture)**
